Her protection post failed because the delegation had not named civilian retaliation as a bearer cost. Toll recognition remained acceptable but supplied no reason to exclude other petitioners.

Kharza’s coalition received conditional passage through South Hollow Gate and the Hollow Salt route. Only three hundred armed travelers could enter each road section at once. City markets set transparent prices. No requisition applied. Sul Venn would send nine observers: three road assessors, two water readers, two treaty advocates, one veteran selected by the black-cap gallery, and one rotating caravan witness. Any three could stop transit on city-maintained sections for an unrecorded cost or breached weight limit.

Sul Venn sent no troops.

The distinction disappointed Mar and relieved half the gallery. Kharza accepted it publicly.

Doru received the veterans’ observer place. “I am not joining your command,” he told her.

“You should not.”

“I may stop your road.”

“If the condition applies.”

“I will decide whether it applies.”

“With two others.”

His mouth moved near a smile. “Still listening for the edge of your authority.”

“Yes.”

At South Hollow Gate on Day twelve, the city lifted three white bars from the road. Beyond lay a descending shelf of salt stone, pale under evening cloud. Farther south, the earth opened into the Hollow Salt desert and the buried route toward its hinge.

Coalition companies passed in groups under public axle limits. Merchants kept alternate road windows. Pell posted payment copies at both gates. Sevrin’s comb traveled in a city-inspected cool box. Lessa rode beside Kharza to enforce rest. Doru and eight observers wore Sul Venn gray, no coalition insignia.

At the first checkpoint, Doru stopped Mar’s weapons cart for exceeding its declared weight by two spear bundles. Mar argued the excess meant little on dry stone. A road assessor pointed to a hairline crack along the culvert lip. The spears came off and traveled by hand. Kharza did not intervene. Conditional passage became real at the first inconvenience.

Bel Dane watched from the east wall with Osra’s rejected amnesty rolled under his arm.

Kharza crossed the gate without a cheer. Sul Venn had opened a road, not offered absolution. Its observers followed because every promised benefit required a named cost, and because confession without enforceable limits would merely give old command a humbler voice.

Behind them, the city closed its military yard to both armies. Ahead, Hollow Salt received the coalition under conditions neither Kharza nor Osra controlled alone.

Chapter 10 — Down the Hollow Stair

The courier reached Maela while six soldiers were carrying the west granary door off its hinges.

He came through the drizzle with salt dried white across his boots and Pell Vask's oilskin tube tied under his coat. Behind him, Vael Tern's lower market moved uphill by handcart: sacks of rye, lamp crocks, dismantled millstones, three cages of indignant geese. The western farms had burned their surplus rather than leave it to Osra's cavalry. Every useful room inside the second wall now had to become a storehouse, an infirmary, or a place to sleep.

Maela broke Pell's wax with her thumbnail. The message named the dead at Ember Ford, the rain over Varkesh, and the salt surge advancing through Reedwater. Pell had put the costs before the victory. That made the account easier to trust and harder to celebrate.

At the foot of the page she had drawn a route: Sul Venn, Hollow Salt, Salt Hinge. Beside it stood a request in her compressed quartermaster's hand.

Find what the crown buried below its own stairs. Send copies by separate roads.

“Marshal?” Jorren asked.

Maela watched masons fit raw plank over the granary doorway. Beyond the western roofs, signal smoke marked three more of Osra's companies crossing the Wheatwater. By tomorrow they could close the road. By the day after, a courier might need a boat or a grave.

“Put the royal stores under ward captains,” she said. “No palace clerk may countermand them without my signature. Tell the council I am inspecting the lower foundations.”

Jorren glanced at the tube. Rain tracked down the old scar beside his mouth. “Are you?”

“No. I am inspecting a lie.”

They entered the citadel through a postern crowded with flour porters. Maela took only Jorren and two hinge engineers, Dasa Mor and young Fenn Cor. Four people could descend quietly. More would turn secrecy into rumor before she had anything worth spreading.

The Hollow Stair began behind the dead king's map room. A bronze door filled the inner wall, plain except for four shallow handprints worn almost smooth. Maela had passed it every week for six years. Ordan had called the lower chambers ceremonial storage and told her the keys had been lost during his grandfather's reign.

No keyhole marked the bronze.

She pressed the royal bearing seal into a depression concealed inside the lowest handprint. The seal warmed against her palm. Lines of dull green light traveled through the door, not decoration but pressure paths, each bending toward the other three handprints. Stone shifted somewhere below with a sound too large for the narrow room.

The bronze opened a finger's width and stopped.

Fenn set his shoulder against it. Dasa caught his belt before the pressure could pitch him into the gap.

“Not swollen,” Dasa said. She crouched and slid a bone gauge beneath the door. “The lower jamb has climbed half an inch.”

“Stone does not climb,” Jorren said.

“Ashwall did,” Maela answered.

They rigged a screw jack from the map room's shutter bar and two chair legs. The work lacked dignity and preserved their fingers. As Dasa turned the screw, the bronze gave in short complaints. Warm air escaped from below carrying salt, old paper, and a faint mineral sting that set Maela's teeth aching.

The gap widened enough for them to pass sideways. Maela went first.

Steps curved down around a shaft whose bottom their lamps could not find. Every ninth tread bore a different mark: a tusk pressed into clay, a branching leaf, a Vennik house knot, seven river strokes. Later masons had chiseled crowns across them. The cuts had not erased the older signs. They had merely made every landing look wounded.

Maela counted doors as she descended. The first opened onto shelves of tax rolls. The second held rusted counterweight chains thick as her wrist. The third had jammed inward, its lintel bowed beneath pressure from the buried sky. Fenn tapped the stone and listened with his cheek near the surface.

“It is carrying load from deeper down,” he said. “If the pressure climbs again, this stair could shear away from the shaft.”

“How long?”

He looked offended by the question because stone did not keep campaign hours. “Until it doesn't.”

That answer belonged in every fortress manual ever written.

They descended faster. At the thirty-fourth landing they found fresh lamp smoke on the ceiling.

Jorren drew his sword. Maela raised one hand, listening. A pen scratched beyond a cedar door. Someone coughed, muttered about margins, and moved a stool.

She knocked.

“Unless the upper city has fallen,” a man's voice called, “you may wait until I finish this line.”

“The upper city is preparing to be surrounded.”

The pen stopped.

Brother Caldrin opened the door with ink on three fingers and a salt-gray beard tucked into his belt. He wore no order's badge. His room held a cot, a brazier, six water jars, and stacks of clay rubbings tied with blue thread. A narrow passage continued behind him into darkness.

Caldrin looked at the bearing seal in Maela's hand. His expression held no reverence. “So Ordan died.”

“You knew?”

“I knew he would never come down here while breathing.” Caldrin stepped aside. “His father appointed me guardian of records he did not wish to read. His son made the appointment permanent by forgetting me.”

Dasa stared at the water jars. “You live here?”

“I work here. Living requires better air.”

Maela entered. On the desk lay a clay tablet impressed with columns in four scripts. A royal copy rested beside it. Someone had scraped whole lines from the newer clay before firing.

Pell's request tightened in her mind. “How many?”

“Compact tablets? Nine complete, fourteen fragments, and three royal revisions pretending to precede their own source.” Caldrin folded a damp cloth over the tablet. “You have no right to them.”

“I have the seal.”

“That opens a door. Rights require witnesses.”

The words struck with the force of a formal challenge. Maela could have arrested him. She could have ordered Jorren to search the room and invoked emergency custody. Every excluded petitioner she had turned away from Crown Hinge had heard some polished version of those powers.

“Then witness this,” she said. “Osra Dane is closing a siege ring around seventy thousand people. Another coalition has moved Ember Hinge without royal command. The movement brought rain to Varkesh and salt into Reedwater. They are going to Salt Hinge because the buried pressure will not wait for our laws. I need to know whom those laws erased.”

Caldrin studied her as if comparing a damaged copy against an original. “Need has excused most erasures.”

He drew a folded list from beneath the damp cloth. It named petitions Maela remembered refusing: a Mahr hearth council seeking access after a mine collapse, Salt Aeli navigators asking to compare tide bearings, a Vennik house offering to weigh the Crown Hinge chains. Beside each petition a palace clerk had written competence acknowledged; standing denied. Maela recognized her own signature on two.

“Those decisions protected a dangerous chamber during unrest,” she said, and heard how readily the old defense arrived.

“Did danger make their measurements false?”

“No.”

“Did your signature make the pressure smaller?”

“No.” The second answer cost more. She took the list without hiding the names beneath her hand. “Put this among the copies. If I ask strangers to trust my warning, they should know what I helped refuse.”

Dasa made another rubbing while Caldrin watched Maela rather than the page. The stair groaned below them, carrying the buried sky's answer through every royal alteration.

“What would satisfy you?”

“Copies sent beyond your control. The originals carried to the old court under Hollow Salt. Your seal stays with you.”

Jorren shifted by the door. “Taking the originals toward two armies is not safekeeping.”

“Leaving them under a dynasty that denied their existence has not served.”

A low concussion rolled through the shaft. Dust sifted from the ceiling. One water jar clicked against another.

Fenn hurried into the passage and returned pale. “The third landing has shifted again. We should leave.”

Caldrin reached for his lamp. “Then help me pack.”

Beyond his chamber, the archive narrowed into a rock-cut vault. Nine dark tablets stood upright in felted cradles, each as long as Maela's forearm and twice as heavy. Their surfaces held columns divided by lines that crossed rather than ranked one another. She recognized court Ternic along one edge. The other marks belonged to peoples royal charters usually named only as labor suppliers.

She did not ask Caldrin to translate. A hurried answer would become a weapon before it became knowledge.

They wrapped the Compact tablets in felt, then fitted them into two saltwood cases. Dasa took rubbings while Maela copied the visible Ternic lines onto thin dispatch paper. Some clauses concerned witness counts and mapped losses. Others referred to bearers without granting the crown first place. One scraped passage on the royal version remained whole on the older clay, but the script used an archaic legal hand she could not confidently parse.

“Who taught you to preserve these?” Maela asked.

“A Salt Aeli jurist whose name the palace catalogue records as a cleaning expense. A Mahr mason who entered by the load shaft. Two Vennik copyists paid to falsify weights and determined to leave honest numbers somewhere.” Caldrin tied the first case shut. “Guardianship has always looked less orderly from below.”

Another tremor struck. The lamp flames leaned toward the shaft. From above came the grind of bronze forced against stone.

“The outer door is moving,” Dasa said.

Maela divided her copies into six packets. One for Pell by the Hollow Salt courier road. One for Sul Venn's public treaty hall. One for the Neris coast. One for the Reed Confederacy, despite Aruk's alliance with Osra. One for the Vael council. The last she put inside her coat.

“Jorren, take Caldrin and the cases to the salt drain.”

He frowned. “The salt drain exits beyond the southern vineyards.”

“Which remain open for perhaps three hours. Choose six scouts, no uniforms. Get him onto the Hollow Stair caravan route.”

Caldrin tightened his beard knot. “I need no escort.”

“You need people who know which bridges still exist.” Maela handed him the packet for Pell. “Give Quartermaster Vask the originals only in a witnessed place. Keep one case with you if the company divides.”

“And you?”

“I go up.”

The archivist's gaze went to the royal bearing seal. “To defend the right that kept you out of this room?”

“To defend the people above it. The right can answer for itself when they are no longer under siege.”

They climbed together as far as the landing with the counterweight chains. There the shaft divided: the main stair curled toward the citadel, while a low service tunnel descended toward the old salt drains. Jorren shouldered one case. Fenn took the other despite his engineer's soft hands. Caldrin carried the rubbing satchel beneath his coat.

Maela wanted to give Jorren an order that would guarantee his return. Six years of command had taught her how often such orders merely disguised prayer.

“Three days,” she said. “If the southern road closes, do not try to come back through it.”

“I know what a ring looks like, Marshal.”

“So do I. That is why I dislike finding you on its wrong side.”

He gave her the brief smile of a scout already measuring distance. Then he followed Caldrin down into the dark.

Maela climbed with Dasa. They squeezed through the bronze door moments before the lower jamb rose again. The screw jack split, flinging one chair leg across the map room. Bronze struck bronze behind them and sealed the Hollow Stair without waiting for the royal seal.

Outside, bells sounded from the western tower. Not alarm. Withdrawal.

Maela ran to the battlement. Across the Wheatwater, Osra's cavalry had reached the north road. Engineers drove stakes for the first siege earthworks while infantry columns extended south through the harvested fields. The ring lacked less than a mile.

At the granary, ward captains were already issuing rye by household rather than rank. Maela handed the Neris packet to the salt-stained courier and the Sul Venn copy to a fishwife whose boat would leave by the eastern culvert. She sent the Reedwater packet with a marsh trader who spat at the royal wax before hiding it under her tunic.

Six copies departed by six routes. None carried enough to command a hinge. Together they carried enough to make concealment harder.

On the southern road, three riders emerged from the vineyard mist around a low cart. Caldrin sat beside the cases, his lamp extinguished at last. Jorren looked back once toward the wall, then led them down the Hollow Salt road before Osra's final companies closed it.

Maela fastened the royal bearing seal beneath her cuirass and resumed command of the surface. By sunset Vael Tern stood encircled, but the old originals had passed beyond the crown's reach.

Chapter 11 — The Compact Under Salt

Pell knew the old bearing court had been built by Vennik before she saw a single house knot.

The entrance tried to kill anyone taller than a loaded mule.

Kharza struck her brow on the lintel despite three warnings. Sevrin folded himself through with the careful resentment of a man entering somebody else's practical joke. Pell ducked, stepped over the drainage groove, and found the foothold cut where a short mason carrying forty pounds would need it.

“An ancestor had standards,” she said.

“An ancestor had enemies with horns,” Kharza muttered, touching the copper wrap on her cracked left tusk.

Beyond the entrance, the passage opened into a chamber beneath Hollow Salt. Four banks of seats descended toward a floor of black stone. Salt crystals coated the walls in pale fans, but the benches remained dry. Grooves converged at the center around a circular bearing table. Every groove had once held colored clay. Someone had gouged out three colors and left the royal blue.

Caldrin waited beside two saltwood cases. His escort looked exhausted enough to sleep standing. Jorren Taal clasped Pell's wrist, gave her Maela's sealed copy, and accepted a canteen before beginning the return journey to Vael Tern. He had less than a day to reach a culvert before Osra's patrols learned it existed.

“Tell her the rye count is still a lie if the ward captains don't weigh the sacks themselves,” Pell said.

“She will be delighted by the affection in your message.”

“Affection is expensive. That advice is free.”

Jorren left with Fenn and four scouts. Caldrin watched them vanish into the passage before he opened either case.

Sul Venn had sent three observers: Ral Olt, a capped-tusk ceramicist who had fought against Kharza in the Three Valleys; Tei Vask, no relation Pell could prove within four generations; and Saan Rel, a Salt Aeli navigator with her leaf-filaments shaved close. They stood in separate seating banks by choice. The coalition's own witnesses filled the fourth: Lessa with her medical roll, Vennik haulers, two refugee delegates, and captains from the ford.

Pell counted thirty-one people, four exits, seven lamps, two suspect ceiling seams, and one archive that could alter a war if nobody dropped it.

Caldrin laid the first of the Compact tablets on the bearing table.

“This court predates the Tern dynasty by three centuries,” he said. “It was repaired after the Inversion and used during the first lawful exchanges. The language crosses four scripts because no translation held priority.”

“Then give us all four,” Kharza said.

“I intend to. It will take hours.”

“Osra's outriders were half a day behind me,” Caldrin's scout said. “You have fewer.”

Pell took the royal copy from Maela's packet and set it beside the original clay. “Then begin with what somebody scraped away.”

Caldrin's mouth tightened in approval. He dampened a square of charcoal paper, pressed it over the oldest tablet, and rolled a felt cylinder across the impressions. Raised characters appeared in black. Tei read the Vennik column. Ral sounded out old Mahr court marks. Saan translated the Aeli branching script. Caldrin supplied river Ternic.

The four readings did not match word for word. They matched in structure.

Each hinge required four equal traditions of bearing: stress heard in stone, celestial pollen carried by living colonies, loads divided through measured counterweights, and consequences witnessed by those who would endure them. No king appeared in the subject of the clause. No ancestry received permanent command. The older language treated each people as a practice, not a blood destiny; a trained bearer could serve across traditions, but no ruler could merge all authority into one office.

Kharza bent over the Mahr column. “This mark means hearths, plural. Not Mahr as a single nation.”

Ral's silver-capped tusks flashed when he turned. “Convenient to remember plurality after you commanded three valleys as one field.”

“It would be more convenient to forget.”

Pell put one missing finger against the clay margin. “Argue after we know what we are arguing over.”

They continued.

A second tablet carried a cost map. Not a painted territory chart, as Pell had expected, but a ledger of gains and losses linked to bearing marks. One degree toward the Cinder Net lengthened Heartland grain warmth by eleven days while pushing brine farther into eastern reed beds. A contrary degree restored upland rain at the price of colder Neris currents. Every benefit named the households expected to lose, the stores owed to them, and the point at which witnesses could halt further movement.

Sul Venn's treaty custom had preserved a piece of this law. Every promised gain required a named cost because the first bearers had understood that weather could only travel, never multiply.

The following lines described payment before motion, not afterward. Grain moved to the coast before a colder current reduced fishing. Reed cuttings moved inland before brine entered nursery channels. The court appointed counters from communities receiving the benefit and communities bearing the loss, and either set could demand a new weighing when stores failed to arrive. Pell read the columns twice. No king had been trusted to compensate his own advantage from memory.

Tei tapped a sequence of house knots. “These weights are indexed to households, not provinces.”

“Because provinces do not go hungry,” said the refugee delegate from Ashwall. “People do.”

One of Kharza's captains objected that no army could wait for every village to approve weather. Saan answered that the Compact required representation and thresholds, not unanimity. Ral pointed to an emergency clause permitting temporary movement when a hinge threatened immediate collapse, provided the bearers published the debt and stopped at the first stable bearing.

Pell thought of Ember Ford. They had met the danger and ignored the debt until rain and salt forced it into view. “We used the emergency power without knowing its terms.”

“We saved people,” Kharza said.

“Yes. We also spent Reedwater's fields without asking. Both statements fit in one ledger.”

The general's copper-wrapped tusk twitched against pain. “Then what do we owe?”

Pell began a list on fresh paper: freshwater carts, seed reeds, salt physicians, ferry priority for displaced marsh households. The coalition did not have enough of any item. Naming the deficit did not fill it, but it kept victory from consuming the evidence of who had paid.

Caldrin turned the tablet to its final register. A series of witness marks had been pressed by fingers, tusks, filament combs, and weighted cords. Not decoration. Receipts.

“Copy every mark,” Pell told Tei. “If descendants can be charged for an old command, they can also inherit proof that consent once existed.”

Kharza looked at Pell. “Ember Ford.”

“The rain and the salt surge fit this table.” Pell followed the old columns with a charcoal nub. “Not perfectly. Pressure has accumulated for ninety-six years. But close enough to prove the movement transferred load rather than releasing it.”

Sevrin had gone still across the table.

His leaf-filaments caught the lamplight in copper-green threads. He touched a line in the coastal column without speaking.

Pell marked the gesture and kept listening.

The royal revisions began two reigns after the First Compact. Their clay looked cleaner. Their language did not. Public witnesses became royal witnesses, then appointed witnesses, then servants of the crown. Compensation tables vanished. The four practices became four labors under one commander. Later copies reduced even that phrase to ceremonial participation.

Caldrin turned the third tablet so a long scraped hollow faced the court. “The palace catalogue calls this damage from salt rot.”

“Salt does not use a chisel,” Tei said.

“No. Kings employ people for that.”

Ral read the surviving Mahr margin aloud. “No bearer shall claim a whole people in the bearing.” His gaze moved to Kharza. “You arrived with Kiln troops and a royal command history. Which part of that sentence troubles you?”

“All of it, because you are using it badly.” Kharza straightened. Pain tightened her mouth as the copper wrap pulled against the tusk crack. “I can read stress. I can command soldiers who consent to my command. I cannot stand here as the Mahr bearer while Reed fleets fight for Osra and Sul Venn sends observers instead of troops. If this court requires one orc to make every Mahr answer, then this court has learned nothing from its own wall.”

Lessa crossed her arms. “Say what you are offering instead.”

“Kiln House authority for the forces that elected me. No claim over Ral. No claim over Aruk. No silence imposed on Mahr who refuse the hinge.”

The refusal cost Kharza something Pell could see in the way she planted both feet. Her old command had promised simplicity. Every quartermaster knew simplicity often meant somebody else's burden had disappeared from the page.

Ral touched his capped tusk, then nodded once. “I will witness that limit.”

Caldrin added the statement to fresh clay.

A rumble passed through the court floor. Salt dust drifted from the eastern benches. Pell glanced at the ceiling seam she disliked most. It had widened by the thickness of a knife blade.

“Move the cases under the west arch,” she said.

Nobody moved.

“That was not a philosophical contribution. Tei, take the near handle. Lessa, clear the center.”

The room obeyed quartermaster tone before anyone remembered to object. They shifted the tablets into the more stable arch and moved witnesses away from the eastern wall. Pell set two haulers to brace the narrow exit with bench planks. The buried sky pressed upward through miles of salt, patient as debt.

When she returned to the table, Sevrin still watched the coastal figures.

“What do you recognize?” Pell asked.

“A variation.”

“That word means nothing without a number.”

His jaw hardened. “The table predicts a reduction in star-bee flight along Neris after a prolonged fixed period. Three seasons of widening loss, followed by apparent stability.”

Saan Rel stepped down from the Aeli bank. “Neris hives have reported stable yields.”

“They reported what I certified.”

Silence spread through the court, not empty but occupied by everyone recalculating him.

Pell placed the charcoal nub on the table before she snapped it. “What did you certify?”

Sevrin looked first at Saan, then Kharza. He did not seek Pell's mercy. That at least saved time.

“Three years ago I combined inland and coastal pollination figures. Rootglass losses made the guild total look catastrophic. Neris merchants threatened to withdraw feed stores if the report caused a harbor closure. I separated the ledgers and published the stronger coastal yield.”

“You hid Rootglass?” Lessa asked.

“I hid unequal failure. I believed a united guild could protect the queen-hive until the next leafing.”

Saan's shaved scalp showed the flush beneath her skin. “Neris did not remain strong. Dockside hives failed first. We were told poor keepers had contaminated them.”

Sevrin closed his eyes. “I know.”

“How many colonies?” Pell asked.

“Forty-seven recorded. Likely sixty.”

“Likely is not a number you get to bury twice.”

The ceiling cracked.

A slab dropped over the eastern seats and shattered three benches. Salt blasted across the court. Pell hit the floor as the bearing table jumped beneath her hands. Someone screamed in the passage. The braced exit folded inward, snapping both planks.

Darkness followed. One lamp remained, rolling in a groove.

Pell crawled toward it and stopped it with her left palm. “Names,” she shouted. “Answer by seating bank.”

Voices came through dust. Mahr bank, six standing, one pinned. Aeli bank, five standing, Saan cut but moving. Vennik bank, all present. Coalition witnesses, two missing near the exit.

Kharza's stone-song rose below ordinary hearing. Pell felt it through her teeth and the clay floor. The general pressed her wrapped tusk near the west arch, listening past pain.

“Load is crossing from east to north,” Kharza said. “We have minutes before the passage closes.”

Sevrin knelt beside the surviving lamp. His leaf-filaments tilted, not toward light but toward a draft leaking under the fallen slab. “Air behind that wall. Perhaps another gallery.”

“Perhaps does not move rock.” Ral was already digging around the pinned observer.

“No, but it tells us where not to suffocate.”

Pell took inventory. Two case handles. Three intact bench rails. One chain embedded in the bearing floor. Twelve able lifters close enough to hear her. She sent Lessa to the wounded, Kharza and Ral to choose the cut, Sevrin and Saan to track airflow, and Tei to strip the counterweight chain from its salt-packed groove.

Nobody asked who had authorized her.

They worked by practices the kings had reduced to symbols. Kharza marked two fracture lines and refused a third where the stone-song doubled. Sevrin held a filament above each crack until he found the cold thread of air. Vennik haulers made an A-frame from bench rails and ran chain through the case handles as makeshift pulleys. Caldrin kept the tablets wrapped while dictating their order to a refugee boy, in case clay broke and memory had to carry what survived.

The pinned observer belonged to Sul Venn, a potter named Menn Olt. The slab trapped her left leg above the ankle. Lessa slid folded felt beneath the stone and warned that lifting too fast would tear the limb.

“Three counts,” Pell said. “Raise one hand's breadth. Hold. Lessa clears. Then raise again.”

Kharza braced the A-frame with her shoulder. Sevrin took a line beside Saan. Old Caldrin wound rope around both forearms. Four peoples, Pell thought, and pushed the thought away before it made labor into a mural.

They lifted.

The chain bit into saltwood. The frame bowed. Menn came free with her boot crushed and blood running dark through Lessa's packing. As soon as they lowered the slab, a second crack traveled across the ceiling.

“North wall now,” Kharza said.

Ral drove a ceramic wedge into the fracture she had marked. Two Kiln soldiers hammered with tablet cradles after Caldrin swore at them and handed over a less important bench leg. Salt split along the cut. Cold air struck Pell's face.

The missing coalition witnesses were in the blocked entrance passage. Their shouts came faintly through stone. Pell could not open both routes before the ceiling failed.

“Names?” she called.

“Bera Venn and Colt Dane,” someone answered.

Pell fixed the names in memory. Then a fist struck from the far side of the north wall.

Osra's outriders had found the adjacent gallery.

A spearhead punched through the opening. Ral knocked it aside with his silver tusk cap. Kharza caught the shaft and dragged the soldier against the gap hard enough to disarm him.

“Enemy gallery,” Tei said.

“Still a gallery,” Pell answered. “Widen it.”

More blows came from beyond. Sevrin kicked the surviving lamp into the breach, plunging the other side into confusion. Kharza's soldiers broke the lower salt blocks while Sul Venn observers held shields over them. When the hole reached shoulder width, Pell sent the wounded first.

Osra's engineers attacked from the dark. They were human and Mahr, wearing salt veils and siege leather. Their objective had not been slaughter; they carried pry bars, packing straw, and royal evidence sacks. They wanted the archive.

Pell looped a counterweight rope around the first sack-bearer's knees and pulled. Saan struck his wrist with a lamp hook. Ral shouted safe-conduct terms in old court Mahr while Kharza ordered her soldiers not to pursue beyond the gallery bend. The court roof began falling in slabs before either side could win.

Evacuation imposed its own truce. Osra's engineers withdrew toward their shaft with two wounded. Pell's people carried Menn, the Compact tablets, and their own injured in the opposite direction. Bera and Colt remained behind the collapsed entrance. Pell heard their pounding cease as the central ceiling came down.

The force of it drove everyone to their knees. Salt air roared through the gallery. The old court disappeared behind white dust, taking its royal-blue groove and its gouged-out colors together.

They emerged an hour later through a sink cut beneath the desert crust. Sunset made Hollow Salt look briefly flooded with copper. Osra's riders watched from a ridge too distant to charge before darkness. Kharza formed a rear guard without claiming the Sul Venn observers. Sevrin walked apart, his face gray with dust.

Pell opened both cases. Eight complete tablets had survived. The ninth had cracked across one corner without losing script. Caldrin put his hands over them, then moved his hands away.

“Custody?” he asked.

“Mine until Vael Tern,” Pell said. “Copies begin tonight. Sul Venn gets a set. Neris gets the coastal figures. Reedwater gets the degree costs. Every coalition company gets the clause limiting sole command.”

Caldrin nodded. “Witnesses?”

Pell looked across the survivors: Kharza, who had refused to impersonate a whole people; Ral, who still did not follow her; Saan, whose coast had paid for Sevrin's quiet; Lessa tending Menn's ruined leg; Tei counting the dead.

“All of them,” she said.

Sevrin approached before anyone called him. “My figures go out under my name.”

“They do,” Pell said. “You will also explain them to every orchard council that asks.”

“I will.”

Trust had not returned. Authority had changed. The Compact tablets lay in Pell's wagon under four separate seals, and by midnight the coalition possessed a lawful claim to Salt Hinge that no crown had granted and no single bearer could own.

Chapter 12 — Osra's Harvest Ledger

Osra placed the last blue marker on the campaign map at dawn.

The southern vineyards lay beneath it. Tavan Roe's engineers had closed the final road with a ditch, an earth bank, and six linked watch posts. Vael Tern now stood inside a complete ring: three rivers, two armies, seventy thousand mouths, no open farm.

Rain hissed against the command pavilion. It had come west from Ember Hinge across country that should have remained dry for another month. The sound drew younger officers to the tent edge. They treated altered weather as wonder because none of them had watched wonder empty a granary.

“Count the ring,” Osra said.

Her clerks read the columns. Eighteen thousand Heartland infantry, five thousand cavalry, four thousand Reed soldiers under Aruk Venn, two thousand engineers, two thousand support and medical workers. Thirty-one thousand at campaign beginning, though sick animals and flooded roads had already reduced what could move on any given hour. The numbers mattered more than banners. An army that admired itself starved as efficiently as a city.

Tavan leaned over the northern approach. “Crown Marshal Ruun pulled the ward stores behind the second wall. She also moved families out of the river quarter.”

Before answering, Osra walked the wet perimeter with her marshal. The ring had to function as a settlement before it could defeat one. Heartland infantry dug runoff trenches uphill from their sleeping pits. Reed cooks had rejected the army's iron cauldrons and suspended broad clay pans between barges, boiling millet without wasting scarce wood. Vennik teamsters in royal service rebuilt a collapsed wagon ramp while cursing every officer who had placed tents across the drainage line. Nobody shared a culture of war. They shared mud, consumption, and the fact that bad orders reached all tents eventually.

At the north hospital, a surgeon showed Osra six cases of marsh ague acquired before the siege began. The medical stores carried fever bark for eleven days at current use. Support workers had already cut doses for camp followers to preserve soldiers.

“Reverse that,” Osra said. “Ill laundresses spread fever through five companies. An ill spearman spreads it through one bed.”

The surgeon stared, then amended his slate.

Farther east, cavalry horses chewed their third day of reduced fodder. A remount officer proposed stripping the last standing hay from two villages inside the ring. Osra refused. Those households had accepted royal occupation under written payment. Breaking the terms would deliver recruits to Maela and still leave the horses hungry next week. She ordered river oats purchased from Aruk's barges at his named rate.

The tour consumed an hour and corrected nine failures no battle map showed. When Osra returned to the pavilion, her hem carried the same clay as a laborer's boots. She trusted the ring more for having found its weaknesses.

“Toward the inner districts?”

“Toward old flood shelters. She is preparing for bombardment and a breach.”

“She is preparing to make prudence resemble legitimacy.” Osra removed two cavalry markers from the west and set them beside the salt road. “Send them after the archivist's party.”

“They missed the closing by hours.”

“Then they can continue toward Hollow Salt.”

Tavan's face did not change, but she pressed one finger on the map where the salt road divided among sinkholes. “Pursuit costs fodder. We have reports that Kharza's coalition intends Salt Hinge.”

“That is why pursuit has value.”

Osra dismissed the officers at first bell. She kept Tavan, Aruk's empty chair, and a cedar box brought from the royal famine archive. The box had traveled under her own bedroll from the Heartland. Its lock opened with an ordinary brass key. Some of the most dangerous records in a kingdom needed no magic because shame guarded them well enough.

Inside lay the original ledger of the Ash Famine.

The cover bore smoke stains from a granary riot forty-two years earlier. Osra had been sixteen. She remembered standing in a ration line with soot in her hair while a clerk announced that the day's barley had gone to soldiers guarding the northern mills. Her mother had traded a silver comb for two handfuls of black peas. Her younger brother had eaten half, vomited during the night, and stopped asking for water before dawn.

The ledger gave him one line.

House Dane, west ward: one male child, wasting fever, unconfirmed.

No name. No correction.

Osra turned the pages. The original tables compared Heartland grain with deaths beyond it. That comparison had been removed from every public copy. When the fixed Cinder Net warmed the central fields, grain yields recovered within four seasons. During the same years, salt storms stripped Varkesh terraces, winter currents failed along Neris, and Reedwater nurseries soured. The gains had not cured the realm. They had moved its hunger until the capital could stop seeing it.

She knew this. She had always known enough of it to avoid asking for the whole table.

A loose page preserved distributions from the famine's worst winter. South Granary received eight hundred wagons under guard. The eastern wards received two hundred and thirteen after spoilage. Varkesh sent kiln grain and took royal debt notes later declared invalid. Reedwater delivered smoked fish while its own nursery islands lost seed stores to a surge. The crown had survived through assistance from regions the official history described as dependents.

Osra followed signatures down the margin. Her father's appeared beside an emergency requisition. So did the mark of Aruk's predecessor, pressed in silver-black ink. A Rootglass caravan had delivered bee-wax candles to keep mills running through ash-dark days. The famine she remembered as Heartland endurance had depended on a realm acting without the equal law its rulers had already hollowed out.

Memory resisted the figures. She could still see neighbors breaking a bakery shutter, a soldier losing two teeth to a thrown paving stone, her mother dividing peas with a sewing needle so neither child could accuse her of favor. Those things remained true. So did the wagons. Trauma did not become false when evidence widened around it. It became less obedient.

Tavan read one entry upside down. “The outer provinces fed us.”

“They fed the capital because the roads still answered the crown.”

“Or because hungry people understood other hungry people.”

Osra's first instinct reached for rank. She suppressed it. “Motives do not move wagons. Orders, contracts, animals, and passable roads do.”

“Then preserve all four next time you tell this story.”

Osra turned another page. A ruler could admit complexity in private and still face a public that demanded one cause, one culprit, one prevention. Yet the ledger refused her preferred simplicity line by line.

Tavan stood across the campaign desk. “Why bring that here?”

“Because Maela believes change has no memory.”

“I asked why you brought the original.”

Osra looked at the line naming her brother only by sex and symptom. “Copies become arguments. Originals remain evidence.”

“Evidence for whom?”

The question carried no insolence. Tavan had served her for nineteen years and still believed exact answers prevented poor orders.

“For the ruler who survives long enough to read it.”

Outside, teams hauled the first stone throwers onto the western bank. Men shouted as a wheel sank in mud. Osra closed the ledger before rain spray reached it.

A siege clerk entered with Vael's projected consumption. Maela had burned outer surplus, brought livestock inside, and shifted royal rye to ward captains. At full ration, the city could last thirty-seven days. At reduction, perhaps fifty-three. Disease would shorten either estimate once wells crowded.

Tavan waited until the clerk left. “We should open civilian passage before we announce terms.”

“Maela would move soldiers in family clothes.”

“Then inspect the columns. We have five thousand cavalry. We can separate weapons from bedding.”

“Five thousand cavalry require fodder. Inspections require guards. Refugees require roads through our stores.”

“They also require food if they remain inside.”

Osra rested both palms on the closed ledger. “The siege succeeds because the city consumes its confidence. A broad passage tells Maela she can preserve both her defiance and her people.”

“A passage tells the wards our quarrel concerns the hinge, not their children.”

“Our quarrel concerns whether their children inherit a country capable of feeding them.”

Tavan glanced toward the cedar box. “The children outside the Heartland might ask which country you mean.”

For an instant Osra saw the girl she had promoted after the Ford Riots, boots covered in flour, refusing to let cavalry trample a bread queue. Competence had survived. Agreement had not.

“Limited sick exchange,” Osra said. “Fifty per day, matched by our wounded prisoners. No general corridor.”

Tavan accepted the concession without gratitude. “And bombardment?”

“Military stores, wall towers, river chains. Not ward wells.”

“For now?”

“For as long as Maela keeps civilians away from hinge approaches.”

That condition let both women hear the threat without writing it down.

The first sick exchange tested the order before noon. Vael's postern released fifty people under a white river banner: fevered children, three women in labor, old men carried on doors, and two wounded soldiers whose uniforms had been cut away. Tavan's inspectors found a knife in a midwife's bag and nearly stopped the column until the woman demonstrated its use by slicing a birthing cord from her roll.

Osra watched from the east bank. Her own prisoners waited opposite, twenty-seven fit enough to walk and twenty-three on litters. Maela had sent exactly fifty, honoring the arithmetic even while forcing Osra to decide whether civilian illness counted against soldiers.

A captain asked which bodies to return so the categories matched.

“All of them,” Osra said. “The agreement says fifty people.”

The columns crossed between sharpened stakes. A Vael boy on a litter stared at the siege works with an intensity that made every ditch seem indecent. Beside him, a Heartland archer returning from the city gripped his sister's hand after recognizing her among Osra's camp laundresses. For several minutes the ring admitted kinship through its own line.

Then an inspector found a ration estimate sewn inside an old man's sleeve. Tavan brought it to Osra. The figures named ward stores and projected twenty-eight days at the new issue, less than their clerk's estimate.

“Maela sent a spy,” Tavan said.

“Maela sent a number she wanted us to believe.” Osra examined the stitching. It had been made conspicuous with blue thread. “If we arrest him, the exchange ends. If we accept it, she knows we have seen her claim.”

“Is it true?”

“Possibly. That makes it more useful than a poor lie.”

Osra let the old man pass and ordered the estimate copied. Mercy, intelligence, and coercion had occupied the same strip of road. She could not separate them cleanly, so she recorded all three.

Aruk arrived near midday with rain running from the edges of his silver tusk caps. He wore Reed scale woven from lacquered cane, no royal badge, and brought two marsh counters who placed a jar of cloudy water on Osra's map.

“From the Lower Nest channels,” he said.

Osra did not need to taste it. Salt crusted the rim.

“Ember's movement drove a surge,” she said.

“Your surveyors predicted pressure. They did not predict this.”

“They predicted a range.”

“Then your envoy chose the clean end of it when she bargained for our four thousand.”

Aruk's voice remained quiet. Reed councils distrusted shouting because sound carried badly over marsh wind and encouraged spectators to mistake volume for consent.

Osra opened a newer survey. “The surge should recede in nine days.”

“Should.”

“We will release freshwater from the east canal.”

“Heartland gates control that canal.”

“I control those gates.”

“That difference is why I asked for autonomy.”

Tavan shifted away from the table, leaving the bargain to its makers.

Osra named what she could grant: eight hundred water carts, salt-resistant seed from royal stores, fishing rights along the Wheatwater, and immediate recognition of three disputed Reed councils. Aruk listened without removing his caps.

“In exchange?” he asked.

“Hold the southeast ring. Deny Kharza passage if she reaches the city. Send twelve companies to seize Salt Hinge before her coalition arrives.”

“You offered marsh autonomy for service against Vael. Salt Hinge was not in our treaty.”

“The altered weather makes it part of the same defense.”

Aruk touched the water jar. “Changed costs make it a new bargain.”

Osra admired the precision and resented having taught it through concealment. “Name yours.”

“Reed command remains under Reed speakers. No burning of civilian boats. Survey figures go to our counters before public release. Freshwater moves today, not after my companies march.”

“Agreed, except survey figures that expose military routes.”

“Then your counters can black out routes under our witness.”

“Agreed.”

Aruk did not offer his hand. “Six companies for Salt Hinge. The rest hold the ring. If your engineers attempt movement without Reed witnesses, my soldiers stand aside.”

Tavan looked sharply at Osra. A subordinate commander would have received an order. Aruk had never been one.

“Six,” Osra said. “Under your officers.”

“Under our war speakers.”

He left with a written canal release, three council recognitions, and more authority than Osra had planned to grant. Bargains made during crisis had a way of surviving it.

Irel Saan did not come in person. Her message arrived in a Rootglass cylinder sealed with orchard resin. The senior gardener reported that Sevrin had taken stolen pollen into the salt desert, that queen-hive workers were striking the hive walls, and that any forced bearing could destroy the remaining migration pattern.

She offered Osra twenty orchard guards and route knowledge through the Sunken Observatory. Her terms required protection for Rootglass, no removal of the queen-hive, and Aeli control over any pollination attempt.

Osra dictated her answer to a Salt Aeli clerk. “Rootglass neutrality recognized. Queen-hive remains under Gardener Saan. Orchard guards retain their own captain. In return, prevent Sevrin Ael from bringing contaminated comb to Salt Hinge.”

The clerk paused. “Contaminated?”

“That is the public word.”

“Do we know it to be true?”

“We know the comb crossed Ember pressure and preceded ecological disruption.”

“That does not establish contamination.”

Osra looked at the clerk's shaved filaments and saw another person testing whether precision still mattered inside her pavilion. “Write unverified comb in the sealed terms. Public notices will say unsafe pollen.”

By evening, the siege ring's first proclamation waited for her seal. It listed Vael's defiance, offered amnesty to soldiers who left Maela, and described the weather crisis. One figure required her decision.

The survey placed projected Heartland grain loss under an uncontrolled Great Turning at between fourteen and twenty-nine percent, depending on timing. Her herald had written twenty-nine as the expected loss.

Tavan read over her shoulder. “That is the upper bound.”

“It remains possible.”

“The line calls it expected.”

“Fear will not mobilize around a range.”

“Truth might.”

Osra changed expected to probable and left twenty-nine. One word softened the lie without removing it. She signed the proclamation.

Tavan did not move. “Maela will publish the lower figure.”

“Then people will choose which risk they believe.”

“They will choose which of us has lied less.”

“People under threat choose the number that resembles their graves.” Osra sanded the ink. “You have your sick exchange. Prepare it.”

After Tavan left, Osra opened the Ash Famine ledger again. She could have burned it. The revised public histories already carried a clean sequence: failed harvest, royal intervention, fixed stars, recovery. No treasonous clerk needed to alter another page.

Instead she wrapped the original ledger in waxed linen, returned it to the cedar box, and pressed her private seal across the lock. She wrote a custody note naming the box, its contents, and the public figure she had changed. The note she hid beneath the false bottom where her brother's silver ration token had rested for forty-two years.

Preservation did not absolve the lie. It ensured the lie had an adversary if she lost.

Before closing the box, she added the day's proclamation beneath the linen. The honest range and the public figure now traveled together. A future court could call that vanity, confession, or insurance against her own ministers. Osra did not choose among them. She only knew that destroying evidence would make her certainty easier, and certainty had already cost too many nameless lines. She locked the box and gave the brass key to Tavan without explanation.

The marshal weighed it in her palm. “What am I guarding?”

“A record I may later order you to surrender.”

“Will you?”

“If the realm survives my prevention.”

Tavan put the key inside her cuirass. Trust between them had narrowed to custody, but custody could outlast agreement.

Osra summoned Tavan and the salt-road commanders at nightfall. On the map, Vael Tern's lamps burned inside a complete ring. Far west, Hollow Salt showed no lights, only reports of a collapsed court and fugitives carrying clay records.

“Six Reed companies and two engineer cohorts march before dawn,” she said. “Irel's guards guide them through the Sunken Observatory. Seize Salt Hinge. Take Caldrin alive if possible. Recover every tablet.”

“And Vael?” Tavan asked.

“No food enters. Sick exchange only. Cut the north aqueduct after our cisterns are full.”

Tavan's mouth flattened. “That will force the lower wards onto river water.”

“It will force Maela to count days honestly.”

Rain struck the pavilion roof harder, each drop sent by a hinge moved without Osra's consent. She set one hand over the cedar box and gave the final order.

“Starve the city before the sky starves the realm.”

Chapter 13 — The Sunken Observatory

The first star appeared beneath Sevrin's boots.

He stopped halfway down the rope ladder. A blue point burned under the salt crust, distant and clean, as if night had opened below the world. Then another brightened beside it. A hooked line emerged across the darkness until the buried constellation filled the chasm under him.

Above, Hollow Salt reflected noon in a white glare. Below, the buried sky held midnight.

“Move,” Kharza called from the ledge. “The ladder dislikes contemplation.”

Sevrin descended. His leaf-filaments leaned toward the blue fire despite the rock between them. Every strand along his scalp and forearms lifted with painful recognition. Upper and buried constellations pressed through his body in contrary directions. The sample comb at his chest began to hum.

The coalition entered the Sunken Observatory through a sinkhole Caldrin had found on an old tablet margin. Pell's haulers lowered the Compact tablets, counterweight bars, water, pollen frames, and wounded in separate slings. No animal could take the descent. Every pound had to be justified before hands carried it down.

At the bottom, black mineral glass curved away in terraces. Ancient observers had cut seats into the walls around a lens pool broader than a market square. Its water reflected no lamps. It showed the lower firmament directly: pale constellations moving beneath transparent stone, their light distorted by accumulated pressure. The Deep Hart's antlers bent against the horizon. A chain of cold stars Sevrin knew as the Salt Mother lay crowded behind a red arc that did not belong near it.

A procession had been carved around the pool's rim. Not kings. Teams. Mahr readers knelt with tusks near four separate pillars, none placed above another. Aeli keepers opened hive frames under mirrored light. Vennik crews hung from weighted ropes, their feet braced against opposing walls. Human river delegates carried bowls from seven waters and poured them into a common channel. Time and royal chisels had damaged faces, but the work remained legible in hands, harnesses, and loads.

Sevrin traced one carved hive. Several keepers had shaved filaments like Saan Rel. Others wore leafing growth long enough to braid. Orchard histories claimed Salt Aeli had abandoned sacred service after the first courts. The wall showed them serving without surrendering their own practice.

Caldrin found names beneath the mineral bloom and began reading. Some belonged to families now separated by borders. One Vennik witness bore the Vask house knot, though Pell refused to claim an ancestor from a shared voluntary name. A Mahr counter came from the drowned eastern reeds. The first court had not gathered pure representatives. It had gathered people with relevant labor and debts.

Coalition witnesses moved quietly along the relief. Refugees recognized grain measures carved beside the river bowls. A Sul Venn observer found an old capped-tusk custom depicted three centuries before her city's treaty. The room changed their posture before Caldrin translated a word. Nobody had been invited as decoration.

Sevrin looked up at the buried constellations. Rootglass teaching described observatories as places where Aeli starwardens interpreted the paired sky for lesser courts. Here interpretation occupied one station among several. The discovery reduced his office and enlarged the work.

The descent itself continued for another hour because supplies jammed at the final chimney. A pulley basket twisted beneath two water casks, trapping a hauler against the wall. Pell stopped every following load rather than cut the basket loose. Vennik climbers traversed the shaft, redistributed forty pounds into shoulder sacks, and lowered the hauler with a splinted ankle. The delay cost daylight through the mirrors, yet it preserved the person needed to rig them.

No pound arrived anonymously. Water carried Tei's chalk mark. Pollen frames carried Sevrin's. Clay tablets bore Pell's four seals. Each ration bundle named the company that had surrendered it. The old court turned logistics into testimony before magic began.

The observatory had sunk unevenly. Northern galleries stood dry above the pool. Southern doors disappeared under brine. Salt Hinge occupied the far wall, a colossal crescent of layered stone held between two natural pillars. Its bearing grooves climbed through galleries and vanished into the crust. Royal masons had fitted blue clamps across older cuts.

Sevrin touched the sample comb. Worker bees moved inside, agitated but alive. They had refused upper pollen at Rootglass. At Ember, they had flown when rain and heat briefly crossed. Here their bodies turned toward the buried light before he opened the case.

“Not yet,” he whispered.

Kharza heard. “What do they need?”

“A path between paired patterns. The pool can show it. I need the upper light carried down.”

Pell pointed at a row of tarnished mirrors hanging from chains. “Those?”

“Those, cleaned and aligned.”

“Excellent. An ancient sacred work requiring rags.”

The work divided quickly. Vennik teams freed mirror chains and set portable counterweights beneath the hinge's lower lip. Kharza placed Mahr readers along the pillars, each listening to a separate stress path rather than echoing her judgment. Caldrin arranged witnesses in four surviving court bays. Refugees from Ashwall, Sul Venn observers, Salt Aeli navigators, and coalition captains each received the cost map copied from the tablets.

Sevrin climbed the lens gallery with Saan Rel. They cleaned salt film from the mirrors using vinegar and felt. One by one, shafts of upper daylight crossed the chamber. The beams struck the pool and seemed to continue below it, spearing toward buried constellations. His leaf-filaments mapped relations faster than thought. Upper Cinder Net paired with a lower rain cluster. Rootglass's absent pollination path curved between the Salt Mother and a dim green pattern beyond the hinge.

The bees knew before he finished.

He opened the sample frame. Thirty-two workers rose in a tight spiral. Their wings scattered points of copper pollen through the daylight beam. They flew downward toward the pool, stopped a finger above its surface, and turned along an invisible road. Sevrin followed their line with chalk on the gallery floor.

Saan caught his wrist. “That path crosses Neris's winter current.”

“I see it.”

“You saw it three years ago.”

The accusation required no raised voice. Sevrin kept his hand on the chalk. “I saw losses. I did not see this chamber.”

“You saw enough to tell us.”

“Yes.”

Below, Pell called for the next mirror angle. Labor continued around his confession.

Sevrin marked the Neris crossing in red rather than conceal it among white lines. “Witness the cost.”

“I will witness that you hid it first.”

“Do both.”

A horn sounded in the upper shaft.

Not theirs.

Kharza ordered shields toward the north gallery. Pell's people locked the counterweight bars but left the lines slack. Caldrin closed the tablet cases. The witnesses withdrew behind the lens pool, carrying the maps rather than abandoning them.

The first arrow struck a mirror and filled the gallery with bright fragments.

Irel Saan entered behind a line of orchard guards in green-glass lamellar. Sevrin had last seen her among Rootglass trunks, holding the queen-hive as though every road beyond the orchard led toward theft. Here she wore a salt veil and carried a pruning spear. Heartland engineers crowded the shaft behind her. Reed soldiers advanced through a western gallery, lacquered cane shields overlapping.

“Close the comb,” Irel called.

The bees circled the broken mirror, confused by scattered light.

Sevrin spread both hands. “Look at their flight.”

“I see stolen workers driven against buried pollen.”

“They chose the lower path.”

“After you starved them of every other path.”

A royal engineer raised a crossbow toward the sample frame. Irel knocked the weapon down with her spear.

“The hive material remains under orchard law,” she said without looking back.

The engineer cursed. Kharza used the hesitation.

Coalition shields struck the gallery mouth. Mahr soldiers drove the engineers back from the mirrors, not toward the shaft, because blocking retreat would turn a contest for position into slaughter. Reed companies pushed along the western terrace toward the lower counterweights. Their objective lay in the ropes. Cut those, and the coalition could not distribute the Salt Hinge's load.

Pell sent half her haulers to defend the lines and kept half at their stations. “A counterweight abandoned in battle becomes expensive scenery,” she shouted.

Sevrin crawled along the lens rail while arrows crossed above him. He gathered the sample frame and tucked it under his coat. Two worker bees clung to his filaments. The rest kept following the paired light, even as boots shattered chalk marks beneath them.

Saan Rel dragged a wounded mirror tender behind a column. Blood soaked the elf's right sleeve. “The upper beam is broken.”

“Three beams remain.”

“Two. Look.”

Smoke poured from the north gallery. Osra's engineers had thrown damp wool onto braziers, not to burn the chamber but to blind the light paths. The bees dropped lower, losing orientation.

Sevrin ran for the chain controls. Irel met him on the narrow bridge above the pool.

Their spears stayed behind them. Their bodies did not.

She caught his coat and forced him against the rail. “If Salt turns now, Rootglass may lose its last leafing.”

“If it does not move, the pressure will break this observatory and reach the orchard anyway.”

“Your figures do not earn trust because you finally admit them.”

“No. The bees earn attention. Watch them.”

A worker stumbled through smoke and landed on Irel's wrist. Copper pollen dusted her skin. The insect turned toward the lower green constellation even without direct light.

A crash from the north gallery broke their standoff. Heartland engineers had reached the first mirror winch. They drove an iron locking spike through its drum, freezing the beam over the wrong buried star. Three bees followed the false line and struck the pool. Their wings flashed once beneath the surface.

Sevrin shoved past Irel. She pursued him, whether to stop him or protect the hive material he could not tell. The bridge ended at a ladder above the contested winch. Coalition archers held the upper rail; royal shields occupied the floor. Neither could shoot without striking mirror tenders.

He climbed down the chain instead.

A soldier grabbed his ankle. Sevrin kicked free, dropped the final six feet, and landed beside the drum. The locking spike had bent under celestial load. Pulling it straight would take tools they did not have. He wrapped both hands around the warm iron while leaf-filaments streamed toward the trapped beam.

Irel landed beside him with greater grace and worse temper. “You will tear your hands.”

“Then turn the drum when it clears.”

She jammed her pruning spear under the spike. Together they levered. An engineer swung a pry bar at Sevrin's head. Irel blocked it with the spear shaft. One of Kharza's captains drove the engineer back, then stopped when Sevrin ordered him not to strike the mirror frame.

The spike came free. The winch spun. Chain tore skin from Sevrin's palms before Irel caught the brake. The beam traveled across the pool, searching. Bees rose from the smoke to follow it.

“Two degrees left,” Sevrin said.

“I can read a mirror scale.”

“Then why are you helping?”

“I am preventing your incompetence from becoming Rootglass's funeral.”

She aligned the beam exactly, then kicked the spike into the brine. For that narrow task they had acted together. The next decision still divided them.

Below the bridge, Reed soldiers captured a counterweight landing. Their war speaker ordered coalition haulers away but forbade his own fighters to cut occupied lines. One Heartland officer countermanded him. A royal sapper swung an axe at the main rope.

The Reed speaker struck the sapper with the flat of his hook. “My treaty says seize. It does not say bury us.”

Conflicting commands opened a gap. Pell's haulers rolled two loose stone weights down the service ramp. The blocks hit shield edges, broke the formation without crushing bodies, and stopped against a drainage curb. Lessa's medics pulled wounded from both uniforms into the dry witness bay. Each rescue reduced somebody's fighting line and enlarged the group with reason to prevent collapse.

For a few breaths the objectives stood exposed. Osra's officers needed intact tablets, hive material, and a locked hinge. Irel needed the pollinators alive. Reed speakers needed control over any cost imposed on their marsh. The coalition needed movement before structural failure. No side wanted the same victory, which made the chamber more dangerous than a simple battle.

Irel stared at the worker on her wrist for one breath. Then she closed her hand around Sevrin's collar again. “One colony does not authorize a region's destruction.”

“Nor does one gardener authorize its slow death.”

The floor lurched.

Brine burst through the southern doors.

A wall of black water swept across the lowest terrace, taking two royal engineers and a Vennik line tender off their feet. Ropes snapped taut. One portable counterweight toppled, crushed a bench, and dragged its partner toward the pool.

Pell jumped onto the trailing bar. Her small weight changed nothing until three haulers joined her. “Cut the fallen line, not the live one!”

A Reed soldier reached the rope first. He raised his hook.

Kharza's niece Lessa tackled him at the knees. They went into knee-deep brine together. The soldier surfaced without his silver-capped helmet and stared at the medic who had chosen wrestling over a blade. Lessa shoved the correct rope into his hands.

“Cut that or we all drown.”

He cut it.

The fallen weight vanished into the pool. The remaining system slammed level. Stone-song rolled through the chamber, deep enough to blur Sevrin's sight.

Kharza staggered against a pillar. Blood appeared beneath the copper around her left tusk. “Load rising from the south. Hinge will jump if it moves without balance.”

“We need all three lower bars,” Pell called.

“One is gone.”

“Then we need its enemy's replacement.”

Across the brine, royal engineers had brought their own load bars to lock the hinge. Pell pointed at them as if requesting borrowed cookware.

Tactics dissolved into water and weight. Coalition soldiers fought to hold the mirror galleries. Reed forces tried to seize the witness bays. Royal engineers defended their bars from the same brine threatening everyone. The pool rose over its stone rim and turned the central floor into a current.

Sevrin saw the paired lights bend. Pressure did not merely distort them now. It pulled the upper beam toward the lower constellation with enough force to ignite pollen in the air. His leaf-filaments shone at the tips.

Pain entered like cold wire.

He released the rail. Tiny burns ran from his wrists to his scalp. The star path had become visible inside his own growth, every filament carrying too much relation between the skies.

Irel caught him before he fell into the pool.

“Close yourself,” she said.

“I don't know how.”

“Drop the reading.”

“If I drop it, the bees lose the path.”

“Then let them.”

Below them, a royal lock bar cracked. Salt Hinge shifted less than a finger and threw everyone toward the southern wall. A ceiling plate fell into the brine. Behind it, the buried sky showed through naked stone, stars burning close enough to cast shadows.

Nobody could leave the hinge fixed. The chamber had lost that option.

Sevrin pulled the red cost map from his coat and thrust it at Irel. “Neris crosses here. Reedwater here. Rootglass beyond this green line. Help me keep the bees on the narrow arc, or the movement spreads across all three.”

“You cannot promise the narrow arc will hold.”

“No. I can show you what breaks if it doesn't.”

Irel read the marks. She looked toward her orchard guards. Three defended the royal engineers from Kharza's soldiers; two had broken formation to pull drowning witnesses from the current. Her command contained disagreement even before she gave it words.

“Orchard guard,” she shouted. “Preserve the hive path. No attack on pollinators or mirror crews. Engineers, bring the west bars to Pell Vask.”

The Heartland captain seized her arm. “Our order requires the hinge secured for the regent.”

“The hinge is breaking beneath your order.”

“Stand down.”

Irel struck his hand away. Half her guards followed her toward the mirrors. The rest stayed with the captain, faces hidden behind salt veils. Aeli did not become one people because an old court had named their practice.

Aruk's Reed war speaker raised his hook above the lower bars. “Our terms forbid movement without Reed witnesses.”

Pell, waist-deep in brine, pointed toward the court bays. “Send witnesses.”

“You have no Reed seat.”

“We have three marsh counters captured at Ember Ford who joined the refugee column yesterday.”

“Prisoners do not speak for us.”

“No,” Kharza called. She had reached the pillar nearest him, one hand clamped around her tusk. “You do. Witness your refusal or witness the cost, but choose before the south load splits this room.”

The war speaker looked at the water jar hanging from his belt, cloudy with Lower Nest salt. He sent two soldiers to the court bay. He did not join Kharza. He ordered his people to loan the bars under Reed supervision.

Separate authority, shared danger.

Before the bearing could begin, they had to recover the southern witness bay. Brine had trapped five people behind a fallen screen, including both Reed counters and Caldrin's youngest copyist. The water already reached their chests. The direct stair had vanished under a current strong enough to roll helmets.

Pell proposed a suspended crossing. Her team fixed a mirror chain between pillars and hung cargo loops from it. Kharza rejected the south pillar after hearing a fracture inside. Ral Olt, still an observer rather than her soldier, tested the west support and offered his own reading. Their reports disagreed by a hand's width. They marked both limits and placed the line between them.

Sevrin could contribute only sight. He watched the buried stars through the flooding pool and called each pressure pulse before it lifted the water. Haulers crossed during the intervals. Lessa went first with bandages tied above her head. She reached the copyist, fitted a sling, and sent him across while arrows struck the upper chain.

An orchard guard covered the rescue from Irel's gallery. A coalition bowman covered her in turn. Neither acknowledged the exchange.

The last Reed counter refused the sling until she had pressed her witness mark into Caldrin's wet clay. “My speaker may withdraw our bars,” she said through chattering teeth. “He may not erase that I saw the cost.”

A pulse lifted the brine. Kharza shouted warning. Sevrin saw the lower Salt Mother brighten and counted the rise aloud. Pell's team hauled the counter clear a heartbeat before the screen collapsed.

Lessa returned with a Heartland engineer she found submerged against the rail. He had stopped breathing. She drained his lungs and worked his chest while both armies watched. When he coughed brine onto her knees, his captain demanded him back.

“He can choose after he breathes twice without help,” Lessa said.

The engineer chose the royal gallery. Lessa let him go with her blanket around his shoulders. Rescue had not purchased allegiance, and nobody claimed it should.

The recovered counters took their court bay. Caldrin read each affected region from the cost map. Neris: colder current and harbor risk. Reedwater: a short brine retreat followed by uncertain nursery pressure. Heartland: rain across siege roads and reduced late grain warmth. Rootglass: possible opening of a dormant pollen route, with no guarantee of orchard survival. Witnesses repeated the entries until every working group could hear.

Only then did Pell raise her hand for the bars to move.

Pell rebuilt the missing counterweight with royal bars and chains from the mirror gallery. The arrangement looked asymmetrical because equal load did not require equal shapes. Kharza assigned each Mahr reader one pillar and demanded independent reports. Caldrin shouted the emergency clause while witnesses pressed marks into wet field clay. Irel aligned the remaining upper beams. Saan Rel held the Neris cost map where every bay could see it.

Sevrin opened the sample comb fully.

The bees rose through smoke. Irel adjusted a mirror by hand, catching daylight on its broken half. A thin beam struck the pool. Workers followed it to the buried green arc, carrying pollen between skies.

Salt Hinge began to turn.

Not far. One degree, measured by the old groove as stone moved against stone. Yet the observatory made that distance enormous. Buried constellations slid under the pool. Brine climbed the walls, paused, and rushed toward the eastern drains. Above them, daylight changed from white to pearl gray.

The movement tore through Sevrin's leaf-filaments.

Every strand flared. He smelled green wood scorched after rain. Pain erased the chamber, then returned it in fragments: Irel's hand gripping his shoulder, bees spinning in copper light, Kharza screaming a stress count, Pell hanging from a line with her boots off the floor.

He tried to release the path. The path had already burned into him.

His filaments blackened from tip to root. Some fell across his eyes like ash. Others remained attached, carrying no light at all. The twelve-year growth beneath his skin closed around the injury. He understood with a starwarden's precision what that meant. There would be no next leafing. The root beds had cauterized.

He kept the frame open until the last worker crossed.

Then darkness arrived, though lamps still burned.

Irel lowered him to the wet stone. “Sevrin. Look at me.”

“I can see your face.”

“Follow the lower green line.”

He turned toward where memory placed it. Nothing in his body answered. The constellations had become ordinary light beyond ordinary distance.

“I cannot.”

The hinge settled into its new bearing.

A second surge hit the chamber. This one came from above. Rainwater poured down the coalition shaft while brine rose through the royal galleries. Kharza listened once and ordered immediate evacuation by separate routes. No side could cross the central floor without fighting the current and each other.

Irel's divided guard withdrew through the northern shaft with the royal engineers and Reed companies. Before leaving, she gathered three surviving workers into a reed vial. The queen-hive remained far away at Rootglass, still under her custody.

“Your stolen sample has changed the hive's law,” she told Sevrin. “It has not changed mine.”

“What will?”

“A path that lets Rootglass live.”

She climbed out with her wounded.

The coalition took the western drain. Pell's people carried the tablets above water in slings. Lessa bound Kharza's bleeding tusk and Sevrin's burned scalp while walking. Caldrin repeated witness names until a clerk had them all.

They reached the salt surface near dawn on Day Twenty. Behind them, part of the Sunken Observatory collapsed into the buried sky. Both armies emerged from different shafts and formed exhausted lines too far apart to resume battle.

Clouds had gathered east of Vael Tern. Wind moved across Hollow Salt carrying moisture away from Osra's siege roads and cold toward the Heartland approaches. Salt Hinge had moved one degree. The siege ring would now have to survive weather it had not provisioned for.

Sevrin stood beneath the altered upper sky. He could see clouds, faces, weapons, and the black rim of the sinkhole. He could no longer see how any star paired with another.

Pell put the recovered sample frame in his hands. Eleven workers remained alive.

He listened to their wings without pretending they restored what had burned away. Then he gave the frame to Saan Rel for safekeeping and began dictating every path he could still remember before memory changed its shape.

Chapter 14 — A Season With Nowhere to Go

Sevrin held the map upside down for half a minute before Saan Rel corrected him.

“The Neris coast lies under your left hand,” she said.

He turned the board. Ordinary ink remained perfectly visible: rivers, orchard councils, tide stations, three generations of corrections. What had vanished lay between the marks. No paired constellation drew his attention from coast to buried sky. No filament tightened at false alignment. The map had become an object instead of a living relation.

Around him, the coalition's weather tent snapped under a cold wind from Salt Hinge. His burned scalp carried a linen dressing. The root beds beneath it felt hot and absent at once. Eleven star-bees worked inside Saan's sample frame near the lamp, their flight unreadable to him except as movement.

“Again,” he said.

Saan placed three transparent sheets over the map. One carried tide measures from before Salt moved. Another held Caldrin's copied cost lines. The third showed reports collected during the retreat from the Sunken Observatory. Sevrin aligned them by nail holes rather than instinct.

Neris appeared first as a widening blue stain.

A one-degree movement had shortened the harbor current by less than a day. That change alone would not flood the coast. The danger came from accumulated pressure and what a full immediate Great Turning would release into the same winter track. Old tide markers predicted water entering the lower wards of Neris and crossing the sea wall at four salt towns.

“Check the scale,” Sevrin said.

Saan did. Tei Vask checked the tablet conversion. A human river surveyor named Hal Crow used a plumb line and arrived at the same contour. Sevrin made them repeat the calculation without his assumptions.

The result remained.

Neris would flood.

They tested the finding against living memory. Saan summoned three coastal caravaners sheltering with the coalition: an elderly rope maker from the lower harbor, a Salt Aeli pilot, and a human well keeper who had left Neris after the third failed hive season. None saw Sevrin's first contour. Pell required them to draw storm water from memory on blank boards.

The rope maker marked the alleys where winter tides backed through drains. The pilot marked an offshore shelf that redirected surge toward the east gate. The well keeper placed the freshwater mouths higher than Sevrin's archive map, because two had been rebuilt after subsidence. Their accounts narrowed one danger and enlarged another. The eastern wards might gain six hours, but evacuees climbing from the ropewalks would converge on the same stair used to move water uphill.

“Boats?” Sevrin asked.

The pilot shook her head. “A flood current enters against the harbor chain. Boats inside cannot clear until the chain drops. Boats outside cannot enter while it holds.”

“Who controls it?”

“Neris council in peace. The harbor captain during storm warning. Osra's appointee at present.”

Sevrin added a required action to the warning: lower the chain before the projected surge, then reserve upper quays for evacuation rather than grain speculation. The rope maker demanded that hive docks receive equal notice. Rich warehouses had bells; dockside keepers had runners.

They built a time estimate from feet rather than hope. A child or elder climbing from the lowest ropewalk needed two hours to reach cliff shelters in dry weather. Moving hive boxes doubled congestion. Night movement required lamps that the lower wards could not afford. With every public bell, open stair, and upper storehouse available, the threatened district could clear in two days. With resistance from harbor authority, four might not suffice.

Sevrin wrote both estimates. Evidence no longer served as a single number chosen for persuasion.

Not all of it. Precision mattered. The upper harbor, cliff storehouses, and western salt enclave would remain dry.
