The lower ropewalks, hive docks, poor wards, and three freshwater wells lay inside the projected surge. Sevrin had grown up above the second well. His family home stood on safe stone. The dockside keepers whose losses he had hidden lived below it.

He wrote the threatened wards by name.

Kharza entered while he reached Reedwater. The general had replaced the bloodied copper around her left tusk. Lessa had wound the new strip wider across her jaw, making speech uncomfortable. Kharza looked at the blue Neris stain, then at the hour marks beside Vael Tern.

“How bad?”

“For Neris, a full movement now sends water above the lower sea wall.”

“How many days to evacuate?”

“Unknown.”

“That means you have not counted.”

“That means roads, boats, tide phase, and warning trust change the count. Give me until tonight.”

“Vael may not have until tonight.”

She placed Pell's latest courier sheet on the table. Osra had closed the ring, cut incoming food, and begun work near the north aqueduct. Maela's coded marks put ward stores below the optimistic estimate. The city had not yet breached. Hunger already occupied the plan.

Sevrin forced himself back to Reedwater.

The marsh calculation carried different harm. Salt Hinge's single degree had briefly pulled brine from three western channels. A complete movement would send it back along a colder route, holding salt in nursery islands through the seed-reed season. Lower Nest could lose two plantings. Reedwater might retain navigable water and still lose the food grown beside it.

A marsh counter named Vek Venn spread channel cords over the map. Silver caps covered both her tusks. She refused Sevrin's first contour because his old survey treated floating islands as fixed land.

“They migrate every flood year,” she said. “Your line drowns empty water and saves farms that moved six miles east.”

“Show me.”

She reset the cords according to barge records. The threatened area shifted. It grew.

Sevrin copied her revision under her name. “How much freshwater offsets this?”

“More than Osra released. Less than the eastern canal carries if Heartland gates open fully.”

“Would Aruk order that?”

“Aruk does not command the gates.”

Every map returned to custody.

Vek took Sevrin to the coalition's marsh camp to show what the cords represented. Reed families had built their shelters on narrow sled-barges that could be pulled when channels shifted. Salt from Ember's rain consequence had dried along the runners. Children scraped it into bowls because wasting edible salt offended their elders, even when too much of it had poisoned the water.

At a seed rack, bundles of reed grain hung by district. Lower Nest seed carried blue ties; eastern islands used yellow shell beads. Vek split one Lower Nest stalk. The center had softened into gray paste.

“Freshwater might save the parent beds,” she said. “It will not restore seed already spoiled.”

“How many households depend on these?”

“Do not ask households until you ask boats. Three families may share one planting barge. A widow may own seed while a council owns the mud bed. Your tables want one owner per loss.”

“Then the tables are wrong.”

They revised categories in the rain. Planting barges, nursery islands, fishing channels, household stores, council stores, and the labor of towing fresh water each required separate lines. A full movement made some eastern channels easier to navigate while destroying their seed banks. Calling Reedwater a single loser would conceal which councils gained passage and which lost food.

A young counter asked whether the coalition would pay them if Kharza ordered movement anyway. Sevrin could promise no treasury. He wrote obligations instead: Heartland canal release, coalition water carts, Sul Venn seed purchases, priority passage for nursery barges. Vek marked each promise unfunded until Pell assigned actual stores.

That word prevented concern from masquerading as provision.

By afternoon the tent held delegates from regions that had never shared a weather table. Sul Venn observers marked caravan wells. Ashwall refugees identified terraces too damaged to absorb more rain. Salt Aeli navigators corrected currents. Pell assigned two counters to every number, one from a benefiting region and one from a burdened one.

No season had anywhere harmless to go.

Kharza waited through the work, increasingly still. When the last counter left, she closed the tent flap.

“We need Salt Hinge moving farther before Osra locks Crown Hinge,” she said.

“Farther now could kill the people we claim to include.”

“Doing nothing leaves Vael under siege and the buried pressure under every wall.”

“I did not say nothing.”

“You said tonight, unknown, repeat, verify. Those words protect an expert from error. They do not protect a city from cavalry.”

Sevrin looked at her copper wrap. “You moved Ember under immediate collapse. You saved refugees and spent Reedwater's fresh channels. Would you repeat that choice knowing the cost?”

“Yes.”

The answer came without pause.

Vek, still outside the flap, went silent.

Kharza heard her. Shame crossed the general's face but did not change her statement. “I would name the cost and owe it. I would not let the ford fold with people on it while searching for a painless degree.”

“This is not Ember Ford. We have time to find a narrower path.”

“How much time?”

Sevrin could not see the stars that might have answered. He relied on instrument marks and Pell's courier intervals. “Days. Not weeks.”

“Vael has days. Rootglass lies away from Vael.”

“Rootglass Hinge controls the cloud shade and river thaw that shape both forecasts. If its pollen route joins Salt's narrow arc, we may move pressure without sending the whole burden through one coast.”

“May.”

“Yes. I have done enough harm turning uncertainty into confidence.”

Kharza leaned both hands on the table. “And I have done enough harm making delay sound like care while somebody else held the wall. We march toward Vael. If you want Rootglass, find a route that does not cost the army the siege.”

“You cannot command an orchard into our road.”

“No. I can command our departure. Bring me a choice before it.”

She left.

Sevrin sat with the disagreement. Friendship had begun between them not as softness but as an ability to hear what the other refused. Kharza refused delay disguised as innocence. He refused urgency that hid where the water went. Both refusals belonged in the plan. Neither could rule alone.

He added Kharza's position to the map margin in her own terms: Vael's siege clock, Crown access, structural pressure, civilians already consuming reduced stores. A cost map that recorded only climatic injury would become another expert's concealment. Delay also had real, named households attached to it.

Saan opened the sample frame. The eleven workers emerged slowly. Since the observatory, they had ignored upper-sky pollen and clustered along the eastern mesh whenever cold wind arrived. Sevrin could no longer read their celestial orientation, so he asked Saan to describe each turn.

“Three circle the lamp. Five walk the lower edge. Two face east. One grooms.”

“Which east?”

“The map's east, not yours.”

Her correction carried no cruelty. His own body no longer supplied direction through star relation. He rotated the frame and observed that the same two bees returned to the eastern edge.

“They are following something toward Rootglass,” he said.

“Or away from Salt.”

“Both can be true.”

They tested temperature, lamp angle, mineral dust, and pollen. Only a sliver of green pollen from the Sunken Observatory changed the workers' behavior. The bees lined along a route northeast across Hollow Salt, then curved toward the Rootglass river basin.

Saan found a faded entry in the Compact cost map. It named seasonal resting gardens between Salt Hinge and Rootglass, places later orchard histories omitted. Tei matched the old Vennik route marks to abandoned cisterns used by caravans before royal toll roads shifted south.

A migration corridor had once connected the two hinges.

It did not pass through the heart of Rootglass. It skirted the western orchards, crossed mineral fields now considered barren, and reached a neglected lower gate. If Irel's queen-hive accepted that corridor, pollen could distribute pressure through living stations rather than force it across Neris alone.

“If the gardens survive,” Saan said.

“If their wells survive. If the lower gate opens. If Irel listens.”

“Your gift for reassurance has not improved.”

“It nearly ruined us when it was better.”

They left before dusk with six scouts, Vek, Tei, and the sample frame. Kharza gave Sevrin one day to find evidence, not permission. The main coalition began packing eastward toward Vael while his party followed the old cistern marks north.

The first resting garden held only salt grass and a collapsed wall. Bees explored its dry stones without landing. At the second, they found a sealed well under glassy roots. Vennik jacks lifted the cover. Cold air rose carrying the scent of dormant green pollen.

The route between them demanded more than following marks. Salt wind had buried the old caravan cuts, and recent rain turned the basin floors into traps with hard crust over black mud. Tei walked ahead with a weighted probe. Twice the crust failed under it. The party shifted along higher mineral ribs, losing hours but not animals.

At one abandoned cistern, they found signs of people already displaced by the altered season. Six Reed households had dragged nursery barges onto dry ground after brine entered their home channel. An Aeli glassworker from the western orchard had traded them root meal for fish. Neither group intended to join Kharza or Osra. They wanted to know whether the next weather change would make the cistern drinkable.

Sevrin showed them the unfinished map. He marked the well as unknown instead of safe. The glassworker recognized an orchard maintenance cut leading toward Rootglass's lower gate but warned that Irel's council had sealed it after fungal blight thirty years earlier. The Reed elder knew a parallel watercourse omitted from Aeli maps because marsh fishers used it only in flood years.

Two local memories produced a viable route where the archive showed none.

They tested the cistern with silverleaf paper. Salt remained high but falling. Vek left one counter to measure it each dawn. Tei issued the displaced families enough lamp oil for four nights from coalition stores and recorded the debt against the corridor survey. Discovery consumed provisions. Pell would insist the map carry that cost too.

By the time the party reached the second garden, Sevrin understood that reviving the corridor meant agreements over wells, passage, quarantine, and seed, not merely releasing bees along an ancient line. Rootglass could not own it alone. Neither could a marching army.

Workers entered the well shaft and returned dusted pale green.

Sevrin felt nothing. Saan's filaments curved so sharply toward them that she gasped.

“The pairing continues below the dead garden,” she said.

At the third station, fresh boot prints crossed the old route. Orchard guards. Irel had already traveled this way with the queen-hive after leaving the observatory. She had not retreated directly to Rootglass. She had tested the corridor herself.

They followed at distance and found a camp abandoned that morning. A hive stand had pressed four feet into the soil. Wax flakes carried the queen-hive's scent. Beside them, Irel had left a gardener's warning cut into bark: Lower gate blocked. Western wells viable. Do not follow with soldiers.

“She knew we would come,” Tei said.

“She knew the bees would,” Sevrin answered.

The evidence did not secure her alliance. It established that she had not dismissed the path.

Sevrin used the abandoned stand as a writing table. He prepared two warnings with identical maps.

To Maela and Kharza he wrote that immediate full movement risked flooding the lower Neris wards and salting Reedwater's seed-reed islands. He named the Rootglass migration corridor as the best surviving route for a narrower distribution and stated that Irel had tested it without consenting to coalition control.

To Osra and Aruk he wrote the same forecast, including the projected harm to Heartland grain and the freshwater gate requirements for Reedwater. He did not soften the danger to Neris to win Osra's delay. He did not soften Vael's siege risk to win Kharza's patience.

Vek read both copies. Saan verified the contours. Tei sealed them with separate witness marks.

One courier rode toward the siege ring under Salt Aeli colors. Another followed Kharza's column. A third carried the Rootglass portion to Irel, though her scouts almost certainly watched from the mineral ridges.

Sevrin remained at the hive stand until the workers settled. Stars appeared above Hollow Salt. He saw every point as light and none as a path.

When he returned to Kharza after midnight, he put the verified map in her hands.

“We do not force a full turn,” she said after reading.

“No.”

“We reach Vael Tern before its food ends.”

“Yes.”

“We bring Reedwater, Neris, and Rootglass into the decision, even if some arrive as opponents.”

“That is the only route I can defend.”

Kharza studied the marked migration corridor, then gave orders to shift the coalition's northern column toward its surviving wells. She had not yielded the siege clock. Sevrin had not yielded the cost.

By dawn their goal had changed. They would not race to complete the Great Turning at the first hinge they could reach. They would carry the Compact tablets, the flood forecast, and the corridor evidence to Crown Hinge with every affected people given a place to answer.

Ahead, the road bent toward Vael Tern. North of it, unseen by Sevrin and visible through the bees, the old migration corridor waited beneath dead gardens for the queen-hive to choose it.

Part III

Chapter 15 — The Long March to Vael Tern

Kharza gave away the first road on the morning they left Hollow Salt.

Three routes crossed the white basin. Her old instinct chose the central causeway, shortest by half a day and broad enough for cavalry. Vek Venn's marsh counters warned that cold from Salt Hinge had softened the crust below it. Tei Vask preferred northern caravan wells. A Sul Venn observer argued for the southern ridge because refugees already occupied the wells to the north.

Every face turned toward Kharza.

“Route council decides,” she said. “Vek judges ground, Tei judges water and wagons, refugee delegates judge displacement. I judge enemy contact. We move only when three agree.”

A captain from her former imperial command frowned. “And if they divide?”

“Then I choose after all three objections enter the march record.”

It sounded slower than command. It prevented her from pretending one mind could feel every load.

They chose the northern route with a diversion around occupied wells. The decision cost four hours and kept thirty water carts from breaking through the softened causeway before scouts found Osra's abandoned stakes beneath the crust.

Thus began eight days toward Vael Tern.

On Day Twenty-Four, cold rain drove across Hollow Salt while upper sunlight remained hard enough to blister skin. Soldiers marched in cloaks with wet cloth beneath their helmets. Kharza's cracked left tusk rang whenever buried pressure shifted. Lessa changed the copper wrap at noon and forbade her to bite through pain.

“Command with your mouth open if you must,” her niece said. “Nobody respects you for bleeding into your own stomach.”

By sunset they reached a field of salt pinnacles. Wind had exposed bodies from an older caravan disaster, preserved with hands still gripping harness. Caldrin identified royal levy tokens from the last delayed Turning. The army could not bury them in salt rock. Vennik haulers built cairns from broken road markers while Salt Aeli witnesses copied names where tokens remained legible.

Kharza assigned one company and tried to move the column.

The burial dispute widened before it resolved. Salt Aeli navigators wanted the bodies left facing the buried current, a custom for travelers who died without sea. Kiln soldiers proposed firing the tokens in a portable ancestor kiln. Vennik haulers objected that claiming unknown dead into one house rite could erase families still searching for them.

Caldrin found a solution in the old road law. Unidentified travelers received markers made from the road itself, with every known token copied and carried forward to the next public court. The cairns would belong to place, not ancestry. Individual families could later reclaim names without disturbing the bodies.

Kharza divided the labor by choice. Mahr soldiers moved stone. Salt Aeli witnesses washed tokens. Vennik scribes made paired copies. Human deserters built a drainage lip so the next rain would not open the graves. Refugee children pressed route marks into wet clay plaques.

One token carried only a number and a grain-porter's hook. Kharza remembered porters from the Three Valleys campaign, people counted under wagon strength until dead and under civilians afterward. She pressed the hook into the first plaque herself.

The rites consumed more than two hours because exposed bodies required wrapping. Pell released six lengths of worn tent cloth and recorded them against burial duty rather than medical stores. Nobody objected to the column. Categories could shape whether respect became theft.

When the final cairn closed, the refugee delegates asked Kharza to speak. Her former imperial troops expected a commander's honor. She gave names, occupations where known, and the fact that the old crown had left their deaths unrecorded. She did not call them sacrifices. No cause had asked their consent.

The halt changed the march. Rear companies began reporting roadside graves instead of passing them. Scouts carried clay and charcoal with signal flags. By Vael Ridge, the copied roll held forty-three names and nineteen identifying marks. The army had lost hours and gained a record broad enough to include people outside its banners.

The refugee delegates had forced that change by refusing the original order to move on. Their dead from Ashwall had received hurried trenches because the vanguard approached; these unknown travelers had waited decades. Leaving them exposed would have announced that march time still outweighed unclaimed bodies.

Kharza had looked east, feeling Vael's invisible siege clock, and granted the burial crews the hours they needed. Then she put her own hands to stone. Delegated authority did not free her from labor.

Day Twenty-Five brought heat from below. Mud steamed under cold air. Two draft oxen collapsed. Pell redistributed their loads across twelve carts and slaughtered the animals before fever spoiled the meat. Every company received less than a mouthful. Hides became rain covers; horn became wedges; bone went into Lessa's medical boil. Nothing disappeared merely because grief made inventory indecent.

A messenger from Maela found them after dark. Vael's outer farms had closed. Osra controlled harvest fields and allowed only sick exchanges. Ward stores issued rye by household. The north aqueduct faced attack.

Kharza read the message to the whole camp. She did not remove the line naming uncertainty in Maela's ration count.

On Day Twenty-Six, three Heartland companies appeared along the ridge with blue cloth tied over their spearheads. They had deserted Osra after receiving Sevrin's Neris warning and learning the public grain figure used the upper bound. Their captain offered seven hundred soldiers if Kharza retained their ranks.

“Why did you serve her?” Kharza asked.

“Because my valley survived the Ash Famine by fixing the sky.”

“Why leave?”

“Because she told us only our graves counted.”

Kharza accepted them under separate companies and appointed no coalition officers over their internal watches. Ral Olt witnessed the terms. The deserters surrendered siege maps but kept their banner furled, neither forgiven nor humiliated.

That night somebody painted queen's dogs on their ration cart. Lessa made the responsible Kiln soldiers clean it. Kharza ordered no loyalty oath. A march taught allegiance through repeated access to food, voice, and danger, or it taught nothing.

The first river rose on Day Twenty-Seven.

Salt Hinge's cold had driven rain into the western hills. The ford Pell expected lay under six feet of brown water. Upstream, an old stone bridge could carry people but not loaded wagons. Downstream, Vennik ferry ruins offered two intact towers and no deck.

Pell took the decision from the route council because weights belonged to her authority. She ran a cable between the towers, hung cargo slings beneath it, and used empty water barrels as floats. Soldiers crossed the bridge in units. Wagons came apart on the western bank, traveled over the river piece by piece, and returned to wheels by morning.

Osra's cavalry attacked while the third grain load hung above the current.

Their objective lay in rope, not bodies. Twenty riders split toward the tower anchors while archers fired oil-wrapped shafts. Kharza gave the defense to the defecting Heartland captain, whose soldiers knew cavalry feints. She kept Kiln troops on the grain bank and did not seize his line when riders broke through.

The captain abandoned twenty yards of ground, drew the cavalry between dismantled wagons, and trapped them behind raised axles. Vek's counters dumped river water over the burning anchor rope. Pell cut one cargo sling so grain, not the main cable, fell into the river.

They lost six sacks, four soldiers, seven attackers, and half a day. The cable held. The defecting company earned trust by preserving food for people who had insulted them the previous night.

On Day Twenty-Eight, they passed fresh graves marked with Vael ward signs. Sick civilians released through Osra's exchange had died on the road after finding surrounding villages stripped of spare food. Coalition medics counted each grave and added the names to Maela's courier roll. Siege policy traveled beyond walls.

Weather reversed again on Day Twenty-Nine. Upper clouds cleared. Buried cold froze yesterday's mud into ridges sharp enough to split hooves. Tei ordered straw wrapped around animals' feet. Cavalry officers complained until Pell showed them the replacement-horse column, which held zero.

Kharza spent the afternoon among rear wagons instead of the vanguard. She heard which axle grease had run out, which new companies drew late rations, and which refugees feared being left outside Vael if the city refused entry. Command maps showed distance. Rear wheels showed whether distance could be crossed.

A fight broke out beside the medical wagons when Kiln soldiers tried to move refugee bedding off a dry cart for arrow bundles. Their sergeant cited battle priority. Nim Vask, who had joined Pell's rear service with evacuation barges dismantled onto wagons, asked him to define battle.

“The part where weapons keep us alive,” he said.

“The bedding belongs to fever families. Move it into rain and you create thirty patients. Your arrows then defend a slower column.”

The sergeant appealed to Kharza. Her old command would have settled ownership by rank. Instead she called Lessa, Pell, and the refugee cart delegate. Lessa confirmed twelve active fevers. Pell found a grain wagon due to empty by midnight. The delegate offered to shift bedding after the grain issued, provided soldiers supplied lifting labor and a new oilskin.

Kharza approved the agreement and entered the delayed arrow movement in the record under her own authority. If an attack came before midnight, the cost would attach to her decision, not vanish into civilian obstruction.

The sergeant obeyed but remained angry. “A general should decide faster.”

“A general did. The decision took long enough to discover what it would damage.”

At dusk the grain wagon emptied. Soldiers moved the bedding under dry cover, loaded the arrows, and repaired two refugee cart wheels while they waited. No one called the compromise unity. It functioned.

News worsened. Osra's earthworks reached the north aqueduct. Vael had stopped baking in private ovens to save fuel. Maela kept the inner gates open between wards, but merchant guards demanded preference at storehouses. Pell sent public ration sheets ahead by three couriers so ward captains could compare siege claims against actual coalition supplies.

Day Thirty brought the second river, divided into five shallow channels across reed flats. Aruk's patrols had removed bridge planks. The Reed war speaker left a tablet on the far bank: Turn back. Your movement harms the marsh you claim to save.

Vek read it twice. “He is not wrong about the harm.”

“No,” Kharza said. “He is wrong about who may close the answer.”

They built reed mats instead of a bridge, spreading wagon load across mud. Marsh families in the column directed the weave. Kiln soldiers cut reeds badly and learned. Salt Aeli scouts found the firmest channels. Crossing took a day without battle. Aruk's patrol watched from distance and reported every wagon toward Vael.

On Day Thirty-One, Rootglass sent no answer.

Sevrin's corridor courier returned with Irel's seal unbroken on the warning packet. Orchard guards had accepted it and refused discussion. The queen-hive remained beyond reach. Kharza could not count Rootglass among allies, yet she kept the northern road open behind the column and assigned two cistern crews to protect corridor wells.

“You are spending people on an undecided orchard,” her imperial captain said.

“I am preserving their ability to decide later.”

“Osra will use the same road if they choose her.”

“Yes.”

He waited for a cleaner explanation. None existed.

The army climbed Vael Ridge after sunset. People who had marched eight days wanted to run the last mile, but Kharza held pace until rear wagons cleared the slope. The city appeared gradually: first the dark line of the outer wall, then towers against the river haze, then the camps surrounding it.

Thousands of siege fires burned around Vael Tern.

They formed a red ring from northern aqueduct to southern vineyards. Stone throwers stood against the western sky. Aruk's barge lamps marked the southeast bank. Inside the walls, windows had been blacked out to save oil. Only the citadel beacon remained, small and blue above the Crown Hinge.

The coalition halted beyond artillery range. No cheer rose. The distance they had crossed ended in an army larger than theirs, a city eating its stores, and an ally in Rootglass who had not chosen them.

Rear wagons continued arriving for another hour. Each axle cresting the ridge changed tomorrow's options: grain, bandages, bridge cable, refugee witnesses, the saltwood cases holding old law. Kharza waited until the final slow medical supply cart entered the marked camp before allowing cook fires. An army reached a battlefield only when its slowest necessary burden arrived, and every injured and exhausted hand beside it.

Kharza listened through her wrapped tusk. Beneath the ridge, stone carried two pressures: siege works above and the buried sky below.

She summoned the route council rather than her captains alone.

“Tomorrow,” she said, “we find which road still reaches the city.”

Chapter 16 — The Quartermaster's War

Pell began the battle by announcing that they were short eleven thousand meals.

She posted the ration ledger on a wagon board where every company and refugee delegate could read it. Grain on hand, animals fit to haul, bridge cable, lamp oil, medical alcohol, and days remaining stood in black columns. Beneath them she wrote the deficit required to feed Vael's nearest wards for three days.

A Kiln captain called the figure enemy intelligence.

“Osra knows we eat,” Pell said. “What she does not know is which carts matter. Secrecy goes there.”

From Vael Ridge she could see cavalry dust along every open approach. Osra's riders did not need to defeat seventeen thousand fighters. They needed to burn flour, kill oxen, and force refugees onto soldiers' shares. Pell's objective required getting supplies through the ring without presenting one precious line to attack.

She divided the train into visible lies and hidden truths.

Forty tall wagons received empty barrels, wet hides, and enough stone to leave proper wheel ruts. Soldiers guarded them ostentatiously along the north road. Real grain traveled in fish carts, medical sacks, dismantled barge floats, and sealed night-soil bins no cavalry scout wanted to inspect closely. Nim Vask assigned voluntary-house teams across companies so no ancestry monopolized a route or bore every risk.

Three bridges crossed tributaries outside the siege ring. Pell measured each span herself. Her right hand lacked two fingers, so she tied the plumb line around her wrist and read tension through the scarred palm. The western bridge could carry loaded carts if they crossed singly. The mill bridge could carry empty decoys at speed. The eastern timber span would fail under cavalry if two counterweight pins were withdrawn after the convoy passed.

“Will it fail into houses?” Nim asked.

“Downstream mill yard. Empty since the ring closed.”

“Animals?”

“Two stray goats. We relocate them.”

Nim put the goats in the route plan. That was why Pell trusted them.

On Day Thirty-Two, cavalry took the decoy.

The false convoy rolled toward the mill bridge under coalition banners. Osra's riders struck from two orchards, arrows burning. Guards abandoned three wagons according to plan and defended the rest badly enough to look desperate. Cavalry captured barrels full of stone while real fish carts crossed west under rain cloth.

Then a decoy driver panicked.

He turned his team off the road and blocked the mill bridge. Riders closed around him. Pell could preserve the deception by leaving him or reveal that the wagons meant nothing.

She signaled the guards back.

Coalition archers returned, pulled the driver from beneath a fallen mule, and surrendered six more decoys to make space. Osra's cavalry saw the light barrels bounce. The lie ended early.

“Now they know,” a captain said.

“They know these are false. They do not know where the grain went.” Pell marked one driver wounded, one mule dead, six wagons lost. “A plan that requires a frightened teamster to die quietly deserves exposure.”

The real convoy reached the west bridge before noon. Pell stationed public counters at both ends. Every sack crossing went onto the board. Refugees saw that soldiers did not receive hidden priority. Soldiers saw the food promised to Vael and understood why their ration shrank.

Cavalry scouts arrived as the last carts crossed. They attacked the bridge weights with long arrows. Pell's tenders lowered water barrels beneath the deck, changing its sway. Mahr shields protected the cable team. Salt Aeli archers used the rain angle rather than competing at range. The convoy escaped with two carts damaged.

Pell withdrew the eastern bridge pins after a smaller medical train crossed. Osra's cavalry reached it at dusk. The lead riders recognized the trap and stopped. Engineers dismounted, replaced one pin, and began a careful crossing instead of charging.

Competent enemies spoiled elegant plans.

Pell revised the campaign that night. Instead of one large deception, she issued six smaller truths that could be mistaken for lies. A pottery train carried actual flour inside clay slip barrels. An empty medical cart traveled with genuine surgeons changing camps. Refugee fishing skiffs carried bridge pins but no food. Scouts who captured any one route would learn something accurate and nothing complete.

She also changed departure times by labor rather than clock. Convoys moved when bread ovens cooled, when river laundresses changed shifts, or when camp goats returned from forage. Osra's observers could count bells. They could not predict a thousand ordinary decisions without entering the camp.

The next cavalry probe struck the pottery train. Drivers overturned the first cart at a narrow lane and smashed barrels into white mud. Riders assumed the cargo lost. Beneath the slip, waxed flour sacks survived. After cavalry withdrew, refugees scraped clay away and recovered all but nineteen meals.

Another patrol captured the surgeons' empty cart. The surgeons treated two injured attackers before being exchanged that evening. Their medical rolls carried no routes. Osra gained proof that coalition hospitals moved; Pell gained the names of cavalry units operating west of the river.

At the northern culvert, a child noticed royal scouts counting fish skiffs. Nim paid her with lamp oil and asked what else she had seen. She identified a rider who returned at the same hour each dusk. Coalition scouts followed him to a relay stable, then left it untouched. For three nights they fed false convoy timings into his line while the real loads traveled during laundry shifts.

War at this scale rewarded no single clever cart. It rewarded correction. Every failed lie taught the next route. Every person watching from a cook line or washing bank could change the map if officers treated them as observers rather than scenery.

Pell posted an additional column naming information sources. The child chose to sign only as South Bank Washer, and that choice remained hers. Public accounting did not require public vulnerability.

Nim proposed burning the span. Pell refused. Refugees still came from farms inside the ring. They loosened the repaired pin, left the bridge unstable but usable on foot, and moved south.

At camp, the public board drew a crowd. Some accused Pell of spending soldiers to save a decoy driver. Others accused her of risking grain on a bridge rather than feeding the coalition. She answered with numbers until one refugee woman asked who had authorized Pell to choose Vael over camps already hungry.

Pell could have cited Maela's writ and the route council. Instead she looked at the ration ledger and saw another board from twelve years ago.

“I have falsified one of these before,” she said.

Nim turned slowly.

Pell told them. During Maela's western campaign, a siege company had run out of food. Pell moved nine hundred meals from a refugee allocation and changed the receiving mark to storm loss. Maela's soldiers held their wall. The refugee camp reduced rations without knowing why. Nine people died during the following fever week.

“You never told me,” Nim said.

“I told myself the wall saved more.”

“Did it?”

“Yes.”

“That answer did not need secrecy.”

Pell felt every eye on the missing fingers of her right hand, the old injury people used as proof she had paid for expertise. Those fingers had nothing to do with the ledger. Pain did not settle unrelated debts.

“I hid the transfer because I wanted Maela to trust my solutions,” she said. “And because I did not want the camp to refuse.”

Nim took the chalk from her. “Then no private correction now.”

“We are in a convoy war.”

“Publish totals, destinations, losses, and decision names. Hide routes only until arrival. If a cart disappears, its households learn before officers rewrite it as weather.”

That rule gave Osra information after the fact. It also denied Pell the power to turn failure into a cleaner column.

The crowd adopted it by witness marks. Pell signed first.

Adoption did not end the hearing. A former resident of the camp where nine died asked Pell to name them. She knew only four names. That failure struck harder than accusation. She had recorded the transferred meals to the last sack and let mortality remain a total.

The woman supplied the missing five. Nim wrote each name beneath the confession. One had died of fever before the reduction could affect her. Two had surrendered portions to children. A camp healer believed scarcity weakened the remaining six, but no ledger could assign a clean fraction of cause.

“Then perhaps you did not kill nine,” a soldier said.

Pell faced him. “That uncertainty does not refund the choice. It tells us not to turn people into a number convenient for either condemnation or pardon.”

A Vael refugee wanted her removed. A Vennik hauler argued that nobody else understood the routes. Nim rejected both shortcuts. Expertise could remain in service without becoming immunity.

They set three constraints. Pell retained operational control of supply movement. Two counters outside her house reviewed every allocation within one day. Refugee delegates could halt a transfer from civilian stores until a public hearing, unless immediate attack prevented one; any emergency override carried Pell's name and expired after six hours.

The constraints slowed orders. They also gave others a way to stop her before deaths required confession.

Kharza attended from the edge and did not rescue Pell with endorsement. After the crowd dispersed, she asked, “Can you still move food?”

“Yes.”

“Can you move it under those limits?”

“I will find out.”

“That is more useful than confidence.”

Maela's next courier received the full confession. Pell included no request for forgiveness and no claim that their former intimacy had caused the decision. She had wanted Maela's trust. She had also wanted to be indispensable. Both motives belonged to her.

At midnight, the ration board showed another consequence. Companies that had demanded secrecy began submitting their own hidden stores before counters discovered them. A Kiln captain disclosed four reserve sacks. Heartland deserters reported cavalry oats fit for porridge. Refugees offered dried roots in exchange for guaranteed return of the cooking kettles. Visibility created inventory where command searches would have created concealment.

Day Thirty-Three tested the promise.

A southern convoy carried grain, fever bark, lamp oil, and two cistern pumps toward a river culvert marked on Maela's oldest maintenance maps. Pell sent it through willow beds under fog. Osra's cavalry did not find it. Reed patrol boats did.

Aruk's soldiers blocked the channel with linked barges. Their speaker offered passage for medical goods but claimed grain under siege authority. Pell's escort refused. Neither side fired while families hid among the willows.

Pell went forward in a skiff with the public ledger held over her head.

“The grain feeds Vael,” she said.

“The city defies our treaty ally.”

“The people eating it did not sign Maela's refusal.”

“The marsh people losing seed did not order your hinge movement.”

Pell showed the Reedwater debt lines, including unfunded obligations. “We published what we owe.”

“Paper does not freshen channels.”

“No. Eight water carts already turned east do.”

The speaker had received reports of only six. Pell produced route seals for eight. He allowed one grain barge and all medical goods through, took two grain barges into Reed custody, and released the civilian skiffs.

It counted as a bargain until Heartland cavalry arrived on the southern bank.

They attacked both groups. Reed archers answered. Coalition escorts cut tow lines. In the confusion, one grain barge grounded beneath burning arrows. Pell ordered the crew off and watched three days of flour become a low white fire. Another captured barge escaped east with Reed boats. The final grain barge and medical skiffs entered the lower river.

The lost convoy had not been one event. One barge burned, one changed custody under negotiation, one arrived. Pell wrote each result separately and sent copies to every camp before sleep.

Critics used the report immediately. Kiln soldiers demanded she classify the Reed seizure as theft. Marsh refugees called it partial repayment. Heartland deserters asked why medicine had received priority over grain. Pell answered in public until her voice failed.

Transparency did not create agreement. It prevented her from choosing which disagreement existed.

On Day Thirty-Four, Nim found the submerged route.

The old map showed a waste culvert outside Vael's southern wall. River shift had buried its mouth beneath ten feet of water. Nim's barge crews sounded the bank and found dressed stone behind silt. The passage sloped toward an abandoned cistern inside the city.

Too narrow for wagons. Wide enough for people, sealed sacks, tablet cases, and pump parts.

The route still needed proof. Pell sent no swimmer until teams mapped every hazard from the outer mouth to the first air pocket. River workers lowered a weighted rope along the drowned masonry. Twenty-six feet inward, the roof dropped. Forty-two feet farther, a side current pulled toward an iron grate. Beyond that lay an air bell large enough for three people, then a second submerged run.

Nim selected swimmers by current experience, not military rank. Two came from Reed fishing families now opposed to Aruk's alliance. One was a Salt Aeli wreck diver. A human sewer mason knew Vael's stone but admitted he panicked underwater. He served on the rope team instead.

They practiced in an open inlet with sacks equal to tablet-case weight. Pell joined once and struck the roof because darkness made her misjudge depth. Her missing fingers could not close securely around the guide rope, so Nim rigged a wrist loop she could release by twisting. Accommodation entered the route plan beside water timing.

The first swimmer reached the air bell and found royal scratch marks under the mineral film. Vael maintenance crews had sealed the passage after a fever outbreak, not structural collapse. The iron grate ahead could open from inside. Jorren would need to find its wheel.

During the second test, the side current caught a flour sack and pinned it against the grate. The escort could not free it without exhausting their air. They cut the tether and surfaced. One hundred meals stayed underwater until low flow, when hook teams recovered eighty-two. Pell recorded eighteen spoiled in a test before anyone called the loss training expense.

“Why publish failed practice?” a route captain asked.

“Because future swimmers should know which current tries to kill them.”

Nim marked three rope knots before the pull, added a breakaway tether, and moved cargo beneath rather than beside each swimmer. The next sack crossed cleanly.

A hostile scout nearly found them at dusk. Refugee fishers continued casting nets over the work site while Vennik teams submerged tools under reed mats. When cavalry questioned them, an old fisher complained for ten minutes about army horses muddying spawning shallows. The scouts left with two purchased carp and no route.

Concealment succeeded because ordinary work remained credible, not because civilians vanished from the operation. Pell paid the fishers at the public rate and listed their names under restricted witness marks.

Pell tested the current with weighted corks. Water flowed inward at dusk when Vael's lower sluices opened, outward before dawn. A strike group could enter during the inward pull if Maela opened the cistern gate. The same route could expose the city if Osra discovered it.

They needed contact.

Pell built a message cylinder around a blue river lamp and sent a Vennik swimmer through the first submerged arch. The swimmer returned forty minutes later with Maela's reply scratched into wax: Cistern accessible. Jorren has the inner map. Send food before soldiers.

Pell read the line twice. Maela still knew which order would anger officers and save civilians.

They packed the first inward loads: fever bark, water filters, flour sealed in waxskin, and copies of the ration ledger. Compact tablets would follow with Pell's strike group after the route proved stable. Counterweight teams hid the remaining supplies in three river depressions rather than one depot.

Priority went through public counters. Fever bark served crowded lower wards before military infirmaries because contagion ignored rank. Water filters went to cistern mouths serving the largest households. Flour parcels carried colored cords so ward captains could report destination without opening waxskin underwater. Pump parts traveled last in the first wave; losing food to preserve equipment would repeat Pell's old habit in a new shape.

A Vael reply requested lamp oil for the citadel. Pell cut the amount in half and reassigned the rest to ward shelters. She wrote her name beside the change and sent the request back with the delivery. Maela could countermand it after seeing the cost, not before civilians knew the oil existed.

Swimmers carried no weapons beyond belt knives needed to cut lines. A fighter drowning in a narrow culvert could block every load behind them. The first soldiers would enter only after food teams established the route and Jorren secured both grates.

At sunset, the first sealed sacks vanished beneath the cistern water. Forty minutes later a blue lamp flashed twice from a drain slit inside Vael Tern.

Food had crossed the ring.

Pell posted the delivery count, the burned grain, the Reed-held barge, and her old falsification on the same public board. Then she marked the submerged cistern route in a private ledger shared with Nim, Kharza, and Maela.

Authority had not come from keeping the cleanest secret. It came from letting people see what her choices cost, then trusting them to keep following the routes that still worked.

Chapter 17 — Through the Cistern Gate

Maela turned the iron wheel while three wards waited for water.

The abandoned cistern had not served Vael Tern in twenty-eight years. Fever moss stained its walls. Silt buried the lower steps. Jorren and six engineers had cleared the inner grate by lantern light while ward captains rationed every remaining bucket above.

“Again,” Jorren said.

Maela leaned into the wheel. Rust broke. River water surged through the submerged culvert and rose to her knees. A blue lamp appeared beneath the surface, followed by a swimmer towing flour.

Food came before soldiers.

For two hours, Vael crews hauled waxskin sacks from the black water: flour, fever bark, filters, pump parts, public ration sheets. Each carried colored cords naming its destination. Lower wards received the first loads. Citadel clerks protested until Maela showed them Pell's signed reduction of palace lamp oil.

Distribution began inside the cistern so no ward could intercept another's share on the stairs. Blue cords went to river families, red to western shelters, uncolored double knots to fever wards. Two counters cut every seal together and weighed water gain before accepting flour. One sack had leaked enough to lose a quarter of its contents. The deficit entered the board before anyone carried it away.

People gathered above the rail. A mother recognized her district cord and wept without making a sound. Soldiers stared at fever bark as if it were treasure because sickness had removed more watch hours than arrows. Pump parts drew no cheers, though they would make the remaining water usable. Maela watched attention gather around immediate hunger and reminded herself that unseen maintenance still required protection.

A merchant factor attempted to purchase six flour sacks for later distribution. Ward captains expelled him from the cistern. Under old law his storehouse contracts carried priority. Under siege rules, public destination cords outranked private debt. Maela signed the seizure and required the factor's name on the board beside hers.

The first outgoing group assembled before the swimmers finished. Twelve fever patients rejected by Osra's daily exchange, four pregnant women, seven children with lung sickness, and six carers waited under blankets. Jorren objected that the return flow had not been tested with frightened civilians.

“Then test it with volunteers before dawn,” Maela said. “Nobody enters submerged water tonight.”

They moved the patients into the emptied cistern keeper's rooms instead. Supplies created shelter by vacating storage space. Every arrival altered more than a ration total.

“Honor the allocation,” she said.

The seventh swimmer brought Pell.

The Vennik quartermaster emerged gasping, wrist loop still tied to the guide rope, black hair pasted over her large eyes. Two missing fingers on her right hand had not kept her from crossing; they had changed the harness. Maela noticed that before the months between them.

“You took the water route,” Maela said.

“You left the road occupied.”

Their embrace lasted one breath and belonged to survival, not settlement.

Pell's strike group followed: twenty swimmers, thirty fighters, eight counterweight workers, Caldrin, and two tablet cases wrapped in inflated skins. The Compact tablets crossed last among essential loads, after medicine and before weapons. Witnesses checked all four seals inside the cistern.

Maela touched neither case. “Open them in the lower council hall.”

“Publicly,” Caldrin said.

“As publicly as siege permits.”

“That phrase has hidden much.”

“Then count the people I exclude.”

They did. One royal guard for security. No merchant delegates until ward captains had seats. No crown clerk without a Vennik counter beside them. Caldrin accepted the conditions.

Jorren closed the grate after the final swimmer. “The route gives us a bridgehead outside the ring.”

“And gives Osra a road in if she finds it,” Maela said.

He spread a wet map across an empty water bench. The cistern gate connected to three flood tunnels. One rose beneath the river ward. One reached a lower Crown Hinge passage sealed by royal order. The third ended near the south wall foundations. Supplies could enter. Civilians could leave in small numbers. Enemy sappers could reverse the same path.

Pell studied flow marks. “We install two breakaway grates, not one lock. Inner grate stays under Vael control. Outer obstruction stays under my river teams. Either side can close without trapping swimmers between.”

“You are creating separate authority over one route,” Maela said.

“Yes. Practice accepting it.”

Old affection made the irritation precise.

In the lower council hall, Caldrin placed the Compact tablets on bread tables because every ceremonial table had become a ration station. Clay showed four scripts and royal erasures. Ward captains leaned close. Mahr residents read hearth marks. Urban Aeli corrected one translation. Vennik pulley workers recognized old weight knots. The discovery entered the city through ordinary expertise rather than proclamation.

Caldrin read the four equal-practice clause in every surviving script. Each version named labor differently. Mahr marks emphasized hearth plurality. Aeli branches described living transfer rather than ownership of bees. Vennik knots tied authority to measured counterload. River Ternic required witnesses from places receiving benefit and places carrying harm.

A noble asked which version ruled when meanings differed.

“None alone,” Caldrin said. “Difference requires another hearing.”

The answer angered people trained to seek one seal. It relieved those accustomed to being mistranslated.

Maela ordered her six years of denied hinge petitions brought from the citadel. Clerks stacked them beside the original tablets. Mahr miners had requested pressure readings after foundation cracks. Urban Aeli keepers had asked to compare failing hives. Vennik bridge houses had offered to weigh Crown chains. Her own refusals cited public order and royal custody.

A miner named Hadr Olt held up a petition carrying Maela's signature. “My sister died when the east cellar folded.”

“I denied your inspection.”

“You denied our standing. The inspection happened after she died.”

No siege necessity could absorb that answer. Maela entered the death and refusal into the public record. She offered Hadr a place among the lower-court witnesses. He accepted without forgiving her.

The first cost map circulated through the hall. Ward delegates added current siege burdens: water shortage, fever concentration, ration cuts, exposed foundations. Pell insisted that military urgency enter beside climatic damage so delayed action could not masquerade as neutral. Caldrin accepted modern entries on fresh clay rather than carving them into the originals.

Copies began at once. Children from the scriptorium pressed damp sheets over tablet marks. Potters prepared cheap clay versions for every ward. Scribes translated into trade language, then read translations back to native readers. Exact authority remained disputed, but concealment no longer depended on possession of nine heavy tablets.

Maela assigned mixed guard to the originals: one ward officer, one Vennik counterweight worker, one Mahr resident elected by hearth delegates, and one Aeli hive keeper. Her royal guard held no sole key.

“Your seal can still open the lower gate,” Hadr said.

“Yes.”

“For how long?”

Maela touched the metal beneath her cuirass. “Until I can alter access without surrendering the city to whoever reaches it first.”

He disliked the answer. She recorded that too.

Pell stood beside Maela without touching her. “Before we discuss the tablets, I sent you another account.”

“The old ration ledger.”

“You read it?”

“I read nine names and four you did not know.”

Pell flinched. “You know now.”

“I know I benefited.” Maela remembered that western wall and the soldiers who had eaten. “You changed the ledger. I never asked which camp lost food because success arrived.”

“That does not split my choice in half.”

“No. It adds mine beside it.”

The council waited. Their former intimacy could not become a private room while public harm sat on the table.

Maela told them she had rewarded Pell after the campaign without auditing the storm-loss entry. Pell described the new review constraints. Ward captains demanded the same rules inside Vael. Merchant councillors objected that open deficits would cause panic.

“Hunger already knows itself,” Pell said. “Only officials require paper to be surprised.”

They adopted public daily store figures and named emergency overrides. Maela surrendered her right to requisition ward food without two counters. The decision reduced speed. It also stopped the citadel from eating first by habit.

At dusk, Jorren led Maela through the flood tunnels. He marked niches where children could rest during evacuation, ladders needing repair, and turns too narrow for litters. The submerged section allowed strong swimmers only. To move civilians, engineers would need to lower the cistern level and build air chambers.

“How many per tide?” Maela asked.

“Two hundred if uninjured. Eighty with children and litters. More invites drowning.”

“Start with sick exchange overflow.”

“We need this route for supplies.”

“We need people alive to use supplies.”

He revised the schedule: incoming loads at dusk, outgoing civilians before dawn. Every person leaving reduced mouths and defenders' families, but also risked exposure. Scouts would spread false culvert marks along two other banks.

At the south branch, Jorren found fresh grit below the wall. Someone outside had sounded the stone.

“Osra?” Maela asked.

“Could be old settlement. Could be engineers.”

“Treat it as discovery.”

They installed a listening post and prepared clay plugs that could flood the branch without drowning the main route. The cistern gate became relief and vulnerability in the same hour.

Before returning above, Maela joined the evacuation test. Jorren selected eight healthy volunteers whose bodies reflected different problems: a strong swimmer, two elders, a parent with a child-sized bundle, a soldier with a splinted arm, a Vennik worker shorter than the air-bell handholds, and Maela in armor without weapons.

They lowered the cistern level by opening the river sluice. The first submerged run shortened, but the current strengthened at the grate. Jorren sent them in pairs along the guide rope. Maela entered behind the elder named Sella Crow.

Dark water closed over her. Stone grazed her cuirass. Sella's feet stopped ahead when panic locked her hands around the rope. Maela could not speak underwater or push without risking both of them. She pressed the elder's ankle twice, the signal for air ahead. Sella moved.

They surfaced in the bell. The Vennik volunteer could breathe comfortably, while Maela had to crouch because the roof sloped. The child bundle floated too high and blocked one adult from drawing breath. Jorren marked a lower sling position. The splinted soldier could not manage the grate turn with one arm. A rope lever solved it after two attempts.

The second run proved worse. Silt reduced visibility to nothing. The elder arrived exhausted. Maela's wet armor doubled effort and scraped the narrow turn.

“No armored guards in civilian groups,” Jorren said when they surfaced outside. “One swimmer per five evacuees. Rest in the bell. Litters cannot pass until we widen the inner throat.”

Sella coughed river water and corrected him. “One per three elders. Your count assumes fear improves after the first drowning place.”

Jorren changed it.

The test reduced projected flow to eighty only under trained escorts, fewer with frail evacuees. Maela ordered no public promise above sixty until the route completed a full civilian tide. The lower figure disappointed ward captains. It might keep them from sending more people than air pockets could hold.

Engineers began widening the inner throat by hand, using cloth to catch every stone chip before it entered the current. Each inch required an hour. Siege logistics offered no miraculous hidden road, only another place where labor bought capacity slowly.

When Maela returned above, bells summoned her to the throne hall.

The council had arranged Ordan's plain iron circlet on a ration cloth. Four nobles, two guild heads, and a frightened priest waited beside it. Outside, stone throwers struck the western wall at measured intervals.

“Vael requires a crowned sovereign,” Lord Pellan said. “The troops need lawful continuity.”

“I am Crown Marshal. They have command.”

“Command does not answer Osra's claim.”

“The city has eaten boiled leather in two wards. You propose spending oil on a coronation.”

“A brief rite.”

“No rite remains brief after witnesses, bells, guards, vestments, and bread.”

The priest lowered his eyes. “Tradition requires distribution to the poor.”

“Then distribute the food and omit me.”

Pellan's face reddened. “Without a crowned heir, the Compact tablets may be used to abolish all royal authority.”

Maela looked at the royal bearing seal beneath her cuirass. It still opened rooms no ward captain could enter. She had not yet decided what to do with it. She knew what not to spend tonight.

“Good law should survive people reading its original,” she said. “If my authority depends on clay staying hidden, it deserves a siege.”

The councillors pressed. A coronation might rally defenders. It might split Osra's Heartland troops. It might make foreign captains recognize orders. Each benefit carried a cost the speakers treated as ceremonial dust.

Ward representatives arrived before Maela answered. News of the circlet had spread faster than flour. Hadr Olt came from the tablet hall, still carrying clay on his hands. Sella Crow arrived wrapped in a cistern blanket after the evacuation test. A baker from the western wall brought the day's fuel count.

Lord Pellan protested that succession belonged to the royal council.

“Then why ask our children to defend it?” Sella said.

The baker placed two cold oven stones on the cloth beside the circlet. “We extinguished forty ovens. If bells ring for a crown, people will think bread follows. It does not.”

A palace guard argued that defenders needed a face above faction. Hadr answered that he would defend Maela's command because she had opened the tablets, not because Ordan's metal touched her head. An urban Aeli captain said some soldiers might indeed rally to a crowned heir, while others would see confirmation that every promise of shared custody ended at the same throne.

Maela made the council list effects as Pell listed stores. Possible Heartland defections. Possible Mahr withdrawals. Guard hours. Public bread expectations. Legal claim against Osra. Delay to cistern works. No column produced a simple total.

The priest confessed that an abbreviated rite could omit feast and vestments but not the royal vow of sole hinge guardianship. The old words required the sovereign to keep Crown Hinge from all unlicensed hands.

“Can you change the vow?” Maela asked.

“Not without a crowned sovereign's authority.”

“Then the rite requires me to promise what the Compact tablets dispute.”

Pellan called the contradiction temporary. Caldrin, arriving at the hall door, called it the purpose.

A stone struck the wall. Dust crossed Ordan's circlet. Maela saw who had been left outside the supposed necessity: people whose fuel, dead, petitions, and labor would purchase her symbol. Refusal would not settle monarchy. It would prevent hunger from settling it by exhaustion.

Pell entered carrying the ration board. “Circlet banquet baseline, even reduced: eight hundred loaves, twelve oil jars, forty guard shifts, six hours of hall labor.”

“Those figures assume a full court,” Pellan said.

“They assume your written requirements.”

“We can waive them.”

“Then you admit the crown exists without them.”

Maela almost smiled. Former lovers remembered where each other kept knives.

She lifted the circlet from the cloth. For a moment the hall inhaled. Then she placed it in an empty flour chest and closed the lid.

“No coronation while the ring holds,” she said. “No food, oil, or guard hour spent converting defense into inheritance. Address me as Crown Marshal.”

A western impact shook dust from the rafters. Nobody mistook the changed condition.

Below, Caldrin began reading the tablets to ward delegates. Pell's swimmers prepared the second tide. Jorren posted guards at both grates and gave each authority to flood its own side if captured.

Maela revised the defense plan around the new route. The cistern would supply lower wards, evacuate those unable to survive the siege, and carry a small strike group between city and coalition. No mass sortie would expose it. Two false tunnels would receive visible guards. Engineers would prepare the real branch for collapse if Osra entered.

Near midnight, Pell joined her beside the cistern water.

“You put the circlet in a flour chest,” Pell said.

“It was empty.”

“That part troubles me more.”

Maela wanted to ask whether they had changed enough to begin again. The question would turn siege survival into leverage. She let it wait.

“I am sorry I benefited from the lie,” she said.

“I am sorry I made you useful to my excuse.”

Neither apology restored the dead. Both altered what they could conceal next.

A guide rope tightened in the water. Another food load entered. On the outer bank, Pell's people held one end. Inside Vael Tern, Maela's crews held the other. Between them the submerged passage remained open, carrying relief through a gate that either side might have to destroy.

By dawn the coalition possessed an internal bridgehead, the Compact tablets rested under mixed guard inside the city, and every defender knew that their newest road could also become the breach.

The first civilian tide carried fifty-eight people outward without a drowning. Jorren reduced the next group after one elder reached the air bell in distress. Pell published the lower capacity beside the delivered food. Relief had opened a narrow, dangerous, measurable passage, not an escape large enough for the hungry, frightened, crowded city. No map or promise made it wider. Maela ordered its limits repeated at every ward board, granary door, infirmary, and guard post before hope could turn them into another dangerous public lie.

Chapter 18 — The General of Three Orc Armies

Three Mahr armies arrived at Kharza's command fire and each refused the place she offered.

Kiln House captains stood closest, black-clay banner behind them. Seven hundred imperial Mahr deserters kept their furled Tern standard to the west. A Reed splinter force of nine companies held the riverward edge under silver-capped speaker Vek Rann. They had left Aruk after Osra's canal release arrived late, but they had not joined Kharza.

Kharza set three equal stones around the map.

“No,” Vek said. “A place at your fire implies your command.”

The imperial captain objected to sitting beside soldiers who had attacked royal convoys. Kiln elders demanded Kharza absorb both forces under emergency field law.

Eleven years earlier, she would have ended the argument by assigning ranks. That method had won the Three Valleys and taught the crown how to use one command against every region.

“Keep your own fires,” Kharza said. “We meet here for shared objectives only.”

She moved the stones apart.

The immediate objective concerned Vael's relief bridge. Osra held both approaches. Maela could sortie from inside if an outer force reached the river. Kharza's coalition needed Mahr stone readers along the bridge foundations, infantry against siege earthworks, and Reed boats to keep evacuation water open.

Each army wanted a different condition. Kiln captains demanded unified timing. Imperial deserters demanded protection from execution if captured. Vek demanded no command that worsened Reedwater without marsh review.

Kharza wrote all three before offering terms.

Separate banners. Separate internal officers. Shared signals for retreat, civilian passage, and hinge danger. No commander could order another army to burn boats, close evacuation roads, or continue over a reported stone failure. Every force received refusal authority when an order violated its written conditions.

A Kiln elder struck the table. “An army cannot fight if every captain debates.”

“Refusal requires a named condition and a public reason,” Kharza said. “Not preference.”

“And if refusal loses the bridge?”

“The refusing commander answers for that cost. So does the commander who gave the order.”

Vek studied her. “You would permit us to leave?”

“I cannot build equal alliance by denying the door.”

The imperial captain asked who held final command during attack.

“I coordinate. I do not own your people.”

They argued until noon. Refusal signals needed to differ from retreat. Reed horns carried poorly over stone; Kiln drums carried badly across water. Vennik signalers designed paired flags and bells. Every order received an objective, a boundary, and an ending condition.

Before any captain signed, each army held its own council.

The Kiln Houses gathered by hearth delegation. Soldiers placed fired tokens in bowls for commanders they accepted; cracked tokens registered dissent without revealing individual hands. Kharza's oldest captains expected automatic support and received two full bowls of objection. Families from terraces damaged under her former central command demanded that supply officers, not only fighters, hold refusal marks.

Imperial Mahr met by company guilds. Many had lived in human-majority cities for generations and rejected Kiln ancestral rites as irrelevant to their service. They chose officers by written slate and required amnesty terms in court Ternic so Vael officials could not later claim mistranslation. One company remained loyal to the dead king's law while opposing Osra. Another wanted the monarchy abolished. Shared defection had not produced shared politics.

Vek's Reed splinter force convened on moored skiffs. War speakers rotated by channel, and silver-capped elders could recall them after each objective. Three boats voted to return to Aruk. Vek allowed them to leave with weapons and food. Two did. The third stayed after its crew learned Osra's freshwater release had bypassed their nursery island.

Kharza attended each council only when invited. At the Kiln fire she answered for the Three Valleys. At the imperial slate she promised captives would receive ordinary law, not revenge. On the Reed skiffs she listened while families described salt-soft seed. Nobody asked her to summarize Mahr interests because the councils had demonstrated that no such single list existed.

The signed command agreement carried three forms of consent. Fired tokens, company slates, and knotted reed cords hung from the same board without becoming interchangeable. Caldrin copied their meanings for non-Mahr witnesses.

Vennik signalers then ran a field test. A black flag meant objective complete to Kiln troops, but mourning to imperial companies. Reed boats read a lowered silver pennant as safe channel, while land soldiers mistook it for surrender. They replaced colors with shapes: square for hold, split-tail for withdraw, ring for civilian passage. Bells carried the same counts at night.

During the final test, a drummer deliberately gave a false advance. Two captains obeyed from habit. The exercise stopped until both could explain why the written boundary mattered more than familiar command tone. Refusal required practice before fear.

Lessa arrived to change Kharza's tusk wrap. The medic listened while captains proposed making her chief surgeon over all Mahr companies.

“No,” Lessa said.

Kharza held still as copper came away from the crack. “You have field trust.”

“I have your name.”

“You also have skill.”

“Then let skill keep me treating patients. Chief surgeon spends half a day resolving tent claims and becomes proof you promoted kin.”

She nominated an imperial Mahr physician and a Reed bone-setter as joint coordinators, with herself leading mobile triage. Kharza accepted before pride turned praise into pressure.

“Your wrap is too tight,” Lessa said.

“It was holding.”

“It was hiding swelling.”

Kharza let her loosen it. The lesson required no military translation.

Aruk requested truce at sunset.

He came to the river island with twelve guards and both silver tusk caps in place. Kharza brought twelve from mixed companies and no banner. Between them, Vael's walls rose over smoke. Osra's siege camps filled the banks.

Aruk set a jar of Reedwater brine on the stone. “Your hinge work did this.”

“Yes.”

“Your warnings arrived after.”

“Yes.”

He had expected defense. Its absence did not soften him. “Six councils lost seed beds. Osra opened freshwater gates after bargaining. You sent carts and called debt public.”

“We sent eight.”

“Eight carts do not replace a season.”

“No.”

Aruk looked toward Vek's splinter camp. “You are recruiting my army.”

“They came under their own speakers.”

“You accepted them.”

“As separate authority.”

“You once hanged officers for less.”

“I once taught officers that obedience could erase their people.”

Aruk touched one silver cap. “Do not turn regret into a claim over my refusal. I remain with Osra because she signed canal authority the crown denied us for generations.”

“She also concealed the surge range.”

“And you moved Ember without our witness.”

Both charges held.

Aruk opened a reed case and removed copies of his treaty with Osra. Each council recognition bore its own seal. Canal gates would transfer to a marsh authority after the war. Salt fishing rights extended for twelve years. Heartland courts had accepted Reed inheritance law in three disputed settlements. These were not slogans Kharza could dismiss as bribery. They corrected injuries the old crown, including Kharza's campaigns, had enforced.

“What have you offered them?” Aruk asked.

Kharza could name future witness seats and public costs. She could not transfer gates she did not hold. “No sovereignty that depends on my victory.”

“Then Osra's paper carries more weight.”

“Until she decides emergency requires the gates again.”

“Every treaty risks breach. Yours risks never becoming material.”

A Reed guard set down two seed bundles. One came from a canal reached by Osra's delayed freshwater. Its center remained firm. The other came from beyond the release. Gray rot filled it. Aruk placed both beside his treaty.

“I serve the councils that received this,” he said, touching the sound seed, “and the councils owed this.” His finger moved to rot. “Leaving Osra now does not open another gate tonight.”

Kharza understood why his army stayed. Understanding did not make the siege lawful.

“Vael's wards are also hungry tonight.”

“Yes. Maela can surrender Crown access.”

“To a regent who falsified the grain risk.”

“To a regent who signed terms your coalition cannot yet fund.”

Their conflict did not reduce to truth against ignorance. It concerned which enforceable harm each accepted while preventing another.

Kharza added a proposal: independent Reed counters at any future bearing, canal releases placed under councils before further hinge motion, no demand that Aruk acknowledge her command. In exchange she asked him to open a civilian lane if Vael's wall failed.

“I already negotiated sick passage with Tavan,” he said.

“Fifty per day does not answer a breach.”

“Nor does your cistern answer a city.”

“Then we need both.”

Aruk would not promise. He agreed to let Reed counters survey two neutral river islands for emergency landing. The concession created capacity without changing allegiance.

A distant stone thrower discharged. They watched the missile strike Vael's western wall. Dust rose around homes neither controlled.

“My soldiers say your name as if you betrayed the Mahr,” Aruk said.

“Mine say yours the same way.”

“Correct them.”

“I have.”

“So have I.”

For once they shared an order.

Kharza showed him the written limits. Aruk read every line. He paused at civilian boats and stone-failure refusal.

“Osra agreed to no civilian burning,” he said.

“Then your terms match ours there.”

“Our terms do not make us allies.”

“No.”

“Good.”

They discussed the bridge. Aruk would defend Osra's southeast bank. Kharza would attempt relief. Neither would target medical barges. Both would mark prisoner channels. If stone-song revealed bridge collapse beneath civilians, either could signal a halt without surrendering position.

“You expect me to honor your bell?” Aruk asked.

“I expect you to hear your own bridge.”

His caps damped that hearing by cultural choice. Kharza did not ask him to remove them.

Aruk rolled the terms and returned them unsigned. “I will meet you at the bridge.”

“As enemy?”

“As the war decides.”

He left with Osra still his ally and no place beneath Kharza's command.

The next two days tested the federation before battle.

A Kiln patrol seized two imperial deserters as suspected spies. Their own captain invoked refusal authority and halted joint scouting. Kharza convened a field hearing. Evidence showed the soldiers had visited relatives in Osra's camp without permission but carried no maps. They lost scout duty, not freedom. Joint patrols resumed with mixed pairs chosen by each army.

Vek's force refused a trench line that would drain runoff into a refugee hollow. Engineers moved it uphill at the cost of two hours and harder soil. During the delay, cavalry attacked. Imperial troops held the incomplete trench while Reed soldiers evacuated the hollow. The altered line cost six wounded and saved sixty families from flood and horse charge.

A Kiln captain then tried refusal for extra rations. The hearing rejected it because hunger applied across companies and violated no written condition. Refusal did not become a polite form of demand.

Every decision entered a command ledger beside its consequence. Kharza read it nightly. Distributed authority produced more words, more delay, and fewer hidden resentments. It did not produce harmony.

They rehearsed the bridge attack on a field marked with ropes. Pell scaled the river, piers, siege banks, and cistern outlet in paces. Soldiers carried weighted sacks to represent wounded people. Refugee volunteers moved through the model at walking speed while formations tried to protect them.

The first plan failed in nine minutes. Kiln infantry reached the western earthwork and blocked the civilian lane with its reserve. The second failed when imperial deserters pursued a simulated cavalry retreat beyond their objective. The third trapped Vek's boats downstream of a lowered chain.

Kharza did not correct each failure herself. The affected commanders revised their sections while everyone watched. Kiln reserves shifted uphill. Imperial companies tied pursuit authority to a split-tail signal from their own captain. Reed boat crews assigned cutters at both chain towers and retained the right to abandon attack if civilians crowded the channel.

A fourth rehearsal introduced false signals. Vennik bell teams repeated only commands carrying the proper shape flag. A familiar Kiln drumbeat ordered advance from beyond the field; two companies started, remembered the earlier test, and stopped. An officer cursed the hesitation.

“Hesitation saved them from enemy command,” Pell said. “Spend it where authentication fails.”

The fifth rehearsal introduced a collapsed bridge span. Mahr readers marked danger with white clay. Captains had to choose between objective and withdrawal. One Kiln officer crossed anyway to maintain momentum. Kharza removed him from bridge command, not for refusing caution but for violating the written stone-failure boundary his own hearths had approved.

“Real battle will not resemble ropes,” he argued.

“Then honor the few conditions we know become worse in reality.”

Lessa tested triage by declaring injuries at random. Commanders repeatedly used medics as messengers because they stood near every line. She stopped the rehearsal.

“My workers carry patients, not your indecision.”

Dedicated runners replaced them. Joint medical coordination remained with the imperial physician and Reed bone-setter Lessa had nominated. Her refusal improved the plan because she remained free to expose its demands.

Pell added supply endings. Every formation carried water for six hours. When empty, it withdrew unless another force voluntarily transferred stores under counters. No captain could prolong battle by silently taking refugee carts.

The final rehearsal lasted through the planned bridge opening and stopped at relief, not pursuit. Kharza wanted instinct to recognize the objective's end. Victorious armies caused fresh disasters when they mistook movement for permission.

On Day Thirty-Eight, the command table fixed the Four Banners plan.

Kharza's black-clay formation would take the western bridge earthworks. Aruk's Reed silver-cap force remained opposing them on the southeast approach, but Vek's splinter boats would hold civilian water lanes without adopting his banner. Maela's blue-river sortie would open from the cistern district when Pell's signal reached the wall. Rootglass's green-glass guard had not committed; the plan preserved a northern lane if Irel came.

The name described the formations required for full relief, not alliances already secured.

Pell assigned ration limits and bridge weights. Sevrin supplied wind forecasts through Saan's readings. Lessa established triage beyond command fires. Imperial deserters received the dangerous work of dismantling cavalry stakes because they knew Osra's pattern, not as punishment for former service.

Kharza issued no speech about one people. She read each force's objective and refusal condition aloud.

Then she required each commander to repeat another force's terms. Kiln Captain Dorr recited the Reed civilian-water condition. Vek named the imperial prisoner protection. The deserter captain described the Kiln stone-failure limit. None could later claim ignorance of a refusal merely because it came from another army.

Civilian witnesses challenged the plan next. A Vael fisher pointed out that opening the bridge for relief would push siege debris downstream toward the cistern mouth. Pell added boom crews.
