An Ashwall refugee asked where families outside the walls should go when fighting began. Kharza reserved two ridge lanes and removed an imperial reserve from one, weakening the attack to prevent another trapped convoy.

A Mahr merchant serving Osra's camp crossed under truce to ask whether coalition lines would accept noncombat defectors. Kiln soldiers called him collaborator. Kharza granted passage under search and public registration. A command federation that recognized military refusal could not deny civilians the same exit from allegiance.

The merchant brought intelligence: Osra had ordered more stakes near the bridge and rotated tired Heartland infantry away from the western bank. Kharza recorded the source without making his admission purchase forgiveness for earlier supply work.

Finally, each army appointed a consequence speaker who did not transmit orders. Their task involved counting when objectives consumed the limits written beside them: water gone, wounded capacity full, civilian lane blocked, foundation song changed. Commanders disliked a voice dedicated to stopping momentum. That discomfort justified the role.

Only after those additions did the three councils press their distinct marks into the plan. Agreement attached to one battle and expired when relief opened. No emergency phrase extended it automatically.

Kiln Houses could halt if the western foundation split. Imperial companies could refuse execution orders against prisoners or defectors. Vek's boats could withdraw from any action closing civilian water. Vael's sortie remained Maela's command. Rootglass, if it came, would answer to Irel.

A young officer asked what made Kharza general if she could not compel them.

“Coordination,” she said. “Information. Consequence. If my plan fails, I do not become less responsible because you remained free.”

At dusk, Aruk's signal fire appeared across the river. One light, then two: acknowledgment of the medical-barge channel. Not alliance.

A consequence speaker counted the signal before any officer answered. Vek's boats shifted one anchor upstream, preserving the agreed channel without entering Aruk's formation. Kiln archers lowered bows along the marked water. One limited promise had changed positions on both banks while leaving every army's allegiance intact.

Kharza ordered the adjustment entered beside Aruk's name, not hers. Shared restraint deserved accurate custody as much as shared victory.

Kharza answered with the agreed bell. Her cracked tusk vibrated beneath its looser copper wrap. Three Mahr armies prepared around Vael Tern without becoming one army, and the rival across the bridge retained every right to oppose her.

She looked from separate fires to the city wall.

At the bridge Aruk would meet her under his own banner. If he ever crossed to their cause, he would cross as an equal or not at all.

Chapter 19 — The Heir Who Refused the Sky

The councilors had set a coronation stool across the map table.

Maela stopped inside the Hall of Rivers with rain drying on her cuirass and listened to the northward artillery. The interval had narrowed since dawn. Osra's stone-casters struck every fifty breaths now, probing for mortar weakened by hunger and three days of cold rain. Each impact traveled through the floor and made the silver cups tremble beside the stool.

Someone had found blue velvet for its cushion. No one had found bread for the children sleeping beneath the west arcade.

"Move it," Maela said.

Councilor Hadran, whose flour mills had supplied the city before the siege, folded both hands over his belly. Hunger had reduced him without making him smaller in his own imagination. "Your accession requires witnesses. All nine surviving councilors are present. The chaplain waits in the lower court. We may not have another hour when the bells are quiet."

"They are not quiet."

A stone struck the outer wall. Lime dust fell from the ceiling ribs. The river captains at the end of the table glanced up, calculating thickness by habit. Maela counted the delay before the answering horn. South bastion. No breach.

She crossed to the stool and lifted it. One leg caught on a red cord stretched across the campaign map. Pell had marked the city's cistern routes in red, evacuation lanes in yellow, and districts too crowded to move in black. The royal furniture dragged all three colors together.

Maela set it against the wall.

"The king named you," Hadran said. "The army already calls you heir. Osra calls you usurper because you refuse the act that answers her. Take the crown, open Crown Hinge by right, and give the defenders a lawful center."

"A lawful center cannot begin by blocking the only useful map in the room."

The council shifted with the particular impatience of people who had turned fear into procedure. Maela understood it. A crown promised a shape they recognized. Walls failed inward; dynasties continued forward. The old answer offered them a line through chaos, and all it asked was that she stand where Ordan had stood.

She unbuckled her sword belt and laid it along the table's eastern edge. "Where are the public copies?"

A young archive clerk raised an ink-stained hand. "At the Hall of Measures, Crown Marshal. Two hundred and eleven made by noon. More if we can boil glue."

"And the original Compact tablets?"

"In Quartermaster Vask's wagon by the inner quay. Under Vennik guard."

That custody mattered more than velvet. Maela looked at the nine councilors and then at the gallery doors, which remained barred from inside. "Open those."

Hadran's expression hardened. "This is a royal succession council."

"No succession has occurred. Open them."

The door wardens obeyed the office she still held. Noise entered first: boots on stair stone, an argument about ration chits, a baby crying with tireless fury. Then came the people who had waited in the passage. Two Mahr kiln workers carried a Compact copy mounted between laths so wet ink would not smear. An Aeli cooper from the river ward leaned on a cane. Three Vennik bridge crews arrived in tarred aprons. Behind them stood human laundresses, wounded defenders, an old Neris fisher, and six residents elected by the shelter circles that had formed in granaries and bathhouses.

Pell entered last, ducking beneath the displayed copy. Mud striped her coat to the elbows. Her right hand, short two fingers since long before Maela had known her, held a bundle of tally slates against her chest.

"You have a chair problem," Pell said.

"It has been relocated."

"Excellent. We have several bridge problems that resist equal treatment."

A few people laughed, quietly and without relief. Maela felt the old pull toward Pell's voice, toward nights when a dry observation had made duty seem briefly proportionate. She let the feeling pass without asking it to carry either of them.

The archive clerk read the copied clauses aloud. Public bearing required four equal labors. Every region receiving a seasonal benefit must witness the cost imposed elsewhere. No dynasty could close a bearing court to a laboring people whose bodies or lands bore its strain. The language had cuts where later royal copies removed names, yet Caldrin's tablets preserved enough to expose the shape of the theft.

When the reading ended, the Aeli cooper tapped his cane against the floor. "Six years ago my enclave petitioned to inspect the lower pollen channels. Your guards turned us away."

Maela faced him. "I signed that exclusion."

"You did."

No artillery answered for several breaths. That silence pressed harder than an accusation dressed in ceremony.

A Mahr mason named Veka lifted the lath-mounted copy. White dust filled the grooves of her black-clay neck plates. "The Crown called our fault-readers dangerous when they asked to hear the hinge. Now you need General Olt below it. Does refusing a crown change who held the door?"

"No," Maela said. "It changes whether I can pass the same door to an heir."

"Convenient timing."

"Catastrophically late timing."

Hadran pushed back from the table. "We can perform confession after the siege. Vael needs command. The outer wall has lost two stair towers. Osra's sappers have reached the north counterscarp. A divided city cannot survive a full assault."

"Then we should stop dividing it into people whose consent counts and people whose labor counts."

Maela motioned to the campaign map. The shelter delegates came nearer, uncertain whether old rules still forbade them from approaching it. One laundress, a broad human woman with burned forearms, stepped first. Others followed. Their bodies covered the painted estates and noble wards. The map improved at once. They knew which alleys remained passable, which wells had fouled, where handcarts could turn, where a fallen lintel had trapped thirty people in a cellar until Mahr masons cut through from the brewery.

Pell spread her slates beside Maela's sword. "If this room wants legitimacy, it can begin with arithmetic. Sixty thousand two hundred forty people remain inside the outer districts and the inner city. We have grain for eleven reduced days if no storehouse burns. Water lasts longer, provided Osra fails to foul the east intake. Evacuation through the cistern route can move eight hundred people an hour under ideal conditions, which have never once visited my profession."

"Four barges are repaired," a Vennik rigger said.

"Three and a half," Pell corrected. "One possesses aspirations."

The river captains bent over the yellow lines. Hadran tried twice to return to accession, but each attempt found a new obstruction: a collapsed stair, a shortage of mule shoes, an exposed queue outside the southern ovens. Defense reclaimed the room from inheritance one fact at a time.

At last Maela asked, "What must exist before any bearing is authorized?"

Pell studied her. "Cost maps. Not a blessing over estimates made in private. Named districts, named regions, water heights, crop losses, migration lanes, food reserves, and people with the standing to stop a movement at an agreed threshold. Civilian seats, because armies have a professional tendency to call a road empty after moving their own wagons off it."

Councilor Sere, the oldest of the nine, rubbed his scalp. "You propose to put refugees over Crown Hinge."

"Beside it," Pell said. "No one should be over it. That arrangement produced our present weather."

"Who chooses these seats?"

The Neris fisher answered before Maela could. "The people paying each cost. You do not appoint my coast to agree with you."

Voices rose. A river captain objected that siege elections invited Osra's agents. Veka demanded Mahr refusal authority over any load they could hear breaking stone. The Aeli cooper insisted Rootglass and Neris could not share one representative merely because both were called elf in trade speech. Shelter delegates wanted the right to publish all ration transfers. Hadran warned that merchants would hide stores rather than submit to seizure by a crowd.

Maela let the argument continue. Command had taught her to cut across uncertainty before it infected a line. Six years at Vael had rewarded speed, hierarchy, and doors that opened for her seal. Now she saw how often the appearance of unity had depended on removing anyone likely to disagree.

Another impact shook the hall. This one brought a runner through the gallery, blood along one sleeve where stone chips had cut him.

"North counterscarp taken," he said. "Marshal Roe's engineers are laying mantlets toward Flood Bastion. Captain Taal requests two reserve companies and says the cistern mouth must be cleared of civilians before dark."

Maela returned her sword belt to her waist. "He gets one company from palace reserve and the brewery archers. The second goes to the cistern mouth. Tell Jorren evacuation outranks salvage."

The runner saluted and left.

His report split the room along a new line. Three officers urged an immediate levy from the shelter circles, arguing that anyone able to carry a paving stone could reinforce Flood Bastion. The laundress with burned arms demanded to know who would carry water if the shelters emptied. A river captain proposed abandoning Lantern Ward to shorten the defense. Lantern Ward's elected resident, a boy-faced cobbler named Edris, spread both hands on the map until his fingertips covered six black alleys.

"There are eleven hundred people in those blocks," Edris said. "Four hundred cannot climb. Give us carts and two hours."

"We do not have two hours," the captain replied.

"Then give us one, and put your soldiers on the carts."

Maela could have decided in a breath. The geometry favored withdrawal. Lantern Ward projected beyond the second wall, its tiled roofs exposed to fire arrows and its lanes too narrow for a shield company. Yet Pell's yellow route crossed the district before turning toward the cistern. Abandoning it without moving those eleven hundred would fill the evacuation road with terrified people at the same moment disciplined retreat required it clear.

"One hour," Maela said. "The brewery archers hold the eastern lane after they deliver the reserve company. Every empty military cart reports to Edris. Anyone who can walk leaves first, carrying water. The bedbound move under guard. At the hour bell, defenders collapse the tile sheds across the northern lane and withdraw."

Edris did not thank her. "Who checks the houses?"

"Your circle names search pairs. Each pair takes one soldier."

The captain started to object, then saw that Maela had given civilians authority over the search while keeping soldiers responsible for its protection. He scratched the change onto his slate. The decision consumed seven minutes. It also meant no officer could later report Lantern Ward empty merely because his patrol had failed to look behind cellar doors.

As messengers carried the order away, voices gathered in the gallery below. A palace lieutenant had shown the Compact copy to his company. Half had sworn they served Ordan's named heir and demanded a coronation before taking the north reserve post. The other half said their oath bound them to Vael rather than a bloodline. Their quarrel blocked a stair needed by stretcher crews.

Maela found the lieutenant at the gallery turn, surrounded by soldiers with drawn faces and clean spearheads. Hunger had made rituals precious to them. They wanted a sovereign who could make their losses belong to a story older than Osra's siege.

"You swore to defend the city under its lawful command," Maela told them. "I remain that command while the council recognizes my office. If any soldier needs my ancestry to carry a stretcher, leave your spear here. The shelter circles require hands. If you can serve Vael without choosing its grandchildren's ruler tonight, take the north stair."

One veteran laid down his spear. Shame colored his cheeks, but he went toward the stretcher crews as ordered. The rest filed north. No cheer followed them. Their morale had not mended. It had changed its object.

When Maela returned to the hall, Edris had already marked Lantern Ward's search pairs beside Pell's route. Defense reclaimed the room from inheritance one accountable name at a time.

"There," Hadran said. "You decide, and we obey. Give that reality its proper name."

Maela looked toward the bronze doors at the rear of the hall. Beyond them, stairs descended to the exclusive court. The royal bearing seal hung at her neck under her cuirass, a disk of pale riverstone in a cage of gold. Ordan had placed it in her hand when illness made stairs impossible for him. She remembered his apology. Not for the locked court, only for the weight.

"Come below," she said.

The chaplain waited on the first landing with a narrow crown wrapped in blue cloth. He had brought oil, a bowl, and the genealogical roll. His assistants occupied the width of the stair. Behind them, the lower bronze door showed four shallow depressions around its frame. Three had been filled with royal plaster generations ago. Only the human handprint remained open.

Maela stopped before it. The crowd packed the stair above her. Pell stood one step back, not at Maela's side as lover or subordinate, but close enough to see every motion.

"Your Majesty," the chaplain began.

"Crown Marshal."

"The city may not understand a distinction made during bombardment."

"Then say it more than once."

She drew out the royal bearing seal.

Its weight had always seemed modest. A guard could wear more metal in buckles. Yet doors throughout Vael answered the stone. Archive locks released. Council chambers admitted. The lower court recognized one bearer while refusing fault-readers, hive keepers, counterweight houses, and the citizens whose fields received whatever season emerged. Maela had used that convenience long after she understood its injustice because every crisis arrived with reasons to postpone a different law.

Veka spoke from the stair. "If you destroy it, can the court open?"

"The door may jam shut."

"Can your smiths cut it?"

"Eventually."

"Then the question concerns time, not holiness."

That drew a sharper laugh. The chaplain flinched. Maela almost smiled.

Pell set her tally slates down. From an apron loop she removed a bridge pin, square-headed and thick as Maela's thumb. "River brass," she said. "Ugly, durable, and already implicated in public transport."

Maela placed the disk on the stair. Hadran pushed down through the crowd.

"Wait. Break the dynasty after we survive it. Osra can claim the city has fallen into mob law. Every wavering company beyond these walls will hear that Ordan's heir smashed her own right while enemy ladders rose."

"They will hear that I remain Crown Marshal. They will receive orders bearing my military authority. They will also hear that Crown Hinge no longer belongs to my children, should I ever have them."

"Words cannot create shared custody."

"No. People must do that. The words prevent me from forbidding them to begin."

Maela took Pell's bridge pin. One blow chipped the gold cage. A second bent it away from the riverstone. The third split the royal bearing seal along a pale fault she had never noticed.

Nothing thundered. No hidden sanction struck her hand. Two halves lay on old stone, unequal in size and equally useless.

The chaplain lowered the wrapped crown. From above came a sound like wind gathering through reeds. It resolved into voices, first uncertain, then steady enough to reach the gallery. Crown Marshal. Crown Marshal. Not queen.

Maela returned the pin to Pell. "Leave the halves on the council table. Publicly."

"I will inventory them as two pieces of failed access policy."

"Use smaller handwriting."

They carried both halves upstairs. Hadran did not leave. He removed the coronation stool from against the wall and gave it to an attendant, who took it into the gallery. That small surrender mattered because everyone saw him make it.

During the next hour, the Hall of Rivers built a defense charter under bombardment. Maela retained authority over walls, sorties, and evacuation orders as Crown Marshal. District circles gained seats over shelter movement and ration publication. Vennik houses received direct control of load limits on bridges and barges. Mahr fault-readers could order a local withdrawal when stone-song crossed a witnessed danger threshold. Aeli delegates could halt pollen-bearing work that threatened an unmapped orchard or coast. No group received power alone. Every refusal required a stated reason entered beside a cost map, open for challenge after immediate danger passed.

It did not produce harmony. Two councilors resigned. A palace company threatened to follow them until Jorren sent word that any soldier with leisure for succession politics could carry cistern stretchers. Merchants protested inventory access. One shelter delegate accused Pell of protecting military grain; Pell answered by opening the army ledger first.

At dusk the north wall bells began a continuous warning. Osra's mantlets had reached Flood Bastion. Maela issued reserve positions, ordered every nonessential cart toward the cistern route, and assigned the palace kitchens to public control. People moved faster because they had argued over the route themselves. Others moved slower because debate carried cost. Neither fact resembled the obedience of a coronation.

Before leaving for the wall, Maela returned to the bronze lower door.

Smiths had chipped out the old plaster from the three blocked handprints. The work exposed darker stone beneath, worn smooth by palms from eight centuries ago. Veka placed her hand in the Mahr hollow. Pell used her left hand in the Vennik mark. The Aeli cooper pressed long fingers into the third. Maela took the remaining human place without seal or title.

Four bodies leaned.

The door resisted, gave a finger's width, then opened on bronze pivots that had not moved for generations. Cool air rose from Crown Hinge. It carried wet mineral scent, dust, and a low vibration Maela felt through her boots rather than heard.

No guard barred the stairs. Laundresses entered behind councilors. Masons followed captains. Refugees carried lamps down to the court from which their costs had once been excluded.

Maela waited at the threshold until every ordinary witness who wished to descend had passed her. Then she turned toward the north wall, still heir to a dead king, still responsible for a living city, and queen of nothing at all.

Chapter 20 — The Breaking of Vael Tern

Pell knew the full assault had begun when every plumb weight in the cistern yard kicked east at once.

The bronze weights struck their frames. Mules screamed between the capstans. A row of water jars toppled from the intake wall before the sound arrived, a single blow from Osra's stone-casters that seemed to flatten the dawn against Vael Tern.

"Reset every line," Pell shouted. "Do not trust a hook you did not touch after that strike."

Vennik riggers moved among the frames with chalk and mallets. Some wore bridge-house knots at their collars; others belonged to no house Pell knew. Hunger had sharpened their faces, but their hands checked pins in the proper order. Load first, anchor second, route third. Panic changed none of those.

Above the yard, continuous horns called from the outer wall. Three long notes marked artillery. Two rising notes marked infantry advancing under mantlets. A broken pulse from Flood Bastion meant sappers had reached its foot.

Nim Vask stood atop an evacuation barge with a sounding pole in one hand and the original Compact tablets packed in a tarred chest behind them. "East channel rose another palm."

"Can you clear the culvert?"

"If the roof stays where roofs generally perform best."

"Your optimism sickens me."

"Inherited defect."

Pell looked at the board nailed beside the cistern gate. The first count on it came from the siege registry: 61,400 residents and 8,600 refugees within the outer walls. Seventy thousand bodies, each of whom required space, water, and time. During the past days, barges and submerged carts had taken 9,760 through the old water road. Another 4,440 needed to clear before the gate shut if they were to reach the planned 14,200 evacuated before a breach.

That would leave 55,800 inside.

No chant could make those figures noble. Pell had forbidden the copyists to round them.

A messenger slid down the yard stair, missed the final tread, and struck both knees. "Quartermaster. Crown Marshal says the north reserve has engaged. General Olt's western companies are attacking Osra's cavalry screen outside the river road. Captain Taal requests the next barge leave half loaded so it can return before the intake drops."

"No half loads," Nim said.

Pell agreed. A barge moved at the speed of its draft and the tunnel current, not at the speed of an officer's hope. Underloading would save minutes outward and waste a full return passage. "Tell Jorren the next barge carries capacity. Two hundred seated, thirty stretchers, no household chests. His scouts choose the walking column."

"He says Flood Bastion may not hold until noon."

"Then he should avoid scheduling the breach before our return tide."

The messenger stared.

"That means go."

At the outer river arch, the first enemy arrows rattled against overhead shutters. Osra's cavalry had found a firing angle across the south embankment. Pell ordered wet hides raised over the boarding lane and sent two counterweight crews to change the draw on the shutter frames. Every crew she moved from the gate slowed the barges. Every crew she left exposed became a body nobody else could replace.

The board changed to 9,960.

A second artillery strike hit close enough to crack the cistern plaster. Water sheeted from a new seam and ran across Pell's boots. She tasted lime in the air. On the upper landing, defenders dragged three wounded archers down from the wall, one with a shaft through his cheek. Lessa Olt met them beside the pump house and pointed without ceremony: walking wounded to the brewery tunnel, chest wounds to her tables, dead against the blue tiles where stretcher crews would not stumble.

"General Olt?" Pell asked.

"Alive," Lessa said. "Her tusk heard the sapper galleries through the west abutment. She has been singing warning lines since midnight. The copper wrap is cutting her gum."

"Can you stop her?"

"Would you like to stop counting loads?"

"No."

"Then respect equivalent stupidity."

A roar from above drowned the horns. Pell watched a ripple cross the cistern pool. Mortar fragments fell through a ventilation grate, followed by daylight where no daylight had belonged.

Flood Bastion's lower horn ceased.

Pell climbed to the wall because the load board no longer told her what would fall on it next.

The stair opened beneath a wooden gallery packed with reserve spears. Through a split in the parapet she saw Osra's assault spread across the northern ground. Heartland infantry advanced behind layered mantlets roofed in wet oxhide. Engineers rolled two timber towers along roads made from doors stripped from evacuated farms. Stone-casters worked from the orchard terraces, their arms dropping in sequence so one missile remained in flight whenever another struck. Tavan Roe had not wasted ammunition on terror. She had cut the wall into separated commands.

Along the west, Kharza's black-clay banners pressed toward the river road. Her federation held three distinct lines rather than one merged army. Kiln troops used ceramic shields against the cavalry archers. Imperial Mahr defectors carried long city pikes. A Reed splinter company moved through drainage cuts where horses could not follow. They aimed for the siege stores and the road to the cistern mouth, not for Osra's command pavilion.

A signal disk flashed from the Kiln line, three white turns followed by red. Pell knew the code because she had written it into the Four Banners plan: road blocked, wagons available, civilian route possible if the obstruction came down. Between Kharza's force and Vael stood a siege barricade made from chained supply carts. Osra's cavalry waited behind it. If the coalition broke through, evacuees from West Sluice could march beyond bow range rather than crowding the downstream quay.

Pell took a mirror from the wall station and answered with the route code. West Sluice active. Capacity constrained. Do not pursue cavalry.

The Mahr disk repeated her final command. Kharza had accepted it.

Below the west parapet, Vennik bridge crews prepared two counterweight baskets on chains salvaged from the river boom. Their plan required dropping the baskets from Vael's wall to drag apart the enemy barricade. Each basket carried broken paving stone and weighed enough to crush the crews if a brake failed. Pell checked the anchor bolts with her thumb. One shifted in wet mortar.

"Replace that pair," she told the crew chief.

"No spare bolts."

"Then run the back line through the butcher's chimney."

"Chimney leans."

"Toward Osra's army. A considerate defect."

They fed hawser across the roof while arrows struck sparks from the parapet. Two Kiln shield files advanced outside, closing the last ground to the barricade. Pell waited until cavalry committed toward them. Then she chopped her hand down.

The bridge crews released both baskets.

Stone loads plunged outside the wall. Chains snapped tight across the siege carts. The suspect anchor tore loose, but the back line caught around the butcher's chimney. Brickwork leaned farther and held. Below, two chained carts rolled sideways. A gap opened just wide enough for six Mahr abreast.

Kharza's Kiln troops did not charge through it. They braced shields and let the cavalry enter the narrowed ground first. Imperial pikes struck from behind their shoulders. Reed fighters came out of the drainage cuts at the horses' flank, silver caps bright in the smoke. The cavalry recoiled, fouling its own barricade. Kharza's second line moved on the wagon teams.

Osra's engineers turned a stone-caster toward the west wall.

Pell saw its throwing arm lower. "Clear the anchors!"

Crews cut free the chains and ran. The missile crossed the river road like a dark moon. It hit the butcher's chimney, drove brick through the roof, and tore one basket line away. Three riggers disappeared beneath the collapse. Pell reached the nearest, a human apprentice with both ankles pinned. She and the crew chief levered roofing off him while another impact showered them with tile.

Outside, the freed supply wagons rolled toward Kharza's lines. The road did not open. Heartland infantry formed beyond the cavalry and drove the Mahr back from the gap. Yet six grain carts and two medical wagons escaped west. More important, Osra had turned artillery away from Flood Bastion for two precious casts.

Pell flashed a final message: take wagons, break contact.

Kharza's disk answered once. The federation withdrew by distinct routes, each formation covering another without surrendering its banner. Pell watched the Reed splinter fighters vanish into the drains last. Kharza had not won the road. She had forced Osra to spend cavalry, infantry, and stone on holding it, and she had carried away stores intended for the assault.

That kind of defeat could feed people. Pell entered eight recovered wagons on the wall slate and three missing riggers beneath them. She refused to let one figure erase the other.

Outside the east arch, Vael defenders dropped hooked stones onto sapper mantlets. The enemy answered with clay pots that burst into greasy smoke. Wind drove it back against the city.

"Quartermaster!" Jorren called.

He crouched below the parapet with a blood-dark bandage around his thigh. He had tied it high and kept moving, which told Pell the wound hurt badly enough to frighten him. His scouts carried no matching shield patterns; they had taken whatever remained in store.

"Your barge leaves full," Pell said before he could speak.

"Good. The north road ends in half an hour. Flood Bastion's footing has separated from the curtain wall. We can hold the upper stones, but when the lower chamber goes, it takes the gate stairs."

"Evacuation pace requires four more barge cycles."

"You have two through the cistern. Move the walking columns through West Sluice after that."

"West Sluice has no counterweight brake."

Jorren pointed to the river chain. "Take one from the palace bell."

Pell almost objected that the bell frame could not bear a side draw. Then she followed his sight line. The bell itself provided the missing mass. Forty years of ceremonies had hung three tons of bronze above a city that presently needed a brake more than a voice.

"I require eighty laborers and every length of hawser in the council hall," she said.

"You get forty soldiers and the coronation ropes."

"There was no coronation."

"Then the ropes are rested."

A stone missile struck the timber gallery two bays away. Planks broke upward. One reserve soldier vanished in a burst of splinters; another hung through the floor by one leg. Pell crawled beneath the rail as the gallery tilted. Jorren cut the trapped soldier's belt and hauled her free. The outer end of the gallery fell against the wall, opening a gap where three enemy ladders rose through smoke.

Vael's spears met them. The fight compressed into hands, ladder rails, and the effort to keep footing on boards slick with rain. Pell had no weapon except a brass tally punch. She used it on the wrist of a Heartland soldier who reached over the parapet, then stamped his fingers until he fell. Jorren drove a broken spear down between ladder rungs.

"Go build your brake," he said.

Pell went.

By the time she reached the palace bell, councilors and shelter workers had already stripped the coronation ropes from their chests. Blue and white cords lay across the square beside tarred river hawser. Maela stood on the bell platform in a plain helm, assigning the reserve by district rather than rank. No crown. The north wind pulled her cloak toward the wall.

"Flood Bastion is separating," Pell said. "Jorren gives the cistern two more cycles. I need the bell as West Sluice brake."

Maela looked up once. "Take it."

Temple attendants protested when Vennik climbers cut the ceremonial lashings. Pell gave them work coiling the ropes. Forty soldiers lowered the bell until its bronze lip hovered over the paving. Counterweight crews built a timber cradle from the abandoned coronation dais and four council benches. Nobody asked whether furniture remembered its old purpose.

The ground shook again. Hairline cracks raced across the square toward the lower court.

Kharza came through the west passage with twelve Mahr soldiers carrying two wounded between shield poles. Blood ran down the copper around her left tusk. The crack from Ember Ford had widened enough to show a dark line almost to the root. Each distant impact made her jaw flinch.

"West abutment will hold," Kharza said. Her speech blurred around the injured tusk. "North will not. Two galleries beneath Flood Bastion have joined. When Roe burns their props, the collapse runs south along the drain course."

"How far?" Maela asked.

"Past the outer wall gate. Possibly to the cistern stair."

Lessa arrived behind her and seized Kharza's chin. "You are done singing."

"I can still hear."

"So can I when you grind the split wider. Hearing does not make the act intelligent."

Kharza's eyes found Pell. "How many out?"

"Ten thousand three hundred sixty."

"Too few."

"Your contribution has been noted."

The bell settled into its cradle. Pell threaded the main hawser through the crown loop, around two basalt posts, and down toward West Sluice. Her missing fingers made the wet rope difficult to grip. A young Mahr mason braced it against her palm without comment. Together they reeved the final line.

Maela sent Kharza to command the inner-wall withdrawal. Kharza refused until Lessa cut away the copper wrap and bound her jaw with leather, muffling stone-song and speech together. The general left furious, which kept her moving.

At the cistern, the next barge took two hundred seated civilians and thirty stretchers. An Aeli baker tried to give his place to a sack of flour. Nim threw the sack aboard and kept him in the seat, solving the argument by capacity. Children wore wax tablets around their necks with names and destination marks. Shelter searchers checked each against district lists.

Count: 10,590.

The barge entered the submerged arch. Teams walked the tow ropes along ledges cut under black water. Overhead, the city sustained another series of blows.

Pell stationed herself at West Sluice. The sluice had once carried floodwater beneath the southern curtain. Its outer mouth opened downstream of Osra's main lines, but a steep run made uncontrolled descent fatal. With the palace bell rigged as drag weight, shallow evacuation skiffs could lower through one at a time. The first carried sixty walkers and no luggage. When the brake line tightened, the dais timbers groaned.

"Hold three turns!" Pell called.

Riggers locked the hawser around basalt. The bell lifted a finger's width from its cradle. The skiff descended without striking either wall.

A second followed. Then a third.

The assault reached the outer gate while the fourth loaded. Heartland infantry poured through smoke beneath the gatehouse arch, using shield roofs against stones thrown from above. Vael defenders dropped the portcullis. It stopped halfway when fractured masonry blocked its groove. Men and women fought beneath the iron teeth.

Jorren's signal runner arrived without Jorren. "Captain says last cistern barge now. After it leaves, he seals the upper stair."

"His withdrawal route?"

"Gate gallery to the brewery tunnel."

Pell knew that route crossed the predicted collapse. "Tell him west stairs only."

"He says west stairs are carrying Lantern Ward."

"Then tell him to leave before the final column."

The runner's face answered. Jorren had already chosen where he stood in the queue.

Pell gripped the child's slate hanging at her own neck, the running total scratched and corrected until its surface resembled cracked ice. She wanted to go to the gate. She wanted to drag Jorren away by his injured leg. Neither desire altered the brake ratio.

Count: 11,830.

Smoke entered West Sluice. Enemy archers had reached roofs along the northern market and were firing toward the loading lanes. Maela sent the brewery archers to answer. Their arrows crossed over civilians crouched beneath wet hides.

A skiff jammed when a floating roof beam turned broadside in the channel. Sixty people halted under open sky. Pell shifted the bell line to lift the skiff's stern, then sent two swimmers with hooked poles. One swimmer took an arrow through the shoulder. The other broke the beam loose. Lessa's assistants hauled both out while the brake crews lowered the skiff.

Count: 12,070.

At midday, Flood Bastion lifted.

The stone did not fall at first. A hundred paces of wall rose on a bed of smoke and pulverized mortar as Osra's sappers burned the gallery props beneath it. For one suspended breath Pell saw daylight under the foundation. Then the bastion folded inward. Towers split. The curtain wall dragged the gatehouse with it. Thousands of tons of stone struck the north districts and sent a cloud across Vael that turned noon brown.

The shock snapped West Sluice's forward post.

Hawser tore through the brake crew. Pell hit the paving on her left shoulder. The palace bell rolled in its cradle, crushing one timber and trapping a Vennik rigger beneath the other. Downstream, the loaded skiff accelerated toward the bend.

Pell crawled to the loose line. Her right hand could not close around it. She wrapped the hawser across her back and thrust her left arm through a loop.

"Second anchor!" she yelled.

Nim reached her first. Together they hauled the line toward an intact basalt post while workers levered the bell. A Mahr soldier shoved his shield through the crown loop as a temporary pin. The shield bent. The line caught.

The skiff struck the bend sideways, hard enough to throw people from benches, but it did not overturn.

Under the collapsed cradle, the trapped rigger made no sound. Pell touched their throat, found nothing, and ordered the body moved clear of the line. Grief had to wait somewhere it would not obstruct transport.

The north district vanished behind dust. Bells from three inner towers called retreat. Another horn continued at the cistern gate, one steady note that should have ended.

Jorren.

Pell ran toward it with Nim shouting after her.

Broken masonry blocked the direct stair. She climbed through a bathhouse whose upper rooms had collapsed into steaming pools. Defenders came south carrying wounded, shields, water buckets, and children found during the Lantern Ward search. Osra's infantry moved behind them through the breach in disciplined blocks. Tavan's officers directed the advance toward the surface Crown Hinge approach and the inner river gates rather than allowing a sack. That restraint made the attack more dangerous. It possessed an objective.

At the cistern gallery, Jorren held a crank that kept the flood gate raised.

A stone beam had fallen across his legs. Blood soaked the channel beneath him. Six civilians crawled under the gate while two scouts tried to lever the beam. Beyond them, the final barge waited in the tunnel current.

"Leave it," Jorren said when he saw Pell.

"An offensively poor order."

"Crank drops if I let go. Gate pins sheared."

Pell examined the housing. The right bearing had split. Without the crank's pressure, the iron gate would fall into the barge roof and block the tunnel. She could brace it with a counterweight if she had twenty minutes, two beams, and a crew not being shot at.

She had a broken spear, one intact pulley, and perhaps two minutes.

"Tie the crank," Pell told the scouts.

"Line slips off," Jorren said. His voice had thinned. "Tried twice."

A child crawled beneath the opening. Jorren's arms shook, and the gate descended a thumb before he forced it back.

Pell set the intact pulley against the cracked housing. Her missing fingers could not hold the pin square. One scout held it while she drove it with her tally punch. Enemy shouts entered the bathhouse behind them.

"Last barge has four hundred sixty," Jorren said. "That makes your number."

"I know arithmetic."

"You also lie when frightened."

The remark struck too close to the old ration ledger, to nine people shifted into hunger because Pell had hidden scarcity behind a corrected total. She met his eyes. "Four hundred sixty makes 14,200. The number will be published with your name and every person we fail to carry."

"Leave my name off the glorious part."

"There is no glorious column."

"Good."

The pulley seated. Pell ran line through it and around the beam, creating enough advantage for the scouts to lift one edge. Jorren tried to pull free. His face emptied of color. The beam moved no farther. Rubble had pinned him below the knees, and enemy boots sounded on the stair.

"Take the scouts," he said.

Pell looked at the crank, the gate, the barge roof sliding beneath it. No arrangement let him release his hands.

One scout began to protest. Jorren ordered him through in the flat voice of a captain. The man obeyed crying.

Pell put her left hand over Jorren's on the crank. "I can stay."

"You have fifty-five thousand eight hundred loads left."

"People."

"Then count them as people. Go."

An arrow struck the stone beside his head. Pell ducked, seized the final scout, and crawled beneath the gate as the barge moved. Behind her, Jorren held the crank. He held it while enemy soldiers reached the gallery. He held until the stern cleared.

The gate fell.

Iron hit water with a sound deeper than the battle. The tunnel went dark.

No one on the barge spoke. Pell sat among four hundred sixty civilians and listened to the gate scrape along its broken bearing behind them. Her tally slate had cracked in the fall. She fitted the pieces together and wrote the final total across both.

14,200 evacuated.

When the barge emerged at the downstream quay, she gave the slate to Nim. "Keep this with the tablets. If the city falls, the count does not."

"You are returning."

"Fifty-five thousand eight hundred."

Nim closed their hand over the slate. "I heard him."

Pell climbed the west maintenance stair with the surviving scouts. By then Osra controlled the outer wall, Flood Bastion, the outer river gates, and the broad surface approach leading toward Crown Hinge. Vael's defenders had withdrawn behind the inner districts. Kharza's coalition remained outside the breach, cut from the city by cavalry and falling stone. Her black-clay banners moved west under pressure. The copper binding on her left tusk had failed; Lessa led her away with blood dark across both their collars.

Maela waited at the lower-court door, holding it open for wounded defenders and civilians. Dust had turned her blue cloak gray.

"Jorren?" she asked.

Pell shook her head once.

Maela shut her eyes. Artillery struck again before grief could take a fuller shape.

"Osra's vanguard reaches the citadel court within the hour," Maela said. "We cannot hold the surface approach."

"Below, then."

They sent district messengers through the inner streets with orders to shelter rather than rush the lower stairs. The hinge chambers could hold command, healers, and only a fraction of the population. A stampede would kill people Osra's assault had not reached. Pell hated the choice. She entered it in the ledger anyway.

Crown Hinge's bronze doors closed on a column of survivors. Above them, Vael Tern's outer wall belonged to Osra. The cistern route lay sealed by Jorren's body and the iron gate he had kept raised. Beyond the buried stone, 55,800 civilians remained trapped among the inner districts.

Pell descended last, carrying no crown, no victory, and one blank slate she did not yet know how to fill.

Chapter 21 — Under the Crown Hinge

Kharza heard Jorren's death in the way Pell counted bandages.

"Thirty-six clean. Twelve boiled and drying. Nine for chest packing. Four reserved for the hinge team."

Pell knelt beside the lower court's dry fountain, sorting linen with her left hand while her right rested against her ribs. Dust whitened her slate-gray skin. She had written no name beside the empty place in her inventory, but each number came out clipped, stripped of the dry irritation that usually made scarcity answerable.

Kharza stood beneath the first arch and let Lessa tighten the leather around her jaw. The binding muted vibration through her left tusk. It did not stop pain. Every step of Osra's troops across the upper court traveled down the Crown Hinge and found the split in the tusk like a chisel entering a seam.

"If you sing again tonight," Lessa said, "I will pack your mouth with wool and assign two children to watch you."

"Children need sleep."

"Then behave more sensibly than they do."

Around them, the survivors of the retreat made a refuge out of a sacred court. Vael defenders lay beneath murals of seven river cities. Aeli healers shaved ruined cloth from burns. Mahr soldiers stacked ceramic shields into privacy screens. Vennik crews hung lamps from old bearing hooks and marked every step where the floor had lifted. Humans from Lantern Ward ladled water without asking which banner a cup belonged to.

Maela moved among the pallets in a gray cloak, still giving wall orders through runners because no one else held the whole defense map. Her face changed when she reached the scout captain's surviving pair. One scout gave her Jorren's knife and reported the last words he had heard. Maela listened without touching the weapon. Then she assigned both scouts to sleep before taking another post.

Grief had no place large enough to lie down. It stood beside every job.

From overhead came three muffled blows. Osra's engineers were clearing the surface approach. Dust sifted into the fountain basin. Refugees looked up, though thirty paces of hinge stone separated them from the assault.

Kharza felt the next pressure through both tusks.

Two notes.

She gripped the arch. For a moment Ashwall returned: refugees outside a ration line, stone rising under their feet, the Deep Hart pressing its shape through foundations built to carry only an upper sky. The outwork had sung in two pitches before it folded upward. She had believed the second note came from a fracture reflecting itself.

Here the lower note arrived first. A higher answer followed half a breath later.

"General?" Sevrin asked.

He stood near the fountain with a white film covering the places where leaf-filaments had burned from his temples. His eyes still saw the court, but no living thread connected that sight to constellations. He carried a brass pollen frame like a blind person's staff, tapping joints and listening to the grains trapped inside.

Kharza raised one hand for quiet. The court obeyed unevenly. Wounded people breathed. Water dripped. Above, iron scraped across stone.

Again: low pressure from beneath, then a thinner reply from somewhere west.

"Paired," she said through the jaw binding. "Not echo. Two load paths."

Sevrin angled his head. "How far apart?"

"Farther than the chamber."

Pell rose slowly. Pain tightened her mouth. "This court has two counterweight galleries on the royal plan. North and south."

"Royal plans omit useful populations," Maela said. "They may omit useful walls."

A palace mason brought three copied floor sheets from the archive alcove. The sheets showed Crown Hinge as one immense crescent below the citadel, with bearing chambers clustered along its inner curve. Kharza laid her palm on the floor. The first note ran across the crescent. The answer did not. It traveled downward at an angle no surface room could occupy.

"There," she said, pointing beyond the drawn foundation. "A road or gallery leaves the west bearing."

"Toward solid crust," the mason objected.

"Toward what your plan calls solid crust."

Pell had already begun checking wall joints. The lower court's western mural depicted the Inversion: two constellations separated by a band of black stone, with laborers standing upright on both sides of the band. Royal plaster covered part of the scene. Veka, the Mahr mason who had witnessed Maela break the seal, chipped at its edge with a healer's blunt knife.

Beneath the plaster, a row of small figures walked under the lower constellation.

"Not decoration," Sevrin said. He touched the pollen frame to the mural. Grains inside shifted toward the hidden figures. "Buried pollen pulls west and down."

Maela assigned twelve rested soldiers to guard the refuge, then led a smaller party through the western arch: Kharza, Pell, Sevrin, Lessa, Veka, and two Vennik riggers. No one argued over ancestry or rank. The people capable of reading the problem went.

The arch ended at a bronze grille thick with mineral scale. Royal records named it a drainage screen. No water had touched the dust behind it for centuries. Veka found hinge pins rather than pipe fittings. Pell inserted her bridge punch, worked the lower pin free, and swore when her injured shoulder failed on the second.

Kharza took the bar. The motion pulled against her jaw. She used her weight instead of her tusks. Bronze yielded with a shriek.

Beyond lay a descending gallery wide enough for two counterweight sledges. Grooves ran along the floor. Each groove split at regular intervals into a shallow fan, then rejoined. Wear had polished the stone along both edges, forward and back.

Pell crouched beside the nearest split. "Sledges traveled down."

"And returned," one rigger said.

"Frequently. Look at the inner lip. A load dragged outward would not polish that face."

They followed the track. The air cooled until breath showed. Lamps revealed old knots of rope turned black and hard with mineral deposits. At every seventh pace, a number had been carved in four scripts. Royal masons had cut lines through three of them, but the depth preserved their shapes.

Sevrin traced an Aeli mark without pretending he could read its celestial sheen. "Measure of arc."

"How much?" Maela asked.

"One degree. Repeated."

The required words seemed too small for the distance beneath them. Kharza had watched Ember Hinge move that far and change rain across Varkesh. Here the same measure appeared not as an accident wrested from battle, but as a worker's mark repeated along a road.

At the gallery's end, the hinge root entered a chasm.

The buried sky shone below.

No roof enclosed it. Darkness curved away into impossible distance, pierced by constellations bright enough to throw shadows upward. The Deep Hart's antlers lay across the lower vault, each star veiled by drifting mineral cloud. Farther west, a river of blue points bent under the world. Between the gallery and that inverted firmament, a stone causeway descended along the hinge root until it vanished behind a luminous ridge.

Kharza's stomach tightened. Her body knew down from weight and up from breath, yet the lower stars asked both senses to renegotiate.

Pell gripped a handrail. "There are anchor sockets."

Square cuts lined the causeway, each matched by a worn circle on the opposite side. Portable counterweights could travel that road in stages. The old laborers had not merely reached the hinge from above. They had crossed its underside.

A narrow platform clung to the causeway's first bend. Bronze rails surrounded a stone table cut with channels. Pell went first on a safety line, testing each slab before shifting her weight. Veka followed with one tusk exposed. Maela ordered the two soldiers to remain at the gallery mouth so a single collapse would not take every witness.

Sevrin crossed third. Without star-sight he kept one palm on the inner rail and asked Pell to describe every change in the lower constellation. He did not hide the care required. The loss at Salt Hinge had not made him helpless, but it had removed the private certainty on which his old authority relied.

Kharza watched from the gallery until the leather binding warmed with her restrained jaw. Sending others into a load path offended every habit she had once mistaken for duty. Veka glanced back only once.

"Your refusal authority works at a surprising distance," the mason called.

"I can revoke it."

"Then it would not be authority."

Pell reached the platform and brushed mineral dust from the stone table. Four recessed bowls occupied its corners. Between them, a set of straight lines crossed arcs divided into small units. Someone had driven a royal chisel through the inscriptions, yet the blows had struck shallow. Oblique lamplight revealed characters beneath the damage.

"Read what survives," Maela said.

Veka sounded the table with a knuckle. "Mahr line says the stress must answer from both roots before release."

Sevrin bent close to the Aeli arc. "This marks pollen drift before movement, then pollen drift after return. Not a completed exchange. A comparison."

Pell found a Vennik notation beneath the eastern bowl. "Load down, load reduced, load back. It gives ratios for every one-degree interval."

The hyphenated measure had come from a laborer's hand, not a royal tutor. Kharza felt anger move through her with unusual calm. Generations of court teaching had declared partial movement an emergency failure. Here, workers had recorded it as ordinary practice, with return loads and comparison marks precise enough for apprentices.

Maela examined the human river-city line. "Witness before advance. Witness after reversal. Each city records its own water mark."

"A cost map on stone," Pell said.

No one answered with triumph. The table could have belonged to a time when lower pressure differed, ropes remained fresh, and all four hinges answered together. An old instruction did not guarantee a present act.

A crack sounded under the platform.

Pell froze. Veka dropped to one knee and pressed her uncapped tusk to the slab. The causeway's inner edge shed a fan of chips into the sky below. They fell toward the Deep Hart until darkness swallowed them.

"Outer bracket shifted," Veka said. "Weight inward. Slowly."

Kharza seized the safety line. Maela braced behind her. Sevrin moved only when Pell told him the path lay clear. He crossed first, one hand following the rail. Veka backed after him while keeping contact with the slab. Pell stayed at the table long enough to press wet cloth into the chiseled Vennik ratios, making a dark rubbing she could copy later.

"Leave it," Kharza said.

"This is leaving it efficiently."

The platform dipped another finger. Pell lunged for the gallery. Kharza and Maela hauled the line, dragging her across as the outer bracket pulled free. Half the platform rotated away from the hinge root. The stone table remained caught on its inner rail, tilted toward the lower stars but still legible.

Pell lay on the gallery floor and looked back at the hanging record. "We now possess a practical demonstration that ancient infrastructure also fails."

"Enter that prominently," Sevrin said.

Maela helped Pell sit. "We have instruction, wear, and a dangerous road. Nothing more."

Kharza listened to the two load paths answering the shifted platform. The paired notes changed in opposite directions. Added weight deepened the lower line; relieved weight sharpened the return. The relation offered information no single pitch could carry.

That, too, belonged in the test. Not proof of safety. A way to know when the stone began to refuse.

Lessa blocked Kharza from stepping onto the causeway. "Your tusk has contributed enough blood to public knowledge."

"I only need to hear the first span."

"No."

"Refusal authority," Pell said quietly. "You gave it to her."

The words hurt more cleanly than the tusk. Kharza stepped back. Command that mattered had to survive inconvenience to herself.

Veka took the forward place instead. She removed the clay guard from one tusk and pressed its tip to the handrail. "Two tracks," she said. "The causeway bears toward us and away."

Sevrin knelt beside a patch of pale growth in a socket. Without star-sight, he used a knife to lift one filament into his pollen frame. The trapped grains moved, first toward the Deep Hart, then toward a dim cluster behind them.

"Paired constellations again," he said. "One below the causeway's forward face, one behind Crown Hinge. The laborers may have aligned to both."

Maela studied the returning polish. "The court taught a single complete motion. Ordan's tutors described anything less as uncontrolled failure."

"Ember disproved part of that," Kharza said.

"Ember moved," Sevrin replied. "It did not move back."

That distinction held them silent.

Pell walked the gallery with a measuring cord. She counted fan-shaped splits, anchor sockets, and scars where old brake teeth had bitten the floor. Each fan covered the same narrow arc. Some scars pointed down the causeway. Others faced back toward the court.

"These are not construction marks," she said. "Builders do not polish return faces after placing a permanent load. Counterweight teams worked both directions."

"Could a retreating sledge make them?" Maela asked.

"Once, perhaps. Not this often. Look at the wear marks. Deep bite forward, shallow bite back, repeated at each arc measure. They advanced under full load and reversed under less."

Kharza listened through the intact right tusk. The low note came from Crown Hinge's weight against the causeway. Its higher partner trembled through a return line under the court. At Ashwall she had mistaken two structures answering each other for one wall breaking twice.

"We need a test," she said.

Lessa made a disgusted sound.

"Not a bearing," Kharza added. "A transfer between brake teeth. Enough to learn whether the second note answers."

Pell evaluated the nearest sledge frame. Its wood had rotted away, leaving stone runners and bronze eyes. The portable counterweights brought below during the retreat could supply a small trial load. Moving it across one fan would shift less mass than the bell Pell had used at West Sluice. Yet Crown Hinge multiplied every load through the roots of the city. Small did not mean harmless.

They returned to the lower court for a public decision.

Maela refused to authorize the test in the gallery alone. She called the provisional civilian seats from the previous day's charter, the surviving wall captains, Veka, Sevrin, Pell, and the healers responsible for people sheltering over the second load path. Kharza described what she heard. Pell drew the scars. Sevrin explained the paired pollen pull and named what remained unknown.

"No claim of safety," he said. "We have evidence of use, not proof that present pressure allows it."

A Lantern Ward delegate asked, "What happens if the old brake breaks?"

"The trial load runs six paces down the gallery," Pell answered. "It may strike the second stop hard enough to crack the western refuge floor."

"Clear that floor first."

The order came not from Maela but from the people sleeping there. They moved their pallets, carried wounded neighbors east, and demanded chalk marks showing the danger line. When the space stood empty, Maela authorized one transfer with three conditions: no stone-song through Kharza's injured tusk, no pollen release, and immediate cutaway if either gallery note rose.

Pell built the trial from two water casks, shield fragments, and a portable counterweight frame. The load weighed less than a cavalry horse. Four Vennik riggers tied separate brake lines so no single failure controlled it. Veka read the forward path. Kharza stood by the return wall with her right tusk against a bronze hook. Sevrin held the pollen frame between both directions.

"First tooth," Pell called.

The riggers eased the frame down the polished groove. Stone runners crossed the beginning of a fan.

Crown Hinge answered with its low note. The court lamps dimmed as dust shook from their wicks.

"Hold," Kharza said.

The frame stopped.

A breath later, the higher note traveled back through the western floor. Not an echo. It carried less force and a distinct rhythm, exactly as the wear had promised.

Sevrin's pollen shifted toward the lower stars.

"Return it," Maela ordered.

Pell reversed the ropes. The frame resisted. The riggers leaned together, boots slipping on dust. For one terrible moment the load continued down despite them. Kharza heard the low note deepen.

"Cut forward line!"

A rigger severed it. Released tension snapped against the grille. Pell's return rope caught around the portable frame, and the load climbed back across the fan until its runner struck the starting tooth.

The higher note sounded first this time. The lower answer followed.

Nothing cracked. That proved only that the trial had not broken this chamber.

Pell sat on the floor, breathing hard. "Reversed across one brake interval."

"With a load too small to move the bearing," Sevrin said.

"Yes."

Maela looked toward the hidden road. "To prove more, someone must take counterweights onto the causeway and measure the hinge from below."

Above them, Osra's soldiers hammered at the sealed upper doors. The rhythm had become organized. They would enter the lower court eventually.

Kharza studied the refuge: wounded enemies treated side by side, citizen delegates guarding a test line, Pell grieving through arithmetic, Maela holding authority without claiming a throne. Defeat had driven them under the structure they had fought to reach. It had also placed them beside an older route no royal plan admitted.

"Small party," Kharza said. "Pell leads the weights. Sevrin reads the pollen. Maela carries public authority. Veka reads stone in my place."

"And you?" Maela asked.

Kharza touched the leather over her cracked left tusk. "I hold the court and make Osra pay for each stair."

Lessa approved by saying nothing.

At dawn, the first expedition lamps moved onto the causeway. Their light descended toward the buried sky, along paired tracks worn by workers who had once carried the heavens forward and back one degree at a time.

Part IV

Chapter 22 — The Road Across the Buried Sky

Pell lowered the first counterweight sledge onto a road that had stars beneath it.

Four Vennik riggers eased the brake ropes while Veka Olt walked backward along the hinge root, one uncapped tusk touching the bronze rail. The sledge carried eight basalt blocks, two water casks, a coil of river hawser, food for three days, and every measuring pin Pell had recovered from Vael's bridge stores. It weighed slightly less than two draft mules and had no opinion about the direction in which it hung.

Pell envied it.

Below the causeway, the buried sky curved through luminous dark. The Deep Hart stretched along the lower vault, its points connected by veils of amber mineral cloud. A blue constellation Pell did not know seemed to pour uphill into the western distance. Dust fell from Crown Hinge and became a faint rain toward those stars.

"Brake three," she called.

The riggers locked the line around a stone tooth. The sledge stopped at the first fan-shaped split. Its rear runner lifted, settled, and remained on the road.

Maela stepped onto the causeway behind Sevrin. She wore no royal insignia. Chalk arrows marked both sleeves, a precaution Pell had added after watching three soldiers vomit at their first sight of the inverted firmament. Sevrin carried two pollen frames and a folded copy of the cost map. The burned skin at his temples remained smooth. He asked Veka to name every change in stone pitch and asked Maela to describe every change in starlight.

Six soldiers completed the party, two human defenders, two Mahr fault-readers chosen by Kharza, and two Vennik riggers from houses other than Vask. Twelve people total. More bodies might have fought better. More food, water, and rope would have left no capacity for the test weights.

At the gallery mouth, Kharza stood with her jaw bound. "Three days," she said around the leather. "After that I close the western arch if Osra enters the court."

"You will leave before closure," Maela said.

Kharza looked at her until the instruction became a request rather than an order. "I will use my refusal authority."

Lessa gave Pell a packet of willow bark and salt. "For balance sickness. Do not let Maela stand after she says she can. Humans lie about dizziness because their pride lacks a useful organ."

"Vennik never do that."

"Of course not."

Above them, Osra's hammers struck the upper bronze doors. The defenders had braced those doors with rubble and the abandoned coronation stool. Each impact traveled through the road.

Pell released the brake. Their expedition descended.

The first six spans followed Crown Hinge's inner curve. Ancient lamps hung empty in wall niches. Mineral growth covered the counterweight teeth, forcing riggers to clean each stop before trusting it. By the ninth span, the gallery roof fell away. Only the causeway remained, a carved ledge along dark stone with the lower firmament visible from both sides.

They tied themselves in pairs.

Pell paired with Maela because neither commented on the choice. Their rope measured twelve paces. Long enough to permit work, short enough that one fall would make the other person's decision immediate.

"This resembles our courtship," Pell said while checking Maela's knot.

"I remember fewer basalt blocks."

"You attended different evenings."

Sevrin smiled without turning. The humor lasted only until they reached the broken span.

Thirty paces of causeway had collapsed. A lower maintenance rib crossed beneath the gap, narrow as a roof beam and canted toward the stars. Anchor sockets survived on both sides. The old route had used a suspended counterweight bridge, but its timbers and ropes had vanished.

Pell inventoried what they possessed. River hawser could cross the gap twice. Bronze pins could anchor the lines. Sledge runners could become bridge rails if she dismantled one frame. Doing so would leave only one sledge for the demonstration and force them to abandon half the supplies on return.

"We need two frames to balance the bearing station," a rigger said.

"We need one frame to reach it," Pell replied.

Maela studied the lower rib. "Can people cross first and rebuild from the far side?"

"People, yes. The weights, no."

Veka pressed her tusk to the near anchor. "The rib carries. It dislikes the center."

"How specifically?"

"Enough to object. Not enough to vote."

Pell sent the lightest rigger across on hands and knees with a guide line. Every movement produced a faint note through the stone. Sevrin monitored pollen drift, which angled toward the Deep Hart when the rigger reached the rib's center. Maela recorded the change by touch marks cut into a wax slate. No observation remained private.

They built a cradle under the surviving sledge. Two hawsers took its weight from above while the lower rib guided its runners. Everyone except Pell and Veka crossed first. Pell controlled the forward brake; Veka called stone stress. The sledge hung over the buried sky.

Halfway across, one ancient anchor rotated in its socket.

"Stop," Veka said.

Pell stopped breathing with the ropes. The sledge swayed beneath the gap. Basalt blocks knocked against one another.

A Mahr soldier on the far side drove a fresh pin into a secondary socket. Maela redirected the guide line without waiting for rank to organize the hands around her. Sevrin called the pollen angle. When the second line took strain, the old anchor tore free and fell through amber cloud.

The new pin held.

They brought the sledge up on the far side. Pell sat against it until her inner ears agreed that stone beneath her still counted as ground. She ate willow bark before Lessa's warning could become relevant.

The causeway bent away from Crown Hinge after the gap. No handrail survived there. Old rope holes marked the inner wall, but mineral frost had widened several until Pell could fit her wrist inside. She rejected them and spent one bronze pin per twelve paces. At that rate, the supply would not reach the bearing station.

A buried weather front solved nothing.

Silver cloud climbed from the blue constellation below and struck the hinge root without wind. Light moved through stone. Every loose metal fitting began to hum. The forward sledge tried to slide sideways across a level road as celestial pressure pulled at its basalt load.

"Down!" Veka shouted.

The party clipped harnesses to the new pins. Pell threw both sledge brakes and watched the first pin bow outward. A soldier caught the rear line around his waist before it tore free. Sevrin's pollen frames brightened inside their wrappings.

"Which direction?" Pell called.

His Aeli assistant opened one cover by a finger. "Toward the road's outer edge. Fast."

"Move living weight inward. Blocks outward."

People crawled against the hinge wall. Riggers shifted two basalt blocks to the exposed runner. The cloud's pull took the stone rather than the frame, forcing that runner down. Pell hated using loss as ballast, but a block could fall without leaving a family on either side of the war.

Pressure increased. One outer strap snapped. The basalt blocks slid together, struck the runner, and cracked it.

Pell cut both lashings.

The blocks left the causeway. Their release slammed the sledge inward, trapping her boot between frame and wall. Maela braced the frame while a Vennik rigger worked Pell free. Pain flashed up her ankle. She could move every toe. The test mattered because later she would have to stand on it.

Silver cloud streamed past for six hundred breaths. Sevrin counted by touching knots along a cord. Veka reported the stone pitch at each hundred. Maela recorded which anchors bent and which held. Even sheltering became evidence if witnesses refused to call it mere endurance.

When the pressure passed, three bronze pins had failed and one sledge runner required splinting. They possessed too little hardware for the station brakes unless they recovered material. Pell sent teams back to the broken span, where the abandoned ancient anchor had left a bronze collar in the rock. It took half a day to cut free. From that collar they forged six rough pins on a charcoal pan, hammering them against the sledge bed.

The delay consumed water. Maela reduced every person's ration equally until Veka objected that larger Mahr bodies doing loaded work required more. Pell revised the shares by exertion instead of ancestry or rank. Sevrin surrendered none of his own portion, explaining that pollen judgment degraded with dehydration before thirst felt severe. Expertise included naming when sacrifice became sabotage.

By night they had repaired the runner and replaced the failed anchors. Pell marked the silver front on the cost map, including two lost blocks, one strained ankle, reduced water, and six hundred breaths during which the road could not be crossed. A future bearing plan would need weather intervals, not merely degrees.

They slept clipped to the inner wall. Buried stars moved below their eyelids even after every lamp went dark.

On Day 44, they reached the first lower bearing station.

Four stone arches met around an open circle. Each arch faced a different hinge direction. Channels in the floor held fossil pollen, bits of old counterweight chain, Mahr listening cups, and human witness marks showing river heights. The sky lay below the circle through a shaft without a bottom Pell could see. Along its rim, twelve broad divisions each contained twelve smaller cuts.

"Not for a full exchange," Maela said.

"No," Pell answered. "For stopping."

The station joined Crown Hinge to a return bearing deep in the root. Two counterweight beds faced each other. Moving the loaded sledge into the forward bed would relieve pressure from Crown's west shoulder. Shifting the empty frame into the return bed could pull that pressure back. The geometry matched the scars under the lower court, scaled across a much larger arc.

A royal mason had smashed the return-bed brake. Pell found its bronze teeth scattered in a drainage channel. She and the riggers rebuilt six teeth, enough to cover one measure and reverse it. Veka listened at every anchor. Sevrin placed one pollen frame toward the lower sky and one toward Crown Hinge. Maela read aloud the witness limits agreed below: halt for a sharp rise in either stone pitch, pollen reversal beyond one division, water loss from either cask greater than one hand, or balance sickness affecting two people.

"We have no coast height," Sevrin said.

"No Rootglass hive response," Maela added.

"No Ember or Salt teams in position," Veka said.

Pell looked at the rebuilt brake. "Therefore one measure out, one measure back. No farther."

They spent the rest of the day rehearsing release without a live load. Each person knew which rope to cut, which tooth to lock, and who could call a stop. Any one of the four labor groups could halt the test for immediate bodily danger. Resuming required all four. It took longer than a command from one mouth. That delay belonged to the structure, not to inefficiency.

During the night, buried starlight changed color. The Deep Hart faded behind a current of silver cloud. Cold moved upward through the shaft and glazed the brake ropes. Pell woke every hour to inspect them. Sevrin remained beside the pollen frames, unable to read their glow without assistance. He asked each waking guard for a description and recorded differences in raised marks he could later follow.

Near dawn, Maela said, "You have not slept."

"Neither have you."

"I am not controlling the brake."

"No. You merely represent fifty-five thousand eight hundred trapped people and several regions threatened by our mistakes. Restful work."

Maela sat beside her. Silence gathered without turning comfortable.

"Jorren disliked memorial speeches," Maela said.

"He requested omission from the glorious column."

"Did you promise?"

"I told him no such column exists."

Maela rubbed a thumb over the chalk arrow on her left sleeve. "Good."

Pell wanted to take her hand. The rope between them, coiled nearby, already represented as much trust as either could responsibly carry. She offered willow bark instead. Maela took it.

They began at first buried dawn.

Veka and the second Mahr listener occupied opposite arches. Sevrin's frames stood in the pollen channels with one Aeli assistant describing color and movement. Maela took the river-city witness mark. Pell and three Vennik riggers staffed the brakes. Soldiers waited beyond the cut lines with axes and wet cloth.

"Forward load to first tooth," Pell said.

The sledge moved into the bed. Crown Hinge's low note entered the station through the western arch.

"Stone holds," Veka reported.

Pollen rose from the lower frame in a pale spiral. Sevrin listened to the assistant's description and placed two fingers on the raised scale.

"Drift within first division."

Maela watched water tremble in the casks. "No loss."

"Second tooth."

Riggers eased the sledge deeper. The station tilted no more than a ship crossing another vessel's wake. Far above, beyond miles of stone, Crown Hinge began to accept the changed load.

At the third tooth, Pell felt pressure close around her ears. One rigger staggered. She called hold before he fell.

The Mahr listeners compared notes. Pollen drift remained inside the agreed division. Maela ordered the dizzy rigger replaced and recorded the bodily cost. He protested until Pell pointed at the written limits. Rules became meaningless if pride could overrule them.

They advanced again.

At the sixth tooth, a sound passed through the lower vault. Not thunder. Every visible point of the Deep Hart shifted by the width of a fingernail. Amber cloud streamed sideways. The forward pollen frame opened into light.

"One degree," Sevrin said.

Crown Hinge had moved.

Pell locked the brake. Nobody cheered. The station groaned through all four arches, and hair lifted on Maela's arms. Water climbed the inner side of both casks against weight.

"Lower note rising," Veka warned.

"Pollen at limit," Sevrin said.

Maela stared toward the causeway. "The road is turning."

It had not moved. Pell saw that at once. Yet Maela stepped as if the floor had rotated beneath her. She reached for the west arch and caught empty air.

Pell crossed the witness line, seized her harness, and lowered her to the stone. "Eyes closed. Name my hands."

"Your left at my shoulder. Right on the buckle."

"Which way lies Crown?"

Maela pointed toward the lower sky.

Temporary disorientation counted as one person affected, not two, but the water had violated its limit and Veka's note continued to rise. Three stop conditions.

"Reverse," Maela said from the floor.

Pell confirmed each labor. Veka: reverse. Sevrin: reverse. Maela pressed her palm to the river-city mark: reverse. Pell released the return brake.

The empty frame moved into its bed while crews hauled the loaded sledge back tooth by tooth. The first pull achieved nothing. Pressure held the forward runners against bronze.

"Increase return load," Pell ordered.

Soldiers transferred two basalt blocks to the empty frame. That reduced the hands available on the rope. Pell took a position with them. Her right hand slipped once; she caught the line against her wrist and leaned with her full weight.

The sledge came back one tooth.

Crown Hinge answered from above. The star points shifted toward their former marks.

At tooth four, frost broke from the ropes in sharp flakes. A brake pin bent. One rigger drove Pell's tally punch through the secondary eye, sacrificing the tool to hold the load.

"Pollen returning," Sevrin called.

"Stone pitch falling," Veka answered.

Maela remained seated with her eyes closed, speaking every witness observation onto the wax record. Her voice stayed steady even when she named her own failure to orient.

Tooth two. Tooth one.

The loaded sledge returned to its start bed. Pell locked both lines. Across the lower sky, the Deep Hart's points settled on their first chalk alignments.

Sevrin's assistant inspected the frames. "Forward pollen folded back. Lower frame matches the starting angle."

Veka listened along both arches. "Paired notes restored."

Water dropped to level inside the casks. Some had spilled, less than two fingers. Pell measured it rather than deciding the amount looked small.

"Reversed," she said.

The one-degree movement had gone out and returned. The station still stood. Their bodies carried the cost: one rigger vomiting beside the arch, Maela unable to point toward Crown for the next hour, Pell's ears ringing, Veka bleeding lightly where stone-song had split the skin around her uncapped tusk.
