Safe did not mean harmless. It meant limits had been stated, witnessed, and obeyed before harm became catastrophe.

By midday Maela could follow the chalk arrows correctly. Pell made her repeat the route six times before allowing her to stand without a guide rope. The disorientation eased, but its warning remained in every record.

They copied measurements onto wax, cloth, and skin. Pell scratched the six brake ratios along her forearm in case water destroyed the other copies. Sevrin sealed lower pollen in a brass tube for Irel. Veka carried paired pitch intervals in a Mahr cadence she repeated at every stop.

The return crossing took a day because the broken span had worsened. They abandoned one sledge frame and all but one basalt block. Soldiers carried Maela across the narrow rib on a safety line despite her objections. No one used the successful test to excuse carelessness.

Osra's troops had entered the upper lower-court stair when the expedition reached Crown Hinge. Kharza's defenders held them behind a wall of broken benches. Refugees crowded the western refuge, ready to retreat farther if the court failed.

Pell handed Maela's wax record to the civilian seats. Sevrin displayed the pollen tube. Veka sang the outward pitch and its returned answer. Pell laid her scratched forearm beside the cost map.

"One degree moved," she said. "One degree reversed. We know the required load, the brake sequence, the stone limits, the pollen limit, and the first bodily costs. We do not know what happens at Rootglass or along Neris unless their witnesses and labor join us."

A shelter delegate read the figures twice. "What do we call a plan that stops before it takes too much?"

Pell looked at the six brake teeth, each one a decision rather than a failure to complete the old command.

"A staged Turning," she said.

For the first time, the phrase named measured proof instead of hope. Runners carried it upward through the refuge with the limits attached.

Chapter 23 — Where the Star-Bees Returned

Sevrin found the first dead orchard by smell.

Rootglass trees had once given off a mineral sweetness before dawn, sap rising through translucent roots while star-bees warmed inside clay hives. Now rot lay under the cold air. Branches clattered without leaves. Salt from the displaced marsh tides crusted the irrigation cuts three days' journey inland.

He stood at the edge of the terrace and waited for someone else to describe the light.

"Cloud shade from the north," said Evi, the Aeli apprentice who had crossed the buried road with him. "Upper stars faint. Rootglass under the soil has a green seam, but it breaks near the lower channel."

Sevrin touched the brass tube inside his coat. Buried-sky pollen clicked softly against its seal.

"Be precise about the break."

Evi counted tree rows and named the direction using orchard markers rather than constellations. Sevrin followed her account across the terrace. Before Salt Hinge, he would have read every influence directly through his leaf-filaments. The loss had removed speed, not responsibility. He asked twice as many questions and accepted that answers arrived through other people's senses.

Irel's guards met them at the ninth terrace. Green-glass plates covered their shoulders. Half were Aeli gardeners; the rest included human orchard tenants and Vennik irrigation workers whose livelihoods belonged to Rootglass without granting them seats on its old council. Bows remained lowered but strung.

"You brought six people and a tube," Captain Ruunel said. "Osra's riders brought six hundred. Which threat should concern us first?"

"The one already in your roots," Sevrin answered.

Ruunel looked at the smooth scars on his temples. Pity entered her face before discipline removed it. "Irel will hear you. She promises nothing."

They walked beneath branch nets toward the surviving inner orchard. Star-bees moved nowhere. Empty clay hives hung among trees whose leaves had turned the gray of old metal. Workers painted root wounds with lime. Children carried water in paired cups because single heavy buckets cracked the glassy soil.

At the council house, Irel Saan had removed every carved shutter from its windows. Nothing obstructed sight lines to the terraces. She sat at a long workbench with the queen-hive under her left hand.

The hive resembled a wide clay jar wrapped in root fiber. Warmth pulsed through its wall. Irel had hidden it through the Sunken Observatory fighting and carried it back after Salt Hinge moved. Her leaf-filaments spread silver around her head, healthy enough to catch the faint upper pattern. She did not rise for Sevrin.

"You have brought lower pollen into my orchard," she said.

"Sealed."

"Pressure does not respect brass indefinitely."

"Neither does a dying hive."

Irel's fingers tightened on the root-fiber wrap. "You lost the right to diagnose what I protected."

Sevrin accepted the blow. "I did."

The council expected argument. He gave them the figures instead.

Three years earlier, Neris shipping orchards had lost eight queen lines under the fixed sky. Rootglass had lost twenty-three. Sevrin had combined the totals in the guild report and blamed salt mites, fearing that a regional split would cost both enclaves Crown support. Neris obtained shade cloth and new comb. Rootglass received inspection teams that found fault with local pruning.

"I concealed the unequal loss," he said. "My silence helped turn your damage into incompetence."

A gardener across the table cursed him. Another asked how many Rootglass children had missed leafing because relief went to Neris. Sevrin did not know. He said so.

Irel watched him without satisfaction. "Why tell us now?"

"Because any plan requiring your trust must begin with the reason you should withhold it."

He placed copies of the lower-station measurements on the bench. Evi described the proof while Sevrin answered technical questions. One degree outward. One degree reversed. Pollen response measured on both paths. Water displacement recorded. Bodily injuries named. Limits that had stopped the test before pressure rose farther.

"A staged Turning," Irel said, tasting the new phrase as if it might contain poison.

"A bounded possibility. Not a promise that Rootglass survives."

"Osra promises it survives if no one turns anything."

"Your dead western terraces give her answer."

Irel carried the queen-hive to a window. "And the Neris flood map gives yours. Movement kills somewhere."

"Then both places must witness each stage and stop it when the mapped cost crosses the agreed line."

"You ask me to place the last viable queen under a court assembled during siege."

"No. I ask you to test what she chooses."

Sevrin set the brass tube beside the hive.

The council moved to the migration terrace at noon. Irel chose the site: a narrow corridor between living inner trees and a western block written off after salt burn. Irrigation crews opened two channels, one carrying clean spring water and one carrying the brackish pulse that had followed Salt Hinge's movement. Children and nonessential workers left the area. Guard teams took roofs and root trenches. Osra's riders still camped beyond the northern ridge under a truce negotiated when Irel had supported fixed custody.

Evi laid white cloth over the rootglass. Any pollen movement would show as color. Irel placed three empty worker combs along the corridor. Sevrin opened his instrument cases without touching the queen-hive.

"You release her," he said.

"You interpret the lower pollen," Irel replied.

Their opposed skills had caused years of conflict. Irel could read the hive's immediate health through upper-star leafing. Sevrin understood paired migration and coastal weather. Neither could perform the other's labor now, and that incapacity protected the test from private certainty.

Irel unwrapped the hive.

Worker bees emerged first, ordinary-sized and dark except for flecks of pale dust along their legs. They circled the mouth but refused the upper-pollen comb. The queen remained inside. A low agitation moved through the jar.

Sevrin broke the brass seal.

Buried-sky pollen held no brilliance he could see. Evi described it as amber with a blue edge, the colors of the Deep Hart cloud and the unknown river constellation beneath Crown Hinge. Sevrin used a bone spoon to place one measured grain on each of two western combs.

The worker bees stopped circling.

One landed on the first grain. Its abdomen lowered. Others followed until the cloth seemed to gather a small dark tide. They carried the pollen toward the hive, touched antennae at its mouth, and returned to the corridor.

Inside the clay jar, the queen moved.

Irel inhaled sharply. Her leaf-filaments lifted despite the still air. "She reads the lower pair."

"Does she accept it?"

"Wait."

The queen emerged into noon.

She had not grown monstrous or divine. She remained a thumb-length insect with a heavy abdomen and one damaged wing. Workers supported her across the root-fiber lip. At the first western comb, she touched buried-sky pollen and began the slow abdomen movement that marked a chosen lay site.

Evi laughed once, then covered her mouth.

Irel did not laugh. Tears stood along her lower lids. "The corridor reaches her."

A horn sounded from the northern ridge.

Green-glass guards turned toward the council house. Two signal flags rose above the trees: Osra's white grain sheaf and Irel's old truce mark. Captain Ruunel watched through a tube.

"Envoy party," she said. "Thirty riders. Two wagons."

"Too many for an envoy," Irel replied.

Another horn answered from the western terraces. Smoke lifted behind the dead orchard.

Osra's agents had waited for the hive to open.

The first arrows carried clay bulbs rather than iron heads. They broke among the outer trees and released oil. Riders came down the irrigation lanes while hidden teams ignited the western block, driving heat toward the migration terrace. Their objective did not require holding Rootglass. Fire could kill the exposed queen and destroy evidence that threatened Osra's fixed-sky claim.

Irel closed the queen-hive wrap. "Inner vault."

"Smoke follows the channel," Sevrin said. "Move her east and you cross the upper-pollen rows. She may reject the comb and leave the workers exposed."

"Fire comes from west."

"Which means they expect east."

Ruunel confirmed riders moving behind the council house.

Irel looked at the brackish channel, the living inner trees, and the lower-pollen comb where the queen remained. "Down through the root drains."

"Water will take her."

"Not if you reverse the clean channel."

Sevrin understood. Rootglass irrigation operated through paired cups cut into the mineral soil. Opening one lowered pressure in the other. If crews reversed the spring gate, flow would pull smoke west while exposing a dry maintenance tunnel beneath the migration terrace.

"Your roots will take salt," he said.

"Western block already has. Name the cost and move."

Irel assigned guards to delay the riders without pursuing. Sevrin ran with irrigation workers to the spring gate. He could not read the blue sheen that showed pressure through rootglass, so Evi called the line as he turned the wheel.

"Two marks. Three. Hold."

Smoke flattened, then moved toward the western channel. Salt water surged into the sacrificed block. Living trees along its edge shed leaves in a single gray wave.

At the terrace, workers lowered the queen-hive into the maintenance tunnel on a sling. Irel descended beside it. Sevrin followed with the buried-sky pollen and both frames. Above them, arrows struck rootglass with bell-like cracks.

The tunnel offered crawling height. Clay pipes crossed between tree roots that glowed faintly through stone. Workers passed the hive hand to hand. No single person carried it far enough to claim custody through rescue.

A blast closed the passage behind them. Oil had reached a dry root pocket. Heat chased air through the tunnel. Bees poured from the hive mouth and gathered on its cool underside.

"Forward fork blocked," a worker called.

Two routes remained. The north drain led under the riders. The south line emerged in a nursery where two hundred young trees grew under shade cloth.

Irel touched the soil. Her filaments leaned north. "Upper-star pressure opens the rider line."

Sevrin lifted a pollen frame. Evi, coughing beside him, described grains pulling south.

"The lower pair opens toward the nursery," he said.

"Fire follows us there."

"Only if the clean channel continues west. Close it after we pass the branch. Flood the tunnel behind us."

"That sends brackish water into the nursery."

"For less than one hundred breaths."

Irel made the decision. "South. Ruunel, tell the gate crews: close on our third rope tug, reopen at one hundred."

The line moved. Sevrin crawled ahead, counting branch joints by touch. Behind him, Irel kept one hand on the queen-hive and one on living roots. Their knowledge pulled in different directions and created a route neither would have chosen alone.

At the third rope tug, water struck the tunnel.

It came cold and salted, filling the passage to Sevrin's elbows. Burning oil hissed behind them. Workers lifted the hive over their heads. Bees covered their wrists without stinging. Sevrin lost his pollen tube, caught it against a pipe with his forearm, and shoved it into Irel's free hand.

The south grate appeared as a pale square. Guards broke it outward. The party emerged beneath nursery shade cloth while brackish water spread ankle-deep among young roots.

"Count," Irel ordered.

Sevrin counted one hundred breaths. At eighty, several saplings darkened. At ninety-four, the flow slowed. At one hundred, clean water returned from the spring gate and pushed the salt back.

The nursery lived. Some leaves would fall. The cost remained visible.

Battle continued above for less than an hour.

Ruunel had divided the inner orchard into irrigation squares before the riders reached it. Each square possessed two gates and one raised glass walk. Her defenders withdrew along those walks, forcing mounted troops into channels too narrow to turn. Human tenants used pruning hooks to pull riders from saddles. Vennik water crews opened gates behind them, leaving horses knee-deep in mud without drowning either animals or soldiers.

Osra's agents still held the council house and the northern pump. Their oil wagons waited on high ground. If they reached the spring wheel, they could send fire along the clean channel and burn every inner terrace at once.

Irel saw the threat through her leaf-filaments. Upper starlight reflected from oil skins beyond the shade cloth. Sevrin could not see that pattern. He asked Evi for the rider count, Ruunel for the available routes, and the irrigation workers for channel capacity.

"Northern pump has a stone overflow," one worker said. "Open it and the terrace floods."

"Including the nursery," Irel replied.

"Not if we use the old root cistern," Sevrin said. "The fixed-sky repairs sealed its western mouth, but lower pollen pulled toward it in the tunnel."

Irel understood the implication. "You want to open a buried channel under armed riders."

"I want their horses standing on a roof instead of ground."

The root cistern had not carried water for forty years. Its glass cover might collapse under pressure and kill everyone above. Irel sent a warning horn before she accepted the risk. Ruunel's guards withdrew from the northern pump in deliberate disorder, inviting pursuit. The riders took it.

Sevrin and three workers crawled to the sealed western mouth beneath the nursery. Old mineral mortar filled the gate. He found its edges by touch while Evi described the pollen glow. They drove pruning wedges into the seam. On the terrace overhead, hooves struck glass.

"Again," Sevrin said.

The workers swung mallets. Mortar cracked. Brackish water from the temporary flood pushed against the far side.

Irel called through the speaking pipe. "Oil team moving south. Twenty breaths."

The third blow freed the gate.

Water entered the root cistern and lifted decades of silt. The roof did not collapse at once. Its panels flexed, turning the riders' footing into a wavering mirror. Horses shied. Ruunel's archers shot oil jars rather than bodies, spilling fuel into water before the fire teams could light it.

One agent reached the spring wheel with a torch. Irel crossed the glass walk alone, silver filaments streaming behind her. She carried no bow. Instead she cut the shade-cloth release. A weighted sheet dropped between the torch and the channel, smothering its flame. The agent struck her with the handle. Irel fell against the wheel but held the cloth rope until guards arrived.

At the cistern mouth, the roof began to split.

"Out," Sevrin ordered.

A young worker stayed to close the gate. Continued flooding would undermine the living terrace. Sevrin went back, seized the worker's belt, and pulled her clear as the first glass panel collapsed. Water and two riderless horses fell into the cistern. The remaining troops scattered toward the northern ridge.

Ruunel could have closed the retreat. She left one irrigation square dry and announced it by horn. Soldiers who dropped fire pots received passage. Most did. A Heartland officer tried to ignite the nursery while withdrawing; an Aeli guard from Irel's own faction shot the torch from his hand and then accepted his surrender.

The fighting ended without pursuit. Rootglass needed every able body for fire lines and injured trees. Human orchard tenants captured two fire teams. One team consisted of Heartland soldiers under written orders from Tavan's deputy; another included Aeli workers whose families depended on Osra's grain guarantee. Irel forbade revenge killings. She took their testimony and locked the oil wagons under mixed guard.

Afterward, the cost map gained another square: northern root cistern broken open, two horses injured but alive, one glass walk destroyed, Irel bruised across the shoulder, one young worker pulled from collapse. The tactical success had preserved the spring wheel by sacrificing old storage and another piece of orchard floor.

When the smoke thinned, the western block had burned to black ribs. Eleven guards and workers were dead. Thirty-two carried wounds. The queen-hive remained warm.

At dusk, Irel reopened it in the nursery. Workers flew first to the buried-pollen comb. The queen followed and settled beneath a young tree whose roots still held a faint amber trace.

"Buried-sky pollen," Irel told the assembled council. "Accepted by the surviving queen. The corridor can function under a bounded bearing. It also costs one western block and salt injury to this nursery under today's attack. Both enter the record."

Sevrin gave the council his concealed Neris figures, signed without guild privilege. Irel added written safeguards: physical custody remained with Rootglass until a public bearing court ratified shared terms; access required Irel plus an Aeli witness from outside her faction; any pollen release required published migration and water maps; Rootglass could call a pause with one other delegation, never alone.

"You will not hold the hinge alone," Sevrin said.

"Neither will you."

"Agreed."

Irel pressed her seal into the document. Sevrin signed only as witness, not starwarden. Ruunel added the orchard guard. A Vennik irrigator and a human tenant signed for workers the old council had excluded.

The queen-hive stayed beneath Irel's hand. Its future access entered shared custody through the safeguards, pending the court that would bind every delegation. Rootglass Hinge opened its lower galleries to mixed teams. Green-glass guards packed for Vael.

Before dawn, Irel gave Sevrin one living comb wrapped in nursery cloth. "You carry evidence. I carry the queen."

"And if either of us fails?"

"The other tells the truth about the cost."

They left the burned terraces together, not reconciled, not subordinate, and carrying the fourth labor toward war.

Chapter 24 — The Bridge of Four Banners

Kharza put her good tusk against the relief bridge and heard three hundred civilians trying not to breathe.

They lay below the deck on evacuation barges, hidden beneath wet reed mats while Osra's archers watched both banks. Children had cloth tied over their mouths. Wounded adults held the barges against the piers with padded hooks. The old bridge carried their shelter through its stone footings: a quick, frightened vibration beneath the heavier song of armed feet.

Above, the west half belonged to Kharza's federation. The east half belonged to Aruk Venn.

Between them rose a broken lifting span chained in the open position. Vael Tern stood beyond Aruk's Reed lines, outer districts smoking behind the breached wall. Maela's blue-river signals waited at the inner quay. To the north, green-glass flashes marked Irel's orchard guard approaching along the flood embankment.

Four banners had been assigned to the plan. Only one had reached the bridge.

Kharza lifted her tusk from the parapet. The leather around her cracked left side tasted of blood each time she breathed. Lessa had packed the split with resin and wrapped the jaw again. Singing through it could drive the crack to the root. She would listen through the right and trust other fault-readers with the rest.

"Load report," she said.

Veka stood at the first pier with two younger Mahr. "Western arch holds. Center pier takes uneven pressure from the raised span. Barges touching underneath add little weight, but their hooks pull against the current."

Pell's bridge crews had fixed counterweight baskets to the lifting chains. If they lowered the span, Kharza's fighters could cross. If Osra's engineers released their upstream boom, timber and fire barges would strike the hidden evacuees before the span settled.

"Enemy?" Kharza asked.

A Kiln scout pointed. "Four thousand Reed began this war under Aruk. Losses and detachments leave near three thousand here. Heartland archers behind them. Incendiary crews at the upper quay."

Aruk's silver-cap banner hung over the eastern tower. Its polished circles flashed whenever smoke opened. He had promised to meet Kharza at the bridge. Neither of them had promised what meeting would produce.

Kharza called her commanders beneath the black-clay banner. Kiln House speakers, imperial defectors, and the Reed splinter captain stood in a half circle. She repeated the objective: lower the span, open a relief route, protect the barges, do not pursue a retreat beyond the eastern tower.

"Any commander may refuse an order that loads the bridge beyond Veka's mark or exposes the barges to fire," she said. "Name the reason and offer another action if time permits."

The imperial captain frowned toward Aruk's tower. "If Reed troops break, we should finish them."

"No. We need their river knowledge and we need the route. Revenge offers neither."

"They drowned our west camp."

"Enter that cost after the battle. Do not add a city to it."

Pell raised a red flag from the counterweight station. Ready.

Kharza gave the advance.

Ceramic shields moved onto the deck in overlapping rows. Heartland arrows struck black clay and burst into chips. Imperial pikes followed at half length because the bridge allowed no full formation. Reed splinter fighters climbed below the parapets, using repair ledges their cousins across the river knew equally well.

Aruk did not contest the first thirty paces. He let Kharza's line enter the narrow center, then opened sluice gates in the eastern tower.

River water surged through channels cut beneath the deck. The old bridge rolled sideways by a hand. Fighters lost footing. One counterweight basket swung into its frame and cracked a timber upright.

"Hold shields!" Kharza shouted.

Veka called from the pier. "Center pressure rising. Two notes."

Aruk had turned the bridge itself into a weapon without striking the civilian barges. Kharza recognized the restraint and the danger. His people understood marsh crossings as load and current, not merely ground.

She signaled the Reed splinter captain.

The splinter fighters dropped from repair ledges onto the west sluice wheel. Aruk's defenders met them below the deck. Kinship produced no hesitation. They fought over handles in water to the waist, one group protecting Osra's line because autonomy had been promised, the other attacking because fire orders had broken their confederacy's conditions.

Pell's crews released the first basket. Stone descended; the lifting span lowered a fraction. Enemy engineers cut one secondary chain. It whipped across the deck, killing two imperial pikers and tearing a shield from Kharza's hand.

The raised span stopped halfway.

Its western hinge pin had twisted under the cut chain. Pell waved two yellow flags from the counterweight station: mechanical obstruction, ten minutes if the deck stayed clear. Ten minutes on an exposed bridge could consume a company.

Kharza ordered the Kiln line into the bridge alcoves. Imperial pikes angled over them, points covering the narrow center without adding feet to it. The maneuver yielded ground Aruk could have taken. He did not advance. Instead his front ranks locked shields at the east rise and waited for the bridge to choose a side.

Below, one evacuation barge drifted out of alignment.

A padded hook had slipped from the north pier. Current turned the bow across the arch. If it rotated farther, the barge would emerge into Heartland bow range. Nim climbed onto its roof and tried to reset the hook, but the line fell short by two arm lengths.

Kharza pointed to the Reed splinter captain. "Can your swimmers reach it?"

"They can. The center pressure sings above Veka's limit. Putting people in the water adds no deck load."

"Do it."

The captain refused. "Archers have the angle. Give us shield cover from the upper ledge first."

Kharza swallowed the instant command that rose in her. The reason held. She redirected six ceramic shields to the north parapet. That weakened her approach line and opened a gap Imperial soldiers had to cover. Only after the shields overlapped did four Reed swimmers enter the current.

Aruk saw them.

He raised one hand. His archers lowered bows along the water angle. Heartland archers behind them did not. Their first volley struck the bridge piers and one swimmer's upper arm. She rolled, surfaced, and kept hold of the line between her teeth.

Kharza sent slingers against the Heartland position. Aruk's officers protested when stones passed over Reed shields. He moved his front rank half a pace east, widening the target. The act protected civilians without joining Kharza. Every allegiance still carried edges.

Swimmers reached the barge. Nim threw down a tow line. Two took it around the north pier while another cut the tangled hook. The wounded swimmer braced against the hull, blood dispersing in the current.

On deck, Pell's crews crawled beneath the lifting chain. They carried wedges instead of weapons. A Heartland skirmisher climbed over the eastern parapet and ran toward them. An imperial piker stepped from Kharza's line to intercept, placing his weight on the raised span.

"Back!" Veka shouted.

The piker ignored her. He killed the skirmisher and took another step.

Stone cracked below.

Kharza reached him, caught the rear of his cuirass, and dragged him off the center boards as a seam opened where his foot had landed. "When the fault-reader refuses your weight, she commands the ground beneath you."

"He would have killed the crews."

"Then throw your spear next time."

The soldier looked toward Veka and offered a stiff apology. She pointed him to the south alcove without accepting or rejecting it.

Under the span, riggers hammered the bent pin free. The chain dropped half a link and trapped one worker's sleeve. Pell cut cloth away with her teeth and passed in a smaller wedge. Her injured shoulder kept her from swinging a mallet overhead, so a human wall mason struck while she called angle changes. Vennik expertise directed; another body's strength supplied what injury denied.

The barge straightened below. Civilians hauled the swimmers onto its roof. The wounded orc pressed a wet mat around her own arm and took the hook line again.

At the east tower, Osra's officer ordered another volley. Aruk stepped in front of his archers. Kharza could not hear the words across the bridge, but she saw the argument travel through Reed ranks. Some bows stayed down. Others rose toward her line.

Pell's first yellow flag vanished. One remained.

A siege stone came from the north terrace without warning. It struck the western approach behind Kharza's main formation. Ceramic shields, bodies, and paving lifted together. The blast did not break the bridge, but retreat compressed toward the deck.

Kharza turned and shouted, "Hold west of the load mark!"

Fear drove soldiers forward anyway. The imperial captain planted his pikes across the approach, barring his own allies from safety. Kiln speakers opened side lanes into the river warehouses. People moved off the bridge rather than onto it. Distributed commands prevented a crush that Kharza could not have stopped from the center.

Smoke revealed the casualty hollow. Lessa crawled into it with medics while stones still fell. Kharza wanted to go back. Her job held at the center until the pin cleared. She remained, hating the discipline she had once imposed on others and now imposed on herself.

Pell raised a green flag.

The twisted pin came free. Counterweight crews took up the chain one careful hand at a time. The span lowered another pace, enough that a fighter could climb its slope without adding a leap to every step.

Aruk advanced then.

Not with his whole line. He came alone for the first ten paces, making clear that the meeting belonged to two commanders rather than a rush against workers. His long marsh spear rested across one shoulder. Silver covered both tusks.

Kharza handed command of the western load mark to Veka. If Kharza fell, the bridge objective would continue without waiting for vengeance.

Kharza climbed onto its slanted boards. Arrows passed over the low edge and struck higher as she ascended. She used a dead soldier's shield until three shafts split it. Ahead, Aruk came down from the eastern tower with a long marsh spear and silver caps on both tusks.

They met where the lifting span peaked.

Aruk thrust for her injured side. Kharza turned enough that the spear grazed the leather wrap. Pain flashed white. She caught the shaft under her arm and drove her shoulder into his chest. He yielded one step, planted, and used the bridge's roll to throw her off balance.

Neither fought like the trade songs imagined orcs fought. No roaring, no test of brute strength. Aruk used reach and current timing. Kharza read board stress and weight transfer. Each tried to place the other where the bridge would decide.

"Osra has lost the city outer wall already," Kharza said between strikes. "What remains of her promise?"

"A marsh treaty with a seal," Aruk answered. "You offer councils that change when armies need roads."

"Then hold us to writing."

"After you cross over our bodies?"

He swept her ankle. Kharza fell to one knee. Her cracked tusk struck the shield rim. Resin broke inside the split. She tasted blood.

Aruk stopped the spear point one finger from her throat.

A white grain-sheaf signal rose from Osra's upper quay.

Three red lamps followed.

Aruk looked past Kharza. His soldiers on the eastern bank hesitated. The signal repeated: burn all craft beneath the bridge. No exception for evacuation marks.

Nim's hidden barges began to move. Wet reed mats slipped from one bow, exposing wounded civilians.

Kharza saw Aruk understand the order. "Your condition was no civilian burning."

"I know my condition."

"Then choose it."

He could have killed her first. The spear remained steady. Kharza did not reach for her knife. A decision made under her blade would make him subordinate to force, and he would never accept that place.

Upstream, Osra's incendiary crews pushed six tarred barges into the current. Fire teams waited with arrows. The floating boom opened.

Aruk lowered his spear.

He pulled the silver cap from his right tusk.

Bare mineral veining met the bridge's song. His face changed. Through cultural choice he had damped that resonance for decades, refusing upland claims that stone hearing defined Mahr worth. Removing one silver cap did not undo that choice. It let him hear this particular bridge.

The civilian vibration reached him first. Then came the center pier, cracked by the lifting strain. Last came the six fire barges striking current upstream.

"The bridge fails if they hit," Aruk said.

"Yes."

He turned toward his banner crew. "Reed condition invoked. Refuse incendiary order. Take the upper quay."

Half his nearest soldiers moved immediately. Others remained frozen between Osra's written treaty and their war speaker. A Heartland officer shouted that refusal constituted treason. Aruk struck the officer's sword aside without harming him.

"The Confederacy named its limit before we marched," Aruk called. "No civilian burning. Those who keep that law, with me. Those who choose Osra, withdraw from the bridge."

The Reed line split.

Some companies lowered spears and backed toward Vael. Two held position under their captains. Most turned on the incendiary crews. Silver-cap shields advanced along the upper quay while Heartland archers pivoted in confusion.

Kharza rose. "Your command remains yours."

"Keep it that way."

Aruk ran east to lead his people.

The fire barges entered bow range. Reed archers shot the tow ropes on the first pair. One grounded against the north bank and ignited harmless mud. Another rotated broadside, blocking the third. The remaining three came on.

Pell signaled that the lifting chain could not lower while the center pier carried combat. Kharza ordered her forward ranks back ten paces. An imperial captain refused, pointing to Aruk's exposed rear.

"Reason?" Kharza demanded.

"We can take the tower now."

"That violates the objective. Withdraw."

He held her gaze, then obeyed. Distributed refusal did not remove command. It made its reasons visible.

With the center load reduced, Pell released both counterweight baskets. The lifting span descended. Broken chain dragged across planks, throwing sparks. Veka and her fault-readers sang a low corrective note through the west pier. Kharza heard but did not join; her injured tusk had begun pulsing with every syllable she spoke.

The first fire barge reached the bridge.

Nim's evacuation crews cast padded hooks around its prow. Civilians below the deck hauled alongside Vennik riggers above, turning the burning hull away from the central pier. Flames crossed the wet reed mats. A child passed water buckets between two wounded adults. Nobody waited for rescue to become passive.

The barge struck the south arch. Stone held. Fire climbed its outer face.

A second tarred craft followed. Reed fighters on the quay dropped a sluice gate before it arrived. The diverted current caught the barge and drove it into Osra's own empty siege wharf.

The third came under the nearly lowered span.

Aruk reached the east wheel and released a flood pulse. Pell saw his intent. She raised one counterweight basket, lifting the span just enough for current to pass below. The fire barge shot through without touching the civilians and emerged downstream, where Nim's empty towboat guided it onto a gravel spit.

For one breath, the bridge belonged to water rather than either army.

Then Maela's blue-river banner appeared above the eastern tower.

Her sortie came through a tannery lane from Vael's inner districts. Defenders carried short shields suited to streets, not fields. Shelter workers rolled carts behind them with water and stretchers. They struck Heartland archers from the city side, opening space for Aruk's refusal companies at the quay.

Osra's loyal Reed captains could have trapped Maela against the tower. One withdrew instead. Another joined Aruk after seeing civilians under the bridge. The third attacked and lost the wheel room in close fighting. Their choices remained separate.

From the north embankment, green-glass armor flashed.

Irel's orchard guard arrived with Sevrin walking beside the living-comb cart. They did not charge the bridge. Aeli archers took the flood dike and shot the ropes of Osra's remaining oil wagons. Orchard tenants opened a drainage cut that flooded the approach behind Heartland reinforcements. The guard secured the north abutment, keeping the relief route from closing as soon as it opened.

Pell dropped a green flag.

The lifting span settled onto its stone rest.

Kharza's black-clay formation crossed first, shields outward rather than leveled against Aruk. His Reed silver-cap banner joined from the quay, still under his command. Rootglass green glass came down from the dike. Maela's blue river flew above the eastern tower.

Four banners stood on the same bridge.

No cheer could erase the bodies on its planks or the fire along the south arch. Kharza ordered lanes left open for healers and evacuation traffic. Pell's crews inspected every chain before allowing supply wagons across. Veka limited the bridge to one company or two wagons at a time. Each commander accepted the restriction.

Aruk returned with soot across his face and one tusk bare. He held the removed silver cap in his palm.

"My treaty with Osra ends on her breach of the no-burning condition," he said. "Reed forces join the bearing action under their own banner. We claim equal marsh authority, separate ration accounting, and one seat in every decision that changes salt or flood."

"Agreed as terms for emergency alliance," Kharza said. "Final terms require public court."

"And if the court refuses?"

"You keep your army and your refusal."

Aruk extended his bare right hand. Kharza clasped it. Neither knelt.

Behind them, relief wagons began crossing into Vael. Evacuation barges emerged from under the deck and moved toward the downstream quay. The route opened slowly, under a load limit, while Osra's fractured companies withdrew toward Crown Hinge.

Kharza looked at the four banners and felt no desire to place one above the rest. The bridge carried them because each remained distinct enough to bear its own weight.

Chapter 25 — The Bearing Court

Maela opened the bearing court in a warehouse whose roof had burned away.

Morning showed through black rafters. Rain fell on the center aisle and ran between benches made from barge planks. Beyond the missing east wall, the relief bridge carried food into Vael under Pell's load limits. Wounded soldiers occupied the dry loft. Prisoners from three armies received water beside them.

No palace door could close this room.

Maela stood at a chalked square rather than a dais. The broken royal seal remained on the council table across the city, beyond enemy lines. She wore the plain blue cloak of Crown Marshal and kept one hand on a witness rail polished by dock labor.

"This court has authority for the emergency bearing only," she said. "It cannot appoint a dynasty, settle every marsh border, or excuse any crime committed during the siege. It can decide whether and how we redistribute present sky pressure, and who may stop us when the cost exceeds what we have named."

People filled the warehouse and the riverbank outside. Mahr Kiln speakers sat apart from Reed delegates. Rootglass gardeners shared a bench with Neris sailors who expected flood from the same movement the orchards needed. Vennik bridge houses yielded front seats to evacuation workers. Vael shelter circles, Heartland deserters, coast towns, human river wards, and refugees sent witnesses. Caldrin's copied clauses hung in four scripts from the rafters.

Pell brought the cost map on sailcloth.

It covered the wet floor from wall to wall. Blue thread marked Neris water rise. White clay showed salt movement through Reedwater. Amber dust traced heat relief across Varkesh. Green glass marked the Rootglass migration corridor. Black counters represented Vael's remaining trapped population and the routes by which they might shelter during each bearing.

"Every benefit gets a loser," said a capped-tusk observer from Sul Venn. "Name them before anyone names victory."

Pell began with Varkesh. Ember pressure would ease if the lower Deep Hart moved farther into exchange, bringing steadier highland rain. Heartland grain warmth would weaken as the Cinder Net shifted. Families already living on reduced siege rations could lose a late harvest.

A Heartland farmer stood from the prisoner section. "How much?"

"First estimate: one part in twelve under the proposed arc," Pell said. "Confidence low because royal surveyors destroyed two field series."

"Then say low confidence in the law."

Nim Vask wrote it into the rule text.

Reedwater came next. Salt Hinge's present position threatened nursery pools if pressure rose without controlled drainage. Aruk named marsh channels that must open before the second action. A Reed fisher challenged him on three settlements omitted from his army map. Aruk added them publicly and assigned his own reserve barges to carry their young and nets.

Rootglass required lower pollen to keep the migration corridor alive. Neris required time to move harbor families above the third-stage flood line. Sevrin read the old concealed figures before anyone could use his expertise without its history. Irel read the orchard losses and the safeguards agreed at Rootglass. Neither asked forgiveness from the other.

Witnesses then tested the map against lives its symbols had compressed.

A Reed woman named Hessa carried a nursery basket to the chalk square. No child lay inside. White salt furred its woven base. She turned it over on the sailcloth beside Aruk's marsh channels.

"We moved eggs from South Mirr when the first hinge shifted," she said. "Your army map says the settlement can take another hand of water. It cannot. The reed beds uprooted. If salinity crosses Pell's fifth mark, there is no return route for spawning fish. Do not write crop loss. Write nursery loss, and write that recovery takes three summers if it comes."

Nim replaced the term. Aruk moved the pause line below Pell's fifth mark and assigned two additional barges. Hessa did not thank him. Her testimony had purchased less risk, not generosity.

A Heartland miller came next under prisoner guard. His left ear had burned away at Vael's breach. He placed a handful of weeviled grain on the Cinder Net sector.

"One part in twelve assumes our southern stores remain dry," he said. "They do not. Reed cavalry cut the mill road before they joined you. Osra's requisition took seed grain from three districts. Shift warmth at the wrong week and your estimate doubles."

"What warning shows that week?" Pell asked.

"Red mold under the husk. It appears two days before the heads bend."

The court added field inspections before the second action and reserved relief wagons from the recovered siege stores. Several Vael residents protested sending grain outward while inner districts remained hungry.

Maela called a shelter delegate rather than answering herself.

Edris of Lantern Ward stood with mortar dust still in his hair. "We have fifty-five thousand eight hundred people who did not fit through the cistern route," he said. "That fact does not turn every sack beyond our walls into ours. But no stage advances if Crown's lower refuge cracks or Osra drives civilians onto the hinge approach. We need shelter counts at every bell."

The map gained black counters at six refuge districts. Each counter required a human runner and a second witness from another people, preventing a military commander from reporting a threatened district empty for convenience.

From Neris, a Salt Aeli navigator named Seli brought tide cords knotted by harbor mothers. She challenged Sevrin's blue flood line because it treated stone quays and floating homes alike. Floating homes rose with water but broke when moorings reached full stretch. Stone-cellar residents drowned sooner, often without boats.

"Three different thresholds," Seli said. "One coast cannot be one line."

Sevrin redrew it as braided thread. Irel asked whether buried-sky pollen might change the current before height increased. Seli did not know. That uncertainty entered as a requirement for live reports rather than a guessed answer.

A Varkesh terrace worker objected to the phrase highland rain benefit. "Rain on cracked ash becomes a slide," he said. "We need slower water before we need more water. Open the old kiln channels before first action."

Kharza assigned Kiln companies to drainage instead of assault reserve. The decision reduced troops available at Crown Hinge. Nobody called the exchange free.

Last came a displaced family from the farms outside Vael. Osra had cleared their village for siege earthworks; coalition cavalry had later taken their surviving mules. The mother asked which delegation represented people whose field no longer belonged to any functioning region.

The court had no prepared seat.

Maela felt the old temptation to promise inclusion later, after urgency passed. That delay had built every locked door beneath Crown Hinge.

"Refugee witnesses choose one rotating cost recorder now," she said. "Not under the river-city delegation. Beside all four, with authority to challenge any map that treats displaced people as belonging to the land that expelled them."

The family selected an older human midwife from the riverbank crowd. A Mahr refugee and an Aeli tenant joined her as alternates. They received their own slate and space at the witness rail.

By the time testimony ended, the sailcloth had become harder to read and more accurate. New threads crossed old colors. Objects weighed its corners: salt basket, damaged grain, tide cords, a refugee's mule bell. The plan lost elegance with every person it admitted.

At midday, the court divided into four bearing delegations.

Kharza Olt and Aruk Venn represented Mahr labor, each retaining separate political authority. Sevrin Ael and Irel Saan represented Aeli pollen work and its contested regions. Pell and Nim Vask represented Vennik counterweight labor, bridges, and evacuation. Maela took the river-city place with one rotating delegate each from Vael, Neris, and the Tern Heartland.

A Salt Aeli navigator objected. "One coast voice rotates while two orchard keepers remain fixed."

"Then Neris chooses who rotates and may replace them between actions," Maela said. "The emergency structure will not become permanent by habit."

"Who controls the queen-hive?" asked a Rootglass tenant.

Irel set the wrapped clay hive on a four-handled cradle. "I retain care of the queen. Access now requires my hand, Sevrin or another public Aeli witness, a Vennik load record, and the river-city cost mark. No one carries her alone."

Sevrin placed his living comb beside it. Shared custody entered physical practice when Pell, Nim, a Neris witness, and a Rootglass worker sealed the cradle latches together.

The court turned to the plan.

Four actions would exchange twelve degrees across the hinges. Each action covered three degrees, but every degree retained its own brake interval. Crown coordinated; Ember, Salt, and Rootglass answered through separate teams. No hinge could advance after another group called a stop until the relevant cost map had been read again.

"That sounds like four chances for an army to interfere," a Vael captain said.

"It also gives civilians four chances not to drown," Pell answered.

The first action would relieve Crown pressure enough to prevent the lower chambers from folding into Vael. The second would open the Rootglass pollen corridor while Reed teams adjusted salinity gates. The third carried the Neris danger. Coastal bells, harbor heights, and evacuation barges would report before any advance. The fourth would settle the twelve-degree exchange only after every hinge answered inside its limit.

A Neris sailor laid a wet knotted cord across the map. "Our pause threshold: water reaches the seventh harbor knot, two tide stairs over present height, or three evacuation bells fail to answer. Any one condition stops the third action."

Irel added Rootglass limits: queen agitation beyond the marked pattern, pollen folding away from both paired constellations, or breakage in the nursery corridor.

Aruk named salinity and child-transport thresholds for Reedwater. Kharza named paired stone pitches that signaled an unstable load. Pell named brake heat, rope stretch, and inner-ear injuries among counterweight crews. Maela entered Vael's shelter capacity and structural movement in Crown Hinge.

"Who may pause?" Caldrin asked.

The old Compact had given each equal bearer a veto. Royal copies had erased all but human command. Restoring a single absolute refusal to each group now risked allowing one captured delegate or factional leader to halt every action for private advantage.

Debate lasted two hours.

Kharza favored one-labor refusal for immediate bodily danger. Aruk demanded protection against Kharza's former habit of calling every tactical delay disobedience. Irel insisted that ecological injury could appear before human instruments confirmed it. Pell warned that any rule requiring consultation during a snapped line would kill riggers.

They agreed on two forms.

Any labor crew could stop its local work immediately for direct danger. Resuming required all four delegations at that hinge. A strategic pause across the entire bearing required two delegations plus a published cost map showing a threshold crossed. No single delegation held an indefinite veto alone. No commander could punish a lawful stop during the emergency action.

Nim read the clauses aloud. Shelter delegates repeated them from the riverbank so the crowd beyond the warehouse could hear.

Then Kharza rose.

Her left tusk remained wrapped in leather darkened by old blood. The black-clay banner hung behind her. Soldiers across the warehouse still called her General, a title that had gathered three armies and could gather them again around one mouth.

"I relinquish sole command of the bearing assault," she said. "Military actions remain under their separate banners. Hinge labor answers to the four delegations and the witnessed limits. I command the Kiln formation until the emergency court closes, not Aruk's Reed force, not Irel's guard, not Vael's sortie."

Aruk stood beside her without taking her title. "The Reed Confederacy accepts coordinated objectives under this court. We do not swear to General Olt."

"Recorded," Nim said.

Maela felt part of the room loosen. Not trust. A load transferred into several supports.

Ratification used no crown seal. Each delegation pressed tools into wet clay: Kharza and Aruk placed opposite tusk-cap edges; Sevrin and Irel marked a comb frame; Pell and Nim used counterweight pins; Maela and the rotating human delegates pressed the river-depth rod. Refugees, coast witnesses, marsh settlements, Heartland prisoners, and Vael shelters signed the cost map itself.

The last question concerned Osra.

Maela wrote the surrender offer in plain language. Osra could stand down the upper court, release all hinge workers and civilians, preserve her right to public defense, and enter mixed custody pending trial. Her soldiers would receive food and safe disarmament. No office, marriage, or secret exile formed part of the terms.

Tavan Roe received the document under a white flag at the Crown approach. She returned an hour later with Osra's answer written across the bottom.

The Queen-Regent recognized no court assembled by rebels under military occupation. She would accept Maela's coronation, Mahr and Aeli advisory seats, and a delayed survey after the Heartland harvest. She would not surrender royal command of Crown Hinge.

Tavan handed the page to Maela. "She also ordered me to prepare the upper lock."

"Will you?"

"I command until her order targets the civilians sheltering below. After that, I decide in the moment."

It was not defection. It was a limit.

Maela placed Osra's refusal beside the ratified map. Outside, four banners moved toward their assigned approaches. The queen-hive cradle left under shared hands. Counterweight wagons crossed the bridge. Neris bells began passing the evacuation signal coastward.

The bearing court had not made peace. It had made the coming danger lawful enough to stop.

Chapter 26 — The Great Turning

Maela entered Crown Hinge through the door her ancestor had built for servants.

It opened behind the upper court's west brace, too narrow for shields and half flooded by a broken bath conduit. Pell went first with a counterweight pin between her teeth. Maela followed on hands and knees, sword scraping stone, while Vael's river company waited in darkness behind her.

Beyond the wall, Osra's soldiers braced the royal doors against assault from the public court. The servants' passage bypassed them. It also opened beneath an iron stair guarded by two engineers.

Pell struck the first behind the knee with her pin. Maela caught the second before he could ring the alarm bell. She put him against the wall and felt him stop resisting when three more blue-river soldiers emerged from the conduit.

"Drop your hammer," she said. "You receive quarter."

He did.

At dawn, four banners moved against one objective: open Crown Hinge long enough for four witnessed actions. Kharza's black-clay troops held the lower western galleries. Aruk's silver-cap companies took the river stair and protected evacuation signals. Irel's green-glass guard escorted the queen-hive cradle toward the pollen court. Maela's blue-river sortie aimed for the upper lock. No formation commanded another. Pell and Nim directed counterweight labor through every line.

Osra held the upper court with Heartland infantry, engineers, and Tavan Roe's remaining siege companies. Three royal lock bars crossed the bearing shaft. If engaged together, they could force Crown to accept one command without the outer hinges. The shortcut had preserved fixed seasons before by sending unmatched strain outward. Under present pressure, a full royal lock might shear Vael's foundations and bury the lower court.

Maela reached the stair landing and signaled attack.

River soldiers climbed. Engineers rolled barrels down at them. Pell kicked open a drain gate; water caught the first barrel and turned it sideways. The second broke against it. Soldiers used both as cover, advancing one landing while arrows struck the iron treads.

From the public court came Kharza's horn. First brace engaged.

Maela took the upper landing. A Heartland captain met her with a hooked sword, driving for the gap beneath her cuirass. She caught the hook on her crossguard and used the narrow stair to deny him reach. He yielded only after Maela's soldiers reached the platform behind her. Several defenders dropped weapons. Others withdrew toward Tavan's central line.

The upper court opened around Crown Hinge's shaft. Rings of dark stone descended farther than torchlight. Three brace towers rose around it, each linked by bronze walks. Royal mosaics showed human rulers laying hands on a simplified sky while the other laborers stood below carrying tools. Fresh barricades blocked the pollen court and counterweight galleries.

Tavan waited at the central bridge in siege armor stained with mortar. "Crown Marshal."

"Marshal Roe. Order withdrawal and safe disarmament. The bearing court offers food, treatment, and public trial."

"The Queen-Regent rejects its authority."

"Do you?"

Tavan looked toward the lower refuge. A new royal order hung from her belt. Maela could see the grain-sheaf wax.

"My troops hold the bridge," Tavan said. "Yours have families under it. Advance and the shaft becomes a grave."

"Your engineers know the same."

The first assault halted. Battle noise continued in surrounding galleries, but neither line entered the central bridge. Maela used the pause to bring civilian witnesses through the servants' passage. Edris from Lantern Ward came with a black refuge counter. Seli of Neris carried harbor cords. Hessa brought the salt-stiff nursery basket. They took places behind cover, present where royal bearing had once admitted only command.

Osra's voice carried from the eastern tower. "Tavan, clear the pollen court. Burn the hive if they enter."

The order found every soldier on the bridge.

Tavan closed her eyes. "There are civilians behind that barricade. Rootglass prisoners."

"Then move them."

"No route remains."

"The hinge cannot pass to rebels. Execute the order."

Tavan drew the royal message from her belt and broke its wax in her fist. She turned to the officers beside her. "I will not burn the pollen court. Units choosing lawful surrender cross west with weapons grounded. Units remaining under the Queen-Regent withdraw east. No fire."

One officer stabbed her below the shoulder plate.

Maela's soldiers surged. Tavan caught the attacker's wrist, but blood darkened her sleeve. Heartland companies split across the bridge. Some struck their former comrades. Others threw down weapons. Pell crawled under the railing and pulled the fire-release chain free before anyone could use it.

Maela reached Tavan as the marshal sagged. "You are under mixed guard."

"Captured?"

"Alive."

"I have had worse mornings."

Healers carried Tavan west. Her refusal collapsed the central defense without erasing the battles she had directed. Maela sent her written orders with two witnesses.

Second brace engaged, Aruk's horn reported.

That left the north brace and pollen court under Osra's control. Maela divided the river company rather than driving everyone toward the royal wheel. One file followed Edris to the refuge stair, where Heartland soldiers had begun forcing civilians upward as cover. Another joined Irel's guard at the pollen barricade. Maela kept twenty soldiers for the counterweight gallery.

"Twenty does not take a brace tower," her captain warned.

"Twenty prevents its engineers from locking Pell out. We do not need the tower if the labor holds."

The gallery ran behind a screen of carved kings. Defenders had knocked holes through the royal faces for arrow slits. Pell sent two Vennik riggers beneath the screen with flat boards over their backs. Arrows struck wood while they crawled to the first weight bed. Maela's soldiers advanced in pairs, one shield high, one low.

At the third royal face, boiling mash poured from above. It had come from Vael's seized kitchens, food turned into a weapon while inner districts starved. The low shield caught most of it. Two soldiers screamed where it entered their boots. Maela ordered the pair carried back and saw that the spilled mash already cooled into edible paste along the floor.

"Save what has not touched blood," she told Edris.

He stared at her.

"There are hungry shelters. Use clean buckets."

The absurd labor began under arrows. Refugee workers scraped food from protected corners while soldiers held the screen. War contained no material too humble to matter.

Pell's crawling riggers reached the bed and removed its retaining pins. The unloaded frame rolled across the gallery, struck the carved screen from behind, and broke two kings off at the knees. Maela's company went through the opening.

Engineers defended their ropes with axes. One Vennik worker fighting for Osra cut a brake line and shouted that royal stores had fed her house through three failed winters. Pell answered that surrender preserved her right to testify. The worker dropped her axe only after Maela's captain swore the terms in front of Edris.

They took the gallery with six wounded and three dead. Osra's engineers lost four dead; eleven surrendered. Pell immediately mixed both crews according to skill. Former enemies began replacing the brake line they had fought over.

The north brace remained.

Aruk's Reed fighters approached it through a flooded stair. Kharza's Kiln troops occupied a dry gallery on the other side. A royal gate separated them. Neither commander could hear the other clearly through Crown Hinge, but their runners met at Maela's position and laid out competing plans. Kharza proposed cutting the gate pins from dry stone. Aruk proposed raising water until buoyancy lifted the gate.

"Cutting takes thirty minutes," Kharza's runner said.

"Raising risks the refuge drain," said Aruk's.

Maela asked the local fault-readers and water crews. Veka's assistant reported weakened lintels above the dry side. A Reed sluice keeper said one hand of water would lift the gate without reaching the refuge, provided a downstream screen opened first.

"Aruk's plan with a witnessed water mark," Maela decided. "Kharza's troops clear the lintel fall line and secure the raised gate."

The runners departed without needing either command absorbed into the other.

Water rose through the stair. The royal gate shuddered, floated a finger, and jammed on mineral scale. Reed swimmers passed lines beneath it. Kiln soldiers hauled from the dry side. When the lower edge lifted high enough, Kharza crawled through first despite Lessa's shouted objection. Aruk entered from the opposite stair. Together their troops seized the north brace wheel.

Third horn. Brace ready.

At the pollen court, resistance worsened.

Osra's guards had driven Rootglass prisoners into the outer chamber and tied their wrists to hive racks. Archers waited behind them. Irel refused a shot through her own workers. She sent green-glass climbers along ceiling irrigation pipes instead. Sevrin stayed below with the living comb, using descriptions from two apprentices to identify where upper-star light entered the court.

"Close the eastern shutters," he said. "Open the lower pollen vents."

A captured gardener protested that the lower vents had been sealed under royal order.

"Then unseal them."

Workers broke plaster with counterweight hammers. Buried pollen rose in pale dust from Crown's lower channels. The queen stirred inside her clay hive. Worker bees slipped through the cradle wrap and flew toward the ceiling pipes.

The swarm did not attack. It followed pollen over the prisoners, making their outlines visible through dust. Irel's climbers could see the empty spaces between bodies. They dropped on the archers there.

Fighting spread through the hive racks. Maela arrived as a guard dragged one bound prisoner toward an open shaft. She crossed the chamber, caught his belt, and put her sword against the joint of his mail. He released the prisoner. Another guard shot at Maela; an orchard tenant blocked the arrow with a clay hive lid.

Sevrin called locations from the apprentices' reports without pretending he could see the pollen himself. Irel changed shutter angles from above. Their opposed knowledge turned the chamber's light into a map. Rootglass prisoners ducked when lower pollen brightened around hostile movement.

The final defenders surrendered when the queen-hive workers gathered on the bearing channels rather than dying in sealed air.

Irel cut the prisoners free before touching the queen. Sevrin ordered every opened vent measured. The battle had altered pollen flow; using old ratios would convert rescue into a hidden error. A Neris witness and a Rootglass worker recorded the new current together.

Only then did they carry the queen-hive toward Crown's center.

Across the upper court, Maela could now trace the whole assault by labor rather than conquered ground. Kharza and Aruk held three brace approaches. Pell possessed the counterweight beds. Irel and Sevrin held the pollen court. Civilian witnesses had reached every station. Osra still controlled the east royal lock, and that single control could endanger all the rest.

The queen-hive cradle reached Crown's center under Irel, Sevrin, a Rootglass worker, and a Neris witness. Bees stirred beneath the wrap. Sevrin kept one hand on a guide cord and one on the living comb.

Pell opened the counterweight gallery. "All stations report ready except east royal lock."

Osra stood there.

The Queen-Regent wore no battle armor, only a dark grain-clerk's coat over mail. Her gray hair had come loose. Two royal engineers held the final lock bar while four guards protected the wheel. Behind her, a painted Cinder Net covered the wall in gold leaf.

"You built a court while my army held this city together against four invasions," Osra said.

Maela crossed the central bridge. "Your army broke its wall."

"To keep a power you do not understand from being turned by grievance."

"We measured it. We set limits. You were offered a seat to answer them publicly."

"I remember the Ash Famine. Public hunger does not pause for beautiful procedure."

"Neither do drowned children."

Osra put both hands on the royal wheel. "Then history will judge which deaths I prevented."

She ordered the full lock.

Engineers drove the bar before Maela's archers could reach them. Bronze entered Crown Hinge with a blow that shook every tower. The other two lock bars dropped through hidden linkages. Royal mosaics split along their grout. The shaft's stone rings began moving as one.

Not forward through the planned first action. Inward, toward a fixed command that forced every outer hinge to conform.

Pell fell against the gallery wall. "She has coupled the braces. Cut east linkage!"

Vael soldiers attacked across the bridge. Osra's guards held long enough for the wheel to descend one turn. Crown Hinge groaned. Through the open roof, the upper Cinder Net brightened at morning, visible where no constellation should show in daylight.

Below, Kharza's horn gave two broken notes. Stone danger.

Maela had one path to Osra and another to the east linkage. She sent soldiers after the Queen-Regent and went with Pell toward the coupling.

The linkage ran inside a bronze sleeve thick as a tree trunk. Pell found its inspection mouth and drove in two opposing pins. Neither stopped the rotation.

"Need relief from outer braces," she said. "Begin first action or Crown tears the pins out."

Maela looked to the witness rail. All four delegations had representatives present or connected by signal. Mahr: Kharza and Aruk below. Aeli: Sevrin and Irel in the pollen court. Vennik: Pell at the coupling, Nim at the western weights. River-city: Maela, Seli, Edris, and a Heartland miller under the open court.

The cost map lay stretched across the west walk.

"First action," Maela called. "Three degrees, one interval at a time. Local stops hold."

Clay horns carried the order below. Signal towers relayed it across Vael, then outward by mirror, river flag, and pollen lamps. At Ember Hinge, Kiln teams opened the old drainage channels. At Salt, Reed crews adjusted salinity gates. Rootglass workers prepared the migration corridor.

Kharza and Aruk sounded Crown's paired roots from different galleries. One sang through volcanic-glass tusk veining; the other listened with one silver cap removed and answered in the cadence of marsh pilings. Their notes did not merge. Each identified a separate stress, and Pell balanced between them.

Counterweight beds took load.

First degree.

Crown Hinge shifted against Osra's lock. Gold leaf fell from the Cinder Net mural. Under Maela's boots, stone moved west, though the bridge remained aligned.

Second degree.

The royal linkage heated around Pell's pins. She packed it with wet cloth and called for more return weight. Nim's crews rolled recovered siege stone into the western beds.

Third degree.

The first action settled. Horns answered from Ember, Salt, and Rootglass. Vael's refuge counters remained inside limits. Heartland field watchers reported no red mold. The full lock had stopped tightening, but it remained engaged.

Osra retreated to the east wheel with six guards. She pulled on it again.

"Second action," Maela ordered after all four delegations consented.

Rootglass opened the queen-hive cradle. Irel lifted the clay jar while Sevrin released measured buried-sky pollen along the paired channel. Workers carried both living comb and lower pollen through opposite arches. Bees entered the court in a dark ribbon, ordinary wings moving celestial influence from one alignment to another.

Aruk's river horns reported marsh gates open. Kharza's listeners gave a stable first pitch.

Fourth degree.

Pressure drove Maela to one knee. East seemed to change places with the shaft for a breath. She checked the chalk on her sleeve and stood.

Fifth degree.

Heartland soldiers still loyal to Osra attacked the western counterweight bed. Edris and shelter workers pulled wounded riggers clear while Vael defenders held the gallery mouth. Pell did not leave the brake. She sent a reserve team through a lower maintenance cut, adding load while arrows struck the frames.

Sixth degree.

A lock bar cracked. One end dropped across the central bridge, separating Maela from Osra. Crown Hinge settled into its second action with all three outer answers inside limits.

Nobody rested. The third action carried the Neris flood line.

Seli tied her harbor cord to the witness rail. Signal runners waited beside four bells. The first reports arrived from the coast by relay mirrors: tide climbing within forecast, floating-home moorings sound, cellar evacuation incomplete in the south ward.

"No advance until south answers," Seli said.

Osra's remaining archers used the delay. They shot into the pollen court from the east tower. Green-glass guards raised shields over the queen-hive. One arrow broke the living-comb frame. Sevrin gathered exposed comb with bare hands while bees crawled across his sleeves.

Maela sent a river company across the fallen lock bar. Its curved surface offered no rail. Three soldiers fell into the lower walk before the rest secured ropes. Osra's guards withdrew toward the royal wheel.

The south Neris bell answered.

"Third action authorized," Seli called.

Seventh degree.

Water rose in both Crown casks. Reedwater reported salinity below the first threshold. Rootglass pollen held its corridor.

Eighth degree.

A Neris mirror flashed red. Seventh harbor knot.

Seli rang the pause bell. Irel struck the Aeli stop plate with her palm. Two delegations. Pell locked the counterweights before Maela spoke.

The hinge halted eight degrees into the exchange, one degree short of the third action's end.

Osra's archers kept shooting. The royal linkage strained against the stopped beds. Crown stone gave a sound like mountains grinding their teeth.

A Vael captain shouted, "We cannot hold here. Finish the degree."

"Pause threshold crossed," Maela said.

"The lock will kill us."

"Then we fight while Neris moves."

She sent every available runner to accelerate the coastal relay. Aruk ordered two Reed reserve barges away from Vael despite the battle, assigning them to Neris cellar evacuation. Pell bled pressure into return beds without advancing the bearing. Kharza and Veka changed their stone-song from movement cadence to holding cadence. Irel returned the queen to the cradle so agitation would not spread through the hive.

Twenty minutes became forty. The east linkage split another layer of bronze. Maela's soldiers reached the royal wheel and lost it twice. Osra fought without a sword, ordering brace positions from the painted map and refusing every demand to surrender.

At fifty-three minutes, the second coastal bell failed to answer.

Seli did not release the pause. "Three evacuation bells must answer."

Maela backed her publicly. The decision might bury them. It also showed the coast that its threshold meant more than a promise made when convenient.

A runner came from the lower refuge. Osra's soldiers had broken into the west shelter. Kharza diverted one Kiln company from Crown's defense, weakening her own position to protect civilians. Aruk sent a Reed company with them under separate command.

After sixty-seven minutes, Neris reported the south cellars clear. Reed barges had taken the last families from the floating-home lane. Three bells answered.

Seli inspected the tide cord. Water had stopped below the eighth knot.

"Resume," she said.

Irel confirmed. Pell confirmed. Kharza and Aruk answered from the roots. Maela put her hand on the river-city mark.

Ninth degree.

The third action settled.

Osra's lock began to fail.

Its east bar did not release. It bent downward into the shaft, catching one stone ring while the others advanced. The mismatched load tore the royal wheel from its base. Guards scattered. Osra clung to the wheel chain, preventing the bar from dropping deeper.

"Fourth action now," Pell said. "If we wait, the bar welds in place."

Maela checked the court. Neris remained inside its renewed threshold. Rootglass queen agitation had eased. Reed salinity held. Vael refuge counters reported the western shelter secure. All four delegations authorized.

Tenth degree.

The world lost direction.

Maela saw the east tower, the shaft, Pell at the brake, and the blue chalk on her sleeve. None occupied east, down, near, or far in the private map inside her body. She stepped toward Pell and struck the witness rail instead. Her stomach rose as if she had fallen.

"Maela," Pell called. "Stay on the rail."

Maela gripped it. "Tell me where the river mark lies."

"Two hands left of yours."

Left still meant something through muscle. She found the mark.

Eleventh degree.

Sky-shear drove through Crown Hinge. Pell's counterweight crews staggered as inner ears failed. Veka dropped unconscious below. Kharza could no longer sing through her injured tusk; Aruk removed his second silver cap and carried the holding cadence while she struck its rhythm into stone with a mallet. Their two methods shared the load without making either commander lesser.

Irel opened the queen-hive again. Star-bees rose through broken mosaics into upper daylight, carrying buried pollen. Sevrin called their movement from other people's descriptions, then adjusted the living comb so the swarm crossed both channels.

The bent lock bar slipped.

Pell shouted a cutaway order. One counterweight rope parted before the crew reached it. A loaded bed ran toward the shaft, dragging two riggers. Maela could not tell where they moved. She followed Pell's voice along the rail and caught the cutaway lever with both hands.

"Pull toward me," Pell said.

Maela pulled. The lever resisted. A second pair of hands closed beside hers.

Osra had crossed the broken bridge.

"The royal return pawl," Osra said. "Below the lever. Kick twice, then pull."

Maela could not find below. "Guide my boot."

Osra seized her ankle and placed it against bronze. Maela kicked. Once. Twice. The pawl released. Together they hauled the cutaway lever.

The runaway bed separated from its stone load. Riggers fell clear. Basalt blocks entered the shaft and vanished toward the buried sky. Crown Hinge lurched, but the return beds caught what remained.

"Twelfth degree," Pell called.

The final action moved.

All four hinges answered. Kharza's struck rhythm came through the west root. Aruk's bare tusks carried the Reed cadence beside it. Queen-hive workers crossed the pollen court in two streams. Vennik brakes locked at the witnessed mark. Maela kept one hand on the river-city seal while direction vanished from her for good.

Upper daylight darkened. Through the broken roof, the Cinder Net slid twelve degrees from its fixed place. Far below, the Deep Hart moved into a new arc. Rain began somewhere beyond Vael. Along Neris, water stopped below the eighth knot. Rootglass pollen held. Reed salinity rose, then steadied under the mapped limit.

The royal lock split along all three bars.

Osra let go of Maela's ankle. Mixed guards surrounded her: one Kiln soldier, one Reed fighter, one Rootglass guard, and one Vael defender. She looked toward the shaft as if she could still command its direction.

"Queen-Regent Osra Dane," Maela said, holding the rail. "You are under public custody. You will live to answer the record."

Osra did not resist.

Across the upper court, soldiers lowered weapons as reports carried the same fact through every gallery. The siege had ended. Immediate pressure receded from Vael's foundations. Outer lands gained movement and new costs rather than rescue from all scarcity.

Maela could no longer point toward the relief bridge, the river, or the room where Pell stood. Pell placed a guide cord in her hand.

"Three steps left," Pell said.

Maela followed the voice. Each step required an instruction her body once supplied without thought. A river soldier tied knots into the guide cord for the nearest exits. Maela learned the first one before she reached Pell. Around them, witnesses continued reading costs aloud rather than celebrating over unfinished casualty lists. Behind her, twelve degrees of changed sky rested on four hinges and no dynasty's exclusive word.

Chapter 27 — The Queen-Regent's Trial

Osra spent the first morning after defeat copying names from wet paper.

Mixed guards sat at either end of the relief table: a Kiln soldier with a chipped clay gorget, a Rootglass archer whose left arm rested in a sling, a Vennik barge worker, and a human defender from Vael's south ward. They did not speak to her unless she misread a line. Her title appeared nowhere on the work slate.

Outside the warehouse, burial carts crossed the relief bridge under strict load limits. Army kitchens served anyone in the queue. Heartland soldiers ate beside families they had starved. Sometimes a person recognized a uniform and left hungry rather than stand near it. The kitchens recorded that refusal as a cost, not ingratitude.

Osra copied a scout's name, unit, district, and condition. Missing. She copied the next. Wounded, found at east brace. The next. Dead at Flood Bastion.

Administrative calm came easily to her. That fact no longer offered comfort.

On Day 52, the public tribunal began in the same roofless warehouse that had held the bearing court. No throne faced the benches. Osra sat at floor level behind a rail with Tavan Roe at a separate table. Tavan's wounded shoulder remained bound, and a guard helped her stand whenever the court required it.

Brother Caldrin placed two ledgers beneath clear oilcloth.

One carried smoke damage from the royal archive and Ordan's private mark on its spine. Osra had preserved it through four relocations, two palace purges, and the siege. Its pages recorded the Ash Famine forty-two years earlier: grain weights, district deaths, locked warehouses, river ice, and the names of officials who had ordered seed stores ground into flour.

The second ledger looked cleaner. Osra's clerks had prepared it during the siege.

Caldrin opened both to the same table.

"Original Heartland reserve transferred to outer regions during the fixed period," he read. "Twenty-seven parts in one hundred. Public copy: nineteen. Original Varkesh deaths linked to pressure relocations: listed by district. Public copy: category removed. Reed nursery loss: three columns. Public copy: folded into storm damage."

A clerk projected the entries onto a whitewashed wall. Every subtraction appeared large enough for the crowd to read.

The presiding panel contained one member from each bearing delegation and three civilian recorders chosen by lot. Maela attended from a marked chair with guide cords tied between rail, exit, and witness place. Pell sat elsewhere, under no obligation to serve as her direction. Kharza appeared without general's command staff. Sevrin read ordinary ink copies and asked witnesses to describe only the star-path evidence his lost leafing could no longer perceive.

"Did you order the changes?" asked Hessa of Reedwater.
