"Yes," Osra said.

"Name the document."

Osra looked at the cleaner volume. "A falsified ledger."

The words entered the record in four scripts.

"Why preserve the original?" Hessa asked.

"Because its figures remained necessary for policy."

Anger moved through the warehouse.

"Necessary for you," Hessa said. "Unavailable to the people paying them."

"Yes."

Osra did not ask the court to confuse admission with repair.

Over the next eighteen days, the trial moved between accusation and relief accounting. Witnesses testified in the morning. In the afternoon, all able prisoners copied lists, packed grain, or cleared safe stone under guard. Osra worked wherever assignment placed her. She learned to distinguish bridge mortar from hinge stone and failed repeatedly. Veka sent her to wash measuring rods instead.

Tavan testified on Day 56.

She described the siege objectives, the order to seize Crown Hinge, and every instruction she had obeyed before the pollen-court fire command. She did not portray herself as secretly opposed. She had placed artillery against Flood Bastion. She had approved sapper galleries. She had known civilians used the cistern route and judged the military objective sufficient.

"When did you decide the Queen-Regent's authority ended?" the panel asked.

"Too late," Tavan said. "Specifically, when she ordered me to burn a chamber containing prisoners and the surviving hive. I had accepted starvation and breach as war. I would not accept fire without a military distinction."

Osra listened from behind the rail. Tavan had warned her before the siege assault that civilians would carry the cost. Osra remembered answering with supply forecasts, as though precision transformed coercion into weather.

A Rootglass guard asked Tavan whether Osra had ordered the orchard attack.

The captured field orders bore a deputy's hand, but the policy of destroying unauthorized hinge evidence came from Osra's council. Tavan placed those orders in the chain without inventing a signature that did not exist.

"Responsibility does not require false proof," she said.

The court entered both facts.

On Day 59, Pell presented Jorren Taal's scout log.

Nim had recovered its wax leaves from the last evacuation barge. The final pages listed gate times, district counts, and individual decisions. Jorren named the Lantern Ward search pairs who checked cellars. He named the scouts who crawled under the gate. He recorded the final four hundred sixty civilians aboard his barge. He did not name how he held the crank.

"His request concerned the glorious column," Pell said. "There isn't one. The record names who got out and who did not."

Osra asked permission to read the last page. Pell carried it to the rail but did not place it in Osra's hands.

Jorren had written one sentence about the attacking force: disciplined movement toward the hinge approach, no general sack before the breach. Osra had considered that restraint evidence of mercy. Beside the sentence, Pell had entered the civilian bodies recovered from collapsed districts.

A tactic could show restraint compared with something worse and still serve a criminal objective.

Survivors followed.

The first came from Flood Bastion. Vela Or, a human mason, had supervised mortar repairs before the siege and spent the breach beneath a stair with her leg pinned. She brought a piece of outer-wall foundation wrapped in cloth. Sapper fire had glazed one face.

"We reported the lower gallery voids before the full assault," she said. "Three copies went out. One to Crown Marshal Ruun. One to siege lines under white flag. One stayed in the wall office."

The panel asked Osra whether she had received the warning.

"Tavan's morning packet included it."

"What did you do?"

"Approved the breach plan."

Vela set the stone on the record table. "Then enter that we told you where civilians sheltered. Do not let anyone call the collapse unforeseeable because engineers used fire underground."

Caldrin attached the warning copy to the assault order. Tavan requested that her approval mark appear beside Osra's. Vela insisted Maela's decision to keep defenders at Flood Bastion also remain. The record accepted all three.

Next, a Vennik rigger named Savo described West Sluice. Their house had supported Osra before the siege because Heartland contracts kept caravan bridges funded. During the breach, the palace bell brake crushed Savo's adopted daughter beneath its cradle.

"Quartermaster Vask chose the bell," Savo said. "I agreed to the cradle. Osra's assault caused the shock. The old timber failed. Which person killed my child?"

No panel member answered quickly.

Pell stood from her separate bench. "I made the load decision. The cradle inspection had six minutes. I accepted green wood from the coronation platform."

Osra felt the room lean toward a single verdict and resist it.

Savo looked from Pell to her. "I do not ask for one name. I ask whether the new rules will let workers refuse green wood when a city is dying."

Nim read the emergency clause protecting local load refusals. The longer tribunal added Savo's question to permanent bridge law. Loss entered governance without dissolving individual responsibility.

A Reed camp cook testified after them. Mevi had marched under Aruk because Osra's treaty promised marsh autonomy and salt relief. She described feeding soldiers while officers concealed nursery losses from them. When the incendiary order reached the bridge, her cooking crews had already filled clay bulbs with oil.

"We did not shoot them," Mevi said. "We still made them."

Hessa asked why.

"Because the order came as bridge denial, not civilian burning. Words shifted the people under the deck out of view."

Osra recognized the method. She had approved that phrase. Bridge denial sounded bounded, strategic, clean. The hidden barges turned it into attempted killing without requiring the paper to say children.

"Who chose those words?" Hessa asked.

"I did," Osra said.

Mevi faced her. "Did you know the barges held evacuees?"

"Scouts reported heat and movement below the deck. They did not confirm the count."

"You ordered fire before confirming."

"Yes."

The cook's testimony joined Aruk's refusal signal and the captured incendiary instructions.

From Rootglass came a prisoner freed in the pollen court. Taen Saan had served Irel's conservative council and had once argued that Sevrin should be barred from every hive. Osra's troops detained him when he objected to burning their retreat route. They tied him to a rack as cover.

Taen did not credit the coalition with moral purity. "Irel withheld evidence from tenant workers. Sevrin hid Neris figures. Osra used both failures to make royal protection seem like the only stable choice. Then her guards used our bodies to preserve that choice."

He presented the arrow that had passed through his sleeve while Irel's climbers entered. Its fletching carried a Heartland unit mark. The archer who loosed it had died. Taen asked that the record name the unit and not invent motive for a dead person.

Osra had spent years turning incomplete information into justification for central control. Taen demanded the opposite discipline: record what could be proven, preserve uncertainty, and still judge the command structure that placed archers behind prisoners.

A Heartland widow spoke on Day 61. Unlike most witnesses, she began by defending Osra.

Her district had survived two failed harvests because fixed Cinder Net warmth kept winter grain alive. Royal reserve schedules had delivered flour when local merchants withheld it. Her husband and eldest daughter joined Osra's army willingly. Both died at Vael.

"If you had turned the sky years ago, would my younger children have eaten?" she asked the panel.

No witness could answer with certainty.

She pointed at the outer-region columns. "These losses matter. So do lives the fixed season saved. If you write her as a ruler who wanted only power, you falsify another ledger."

Seli agreed. "The warning belongs in the record."

Then the widow faced Osra. "Why did you change the numbers? If the danger spoke for itself, why hide who paid for our grain?"

Osra considered every old reason: preventing regional panic, keeping barges moving, preserving one authority during famine risk, stopping rival councils from turning figures into secession. Each reason described an intended effect. None answered the moral question.

"Because consent might have produced an answer I could not control," she said.

The widow wept without forgiving her.

In afternoon labor, guards assigned Osra to copy that exchange. She wrote the woman's defense first, then the accusation, then her own answer. Rain blurred one line. The Kiln guard made her start the page again rather than decide the damaged words remained readable enough.

Another day's testimony came from the displaced hinge villages. Families had received relocation orders marked temporary. Years later, royal farms occupied their terraces. One Mahr potter described his kiln taken apart because its stone-song interfered with survey instruments. An Aeli laborer described pruning records seized under security law. A human village head admitted she had helped choose which neighboring families lost carts first.

Caldrin built a chain of custody for each decree. Some bore Osra's mark. Others came from provincial officials exceeding broad authority. The panel separated them rather than making Osra responsible for every theft committed under her policy. It also asked whether she punished any official after reports arrived.

She had punished two for stealing tax grain. None for removing hinge witnesses.

The distinction showed what her administration had valued.

By the end of the survivor sessions, the evidence tables held objects beside paper: glazed foundation stone, a broken brake pin, salt-stiff basket fibers, hive-rack rope, a child's name tablet, and grain from a protected Heartland district. Osra had once trusted figures because objects could provoke sympathy beyond proportion. Now every object carried a figure her summaries had reduced, and every figure belonged to a material consequence someone could touch.

Edris testified about the delayed Lantern Ward withdrawal and people lost when Flood Bastion collapsed. A Heartland camp medic testified that Osra's ration rules favored fit soldiers after the siege road closed. An Aeli worker from the orchard fire team explained why she accepted grain guarantees for her family and why she surrendered after seeing the queen choose lower pollen. A Mahr soldier from Kharza's old imperial command described villages relocated from hinge faults under Osra's earlier decrees.

Not every witness blamed Osra for every death. One Vael defender said Maela had chosen to hold an outer wall after evacuation capacity failed. A Reed captain blamed Aruk for joining the siege in exchange for autonomy. Rootglass tenants named Irel's former cooperation. Pell entered her own old ration falsification into the governance record. The court refused a simple tale in which one guilty ruler made everyone else innocent.

That refusal left Osra with nowhere to hide inside exaggeration.

On Day 63, the panel asked about the Ash Famine.

Osra described being sixteen when cold reached the grain districts. Her mother boiled leather bindings from account books. People broke warehouse doors and found seed stores already spoiled. Local councils issued conflicting orders. River towns kept barges tied while villages upriver starved. When warmth returned, officials destroyed death lists to keep tax claims alive.

"I learned that delay kills," Osra said. "I learned that fragmented authority can turn food into a border weapon. The fixed Cinder Net gave the Heartland seasons it could plan. I believed another uncontrolled exchange would repeat the famine on a larger field."

Seli of Neris asked, "Was that warning false?"

"No. Your coast water rose during the third action."

"And the pause?"

"It prevented deaths."

"Would your royal command have paused?"

Osra looked toward the oilcloth-covered ledgers. "No."

The court allowed the distinction to remain. Her warning had substance. A full uncontrolled exchange could have flooded Neris, salted Reedwater, damaged Rootglass, and reduced Heartland grain. The danger did not authorize falsifying who already suffered, starving Vael into surrender, executing surveyors, or reserving all consent to one dynasty.

"Why did you help release the return pawl?" Maela asked.

Osra could have described it as proof that she always intended preservation. That would have turned one necessary act into a lie about every choice preceding it.

"Because the bar would have torn Crown Hinge," she said. "Because you could not find it after sky-shear took your direction. Because I knew the royal fitting. I had already lost command. Help no longer threatened my custody because my custody had failed."

Maela received the answer without gratitude or contempt. "Recorded."

By Day 68, the missing lists had narrowed. Four hundred twenty people unaccounted for after the breach had been traced through barge rolls, hospital transfers, burial records, and families sheltering under changed names. Some entered the civilian-death count. Others entered the survivor registry. No separate missing number remained to disguise either.

The military dead across both sides totaled 6,840.

Civilian dead totaled 1,130.

The panel read each figure once, then began reading names. The work continued through sunset and into the next morning. Categories included Mahr, Aeli, Vennik, human, unknown, soldier, camp worker, prisoner, resident, and refugee, but no category replaced a name when one could be found.

Osra listened to all of them.

On Day 70, the panel issued an interim sentence. The longer tribunal would still hear forced relocation, surveyor executions, district seizure, siege starvation, and orchard policy. Until then, Osra lost every office, military authority, estate command, and private access to state records. Mixed guards would supervise her public labor. She would copy the original and altered ledgers side by side for distribution, assist grain restitution records, and answer witnesses without controlling the order of questions.

One Vael man shouted for execution. A Heartland widow shouted that the court had humiliated the person who saved her district from famine. Guards removed neither until both finished speaking.

Osra stood when instructed. "I understand the sentence."

Understanding did not mean she accepted every judgment. Part of her still counted the harvests preserved under the fixed sky and imagined the dead a chaotic exchange might have made. That part would not vanish because she had lost. The tribunal required something harder than inward conversion. It required records others could inspect and power she no longer possessed.

After adjournment, Maela signed the order by guiding her hand along a raised rule. Aruk and Kharza signed separately. Irel pressed the shared-hive mark. Pell and Nim marked the counterweight pin. No signature stood above the others.

Outside, emergency military offices began transferring food, bridge, and shelter decisions to mixed councils. Disputes started before the first meeting ended. Heartland delegates demanded planting priority. Vael shelters demanded stone. Reedwater asked for barges already promised to Neris. Governance arrived not as quiet, but as conflict forced into a room with a public ledger.

A guard returned Osra to the copying table.

She opened the original Ash Famine volume and set a clean sheet beside it. This time the outer-region columns remained visible. When rain spotted the page through the open roof, she waited for it to dry rather than closing the book on the figures.

Chapter 28 — When Both Skies Had Dawn

One year later, Kharza argued with a rain channel.

Water came down the repaired Varkesh terrace in a clear, orderly sheet and struck the new spill stones exactly where the cost map predicted. Then it jumped the third lip, cut across a bean row, and began carrying black soil toward the lower kiln road.

"Close south gate," Kharza called.

A terrace worker ignored the title used by visiting clerks and obeyed the emergency mark instead. Two people dropped a ceramic gate into the channel. Flow divided toward empty ash beds. Mahr children packed the break with clay while a human surveyor recorded water height. Nobody waited for Kharza to sing the stone.

Her left tusk appreciated the restraint.

The crack still ran from the lower curve toward the root, dark beneath a fitted copper brace. Cold made it ache. Rain vibrated through it like a second pulse. Lessa had designed the brace to open along one edge, allowing Kharza to hear enough stone-song for ordinary work without taking a bearing load. She had also stamped NO HEROICS across the inner plate.

"The third lip needs widening," said the terrace worker.

"It passed inspection last month."

"Last month had less water."

Kharza entered the failure on her delegate slate. The new compact required local conditions to defeat old approval without anyone pretending the approval had never made sense. Repairs would cost kiln clay promised to Vael. She marked the conflict for public decision.

Varkesh no longer cracked under constant buried heat. Rain had returned in intervals, enough to plant upper terraces abandoned during the fixed years. It also loosened ash slopes and brought molds older growers did not recognize. Two villages argued over a reservoir built where neither remembered a stream. Every benefit arrived with work attached.

At midday, the road caravan left for Vael Tern.

Kharza traveled without command staff. Her black-clay badge read Delegate Olt, elected for a two-year term by Varkesh hearth councils and removable after any public petition reached the required names. Two former soldiers served as road guards. Three terrace workers carried the drainage objection. A family from Ashwall rode in the rear wagon with stones recovered from the outwork's ruins for the Vael memorial.

The journey crossed land altered by twelve degrees of sky.

Heartland grain stood shorter under the shifted Cinder Net. Relief cooperatives had replaced part of the lost harvest with marsh millet and Varkesh beans. Some farmers cursed the compact at every toll. Others had discovered that cooler nights reduced blight. Royal estates divided during restitution now carried boundary stakes in three colors, each disputed.

Along Reedwater, new salt gates shone among the reeds. Aruk's councils had moved nursery beds farther inland and converted two drowned fields to fish channels. South Mirr still had no spawning return. Hessa's empty basket hung in the local court as a warning against calling adaptation recovery.

Aruk met the caravan at a flood quay. Both silver caps covered his tusks again.

"Your terrace water exceeds the first forecast," he said instead of greeting her.

"Your northern salinity report omits two tenant pools."

"They filed after the deadline."

"Salt did not."

He showed his tusks in a brief smile. Their political rivalry had survived alliance, which reassured Kharza more than affection would have. Aruk joined the caravan under the silver-cap banner. His guards remained Reed Confederacy soldiers, never hers.

Rootglass sent Irel, Sevrin, and a cart of living comb.

The orchards had changed. Western terraces remained black after the fire. The nursery had lost a third of its young trees to salt injury over the winter. Elsewhere, buried-sky pollen produced thick leaves with blue undersides and fruit that ripened later. Whether those trees would live through another twelve-year cycle remained unknown.

Sevrin stepped down from the cart using a guide rope marked with pollen samples. No filaments had returned at his temples. They never would. He no longer answered to Starwarden.

"Describe the clouds," he asked Kharza.

"Low over Reedwater. Broken west. Bright line toward Vael."

"Any amber under the west edge?"

"None I can see."

An apprentice beside him gave a more exact color account. Sevrin combined both descriptions with the queen-hive's morning pattern. Loss had changed his practice from solitary reading into a public chain of observations. It had not softened his anger when reports arrived late.

Irel kept physical care of the queen within Rootglass's public apiary, but the cradle latches required shared marks before travel. A Neris witness, a Vennik keeper, and a Mahr pollen worker rode with her. Rootglass had not surrendered its knowledge. It had opened custody around it.

Vael Tern appeared on the fifth morning.

Reconstruction had made the city less symmetrical. The north wall used black Varkesh brick, pale Heartland limestone, and panels of green rootglass that flexed when buried pressure rose. Old royal builders would have hidden the joins beneath white plaster. New masons left every material exposed and stamped each contributing district into the mortar.

The relief bridge carried two separate lanes. Supply wagons used the center under posted weights. Pedestrians and handcarts used timber galleries bolted to the outer arches. Nim's bridge council had refused merchants' requests for priority tolls, then accepted a timed grain lane after Heartland farmers proved that waiting spoiled seed. Compromise had produced a schedule nobody admired and everyone could inspect.

At the west weigh house, Kharza found her terrace dispute waiting for her.

Vael's repair council requested the same kiln clay needed to widen Varkesh's third channel lip. A blue-river mason displayed drawings of an inner shelter arch still supported by temporary beams. The Varkesh workers displayed a jar of soil carried from the washed bean row.

"If our arch waits," the mason said, "two hundred residents remain in winter tents."

"If our lip waits," replied the terrace worker, "the next rain takes planting soil from six families."

Kharza possessed no authority to allocate either supply alone. A year earlier she would have weighed military importance, chosen, and called speed a virtue. As delegate, she called a temporary cost session at the weigh house.

Pell arrived with transport figures. Four clay wagons stood loaded outside Varkesh. Moving them all to Vael required draft teams scheduled for Rootglass hive transport. Splitting the shipment would repair neither structure fully.

A Vennik kiln builder proposed a third option: fire hollow arch blocks in Varkesh using less clay, send those to Vael, and use the saved material for the spill lip. The blocks would demand two weeks and Heartland charcoal.

"Our southern district already lost warmth," a Heartland delegate said. "Charcoal cutting strips windbreaks."

The discussion expanded instead of resolving. Rootglass offered dead firewood from burned western terraces, but transporting it risked carrying salt mites. Sevrin required heat treatment first. Reed barges could move treated wood upriver if Vael supplied iron gate fittings.

By noon, five regions owned a piece of one rain-channel repair.

"This is intolerably slow," the blue-river mason said.

"Yes," Pell replied. "We should compare it with rebuilding the arch after stealing the soil from someone else."

They agreed on treated Rootglass wood, Reed transport, hollow Varkesh blocks, and temporary shelter reinforcement from reusable siege timbers. Kharza signed only the Varkesh share. Aruk signed river carriage. Vael's council took responsibility for iron. The plan could still fail at three routes, but failure would arrive in named places rather than vanish inside a royal order.

Lessa found Kharza outside the weigh house.

She had refused every offer of a court medical title and now managed a cross-banner clinic near the breach. Her staff included former siege medics, orchard healers, and two Heartland physicians who had treated Osra's cavalry. A ceramic plate covered one side of the clinic where artillery had opened the old wall.

"Open," Lessa said, tapping Kharza's jaw.

"I have a bearing in three hours."

"Then waste less time objecting."

Kharza unlatched the copper brace. Lessa inspected the crack with a small mirror. No new split showed. The root remained sensitive and always would.

Across the clinic, sky-shear patients practiced balance on painted lines. Vennik riggers learned to work seated. A Mahr fault-reader with both tusks capped trained apprentices to interpret mallet vibration. Human patients with disrupted direction followed textured cords between rooms. Maela had helped design the paths but did not own them.

"Your election petition has passed half the removal threshold," Lessa said while replacing the brace.

Kharza held still. "For the Heartland grain agreement?"

"For giving Reedwater too many barges, depending which tavern you ask."

"Will you sign?"

"If you make the next bearing report sound like a victory speech."

Kharza believed her. Public office had not reconciled the Kiln Houses around her past. Veterans of the Three Valleys still opposed her. Families saved at Ashwall supported her more warmly than she trusted. Election changed the means by which conflict reached her; it did not erase the conflict.

Near the archive yard, Osra worked beneath guard at a long public table. Her hair had gone almost white over the year. She copied outer-region columns from the original ledger while petitioners waited to challenge omissions. The longer tribunal had begun hearing relocation cases and had issued no final term.

Osra saw Kharza but did not call to her. Kharza did not approach. Survival had given them time for judgment without requiring intimacy.

Tavan supervised no soldiers. After treatment and testimony, she worked with a prisoner-restitution team mapping stores seized during the siege. Some Vael residents refused her presence. Others valued her knowledge of where the army had sent grain. Mixed guards kept both truths from becoming a private settlement.

At the former council square, children played around the broken halves of the royal bearing seal. Clear boxes protected the pieces beside an inscription naming what they had opened and whom they had excluded. No one had repaired them.

The city had not turned ruin into purity. Merchants profited from rebuilding. Former officers sought seats on district councils. Refugees argued over whether return orders protected them or cleared valuable rooms for old owners. Yet every argument Kharza passed occurred beside posted figures, named custodians, and a place to file refusal.

The outer wall still gaped at Flood Bastion. Rebuilders had stabilized its edges and left the central breach open around a memorial path. Names of 6,840 military dead and 1,130 civilian dead covered fired-clay tiles along both sides, grouped by recovery place rather than army. Jorren Taal's tile stood beside the cistern-gate count, with no glorious column anywhere.

Kharza placed Ashwall stone beneath the names of refugees who died later in the siege. Other visitors left seed, bridge pins, hive clay, silver wire, ration chits, and pieces of damaged household doors.

Maela waited at the end of the path with one hand on a blue guide rail.

Directional blindness had not altered her eyes. She looked directly at Kharza and could not point toward Crown Hinge without touching the raised city map at her belt. Vael had marked public floors with textures: river grooves toward quays, round studs toward shelters, paired lines toward hinge courts. Visitors learned the system because making Maela navigate alone would have turned accommodation into private burden.

"Delegate Olt," Maela said.

"Crown Marshal."

Maela kept the office under an elected city charter with fixed review dates. She commanded the walls and no hinge. Her blue cloak carried no dynastic mark.

Pell approached from the bridge office wearing a brass badge that read Master of Bearings. She and Maela greeted each other with care rather than performance. Their hands touched, separated, then found each other again after Pell asked, "Guide?"

"For the east stair," Maela said.

Pell offered her left elbow. They walked together until the stair ended. At the landing, their offices led in different directions. Maela used the raised wall line; Pell went toward the counterweight yard. Nothing in their affection promised that either would surrender work or uncertainty.

They had begun sharing supper twice each week, when duty allowed and neither mistook a missed meal for rejection. Pell kept her rooms above the bridge office. Maela kept hers within the marshal's court, mapped by textured cords she could change without asking anyone's leave. Some nights they slept together. Other nights old command habits turned conversation into an argument and sent them home separately.

"Tomorrow?" Pell asked at the landing.

Maela considered rather than performing confidence. "Ask me after the bearing."

"A dangerously reasonable answer."

They parted smiling, with no witness required to turn the moment into settlement.

The first scheduled rotation began at noon.

A year of the new compact had produced amendments, failures, and three threatened withdrawals. Heartland delegates wanted the movement delayed until after grain heading. Neris wanted earlier rotation before storm season. Rootglass requested less pollen release. Reedwater demanded more drainage labor from the cities benefiting from millet shipments. Varkesh brought the failed third channel lip.

The bearing court met in the repaired lower hall with every door open.

Four delegations retained the emergency structure but rotated their people. Kharza and Aruk held Mahr seats for this action. Sevrin and Irel represented Aeli labor, with apprentices empowered to contradict their readings. Pell and Nim represented Vennik counterweights. Maela sat with rotating Vael, Neris, and Heartland delegates. Refugee cost recorders remained beside all four.

No single delegation possessed an indefinite veto. Any crew could stop for direct danger. Two delegations and a public threshold could pause the whole bearing.

The cost map had grown too large for one sailcloth. Panels covered the walls. Varkesh channels linked by amber cord to Heartland mold reports. Reed salinity cups stood beside Neris tide knots. Rootglass comb patterns faced Vael foundation gauges. Remote southern observers had sent questions about whether their own stone formations belonged to the same sky network; the court recorded the inquiry without allowing it to delay today's bounded work.

Pell called the load sequence.

Counterweight teams inspected ropes in public. Her right hand still lacked the two fingers lost before the war. She had redesigned several grips so workers with similar injuries could lock lines using wrist and palm. The change served more people than its origin.

Kharza and Aruk took separate listening stations. She opened her copper brace. He removed one silver cap. Neither action carried purity. Kharza's exposed crack limited what she could hear. Aruk's damped tusk expressed a Reed choice he resumed after each bearing.

Irel set the queen-hive cradle between paired pollen channels. Sevrin stood beside two apprentices, asking them to describe the living comb before he offered interpretation. Maela placed her hand on the river-city witness mark and tied a guide cord to her belt.

Outside, residents crowded public galleries. Heartland opponents carried signs demanding grain delay. Reed delegates displayed nursery maps. Children from Vael and Varkesh sat along the floor studs, arguing about which visible constellation resembled a cooking pot.

"Authorize one degree," said the court recorder.

Each delegation answered.

Weights entered the forward beds.

Crown Hinge sang through Kharza's good tusk. The repaired stone no longer offered the crushing double note of Ashwall, but two load paths remained distinct. Aruk called the marsh-root pitch. Kharza called the Varkesh return. Pell adjusted between them.

Star-bees left the cradle and crossed the lower pollen channel. Irel reported queen agitation within the first mark. Sevrin's apprentices described blue-amber dust along worker legs. Neris mirrors signaled tide within limit.

Half a degree.

Varkesh's third channel lip failed again. A terrace signal arrived by relay mirror, yellow over black. Water entered the empty ash bed as planned. The local crew did not call a stop because no soil or dwelling crossed the threshold. Kharza entered repair priority without pretending the failure did not count.

Three-quarters.

A Heartland field reported red mold under the husk. The farmer's delegation requested hold. Pell locked the beds before the request reached Maela. Inspectors compared samples. Mold affected one low field already damaged by flood, not the district-wide threshold. The Heartland delegate withdrew the hold and demanded relief grain entered anyway.

The court approved the relief after argument.

"Resume," Maela said when every labor confirmed.

Final brake teeth moved.

One degree settled across four hinges.

No tower fell. No coast alarm crossed its eighth knot. Rootglass bees returned to the living comb. Reed salinity rose by one marked line and stopped. Heartland lost warmth at the edge of the forecast. Varkesh rain shifted toward the repaired channels.

The public galleries applauded, protested, and began disputing the next schedule before workers had finished locking the return beds.

Kharza closed her tusk brace. Pain remained. So did the capacity to hear stone without obeying it alone.

At evening, the delegations climbed to the hinge chasm. Maela used Pell's voice and the paired floor lines. Sevrin walked with apprentices describing color. Aruk wore his cap again. Irel carried one empty comb rather than the queen. They stood among survivors, opponents, children, and workers while the exchanged constellation sectors reached the horizon of the lower vault.

Upper dawn entered through the open roof in a band of gold. Beneath the chasm, the buried sky brightened blue around the Deep Hart. For several breaths, both skies held dawn.

Kharza listened to the stone carry two lights through one world. Around her, custodians argued over the cost of the next degree, and every door remained open.
